L. 209 (1890), as 444 (0952) 68 1-7.

Though legal rules against monopoly are as old as our knowledge of law, government action against restraints on competition hallmarks American capitalism. Indeed, within this very decade, Congress has reiterated competition as a prime means for safeguarding our free market economy from severe booms and slumps and for insuring its capacity for sustained growth. Though many forces promote our creative economic system, among the most important remains antitrust. These laws have helped release energies essential to our world lead in industrial volume and productivity. And, despite formidable obstacles, they reinforce our ideal of careers open to talent, a crucial norm of a free society. As a result, the essentials of antitrust are today proclaimed by both political parties as necessary to assure availability of economic opportunity and some limitations on economic power--both prerequisites of personal and political freedom.

To these antitrust fundamentals, this Committee adheres with full vigor. True, since the Sherman Act's 

Act's 2/ passage in 1890, there have been fluctuations in the vitality and wisdom of antitrust enforcement as well as occasional setbacks in Congress and the courts. Nonetheless, a backward look across antitrust's more than three score years reveals a steady and, on the whole, healthy process through which antitrust principles have grown in strength and effectiveness. 

See The Employment Act of 1946, 60 Stat. L. 23 (1946), 15 U.S.c.

Basic to our antitrust scheme is the Sherman Act. Supplementing this charter are Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act 3/ passed 4/ in 1914, as well as the Clayton Act enacted in 1914, and amended, in 5/ and, in 1950, by added anti-merger 

1936, by Robinson-Patman provisions strictures. In addition, Congress has specified a public policy favoring competition, as an essential ingredient of the public interest concept, in a wide variety of regulatory statutes dealing, for example, with banking, transportation, communications and the disposal of government surplus properties. These provisions and others 7 form the matrix of our antitrust policy.

From applications of these varied statutes has emerged a pattern of construction--matured in some areas, retarded in others, but still leaving antitrust frontiers in need of metes and bounds. As might be expected, not all interpretations are consistent or equally in conformity with paramount antitrust goals. Nonetheless there exists a related body of antitrust doctrine which this Report considers. We turn:

First, to the central core of Legal and Economic Concepts; 

Second, we survey these concepts as applied to secure Patent protection while laboratory research is translated into consumer products; 

Third, once goods enter the channels of Distribution, antitrust prohibitions may come into play;

Fourth, these goods, of course, may be sold not only in domestic but also in Foreign Commerce where special antitrust problems may be posed;

Fifth, covering this entire range of antitrust problems are issues of Administration and Enforcement. 

Finally, a special variety of issues is posed in marking the boundaries of Exemptions carved out by Congress from our general policy favoring competitive enterprise.

Our plan is, first, to analyze the main course of antitrust policy and decisions. This analysis will provide the basis for evaluation, in light of established antitrust goals, of antitrust developments thus far. From this evaluation follow conclusions and specific recommendations as future guides to enforcement agencies, Congress and the courts. 

Our aim is not to amass a storehouse of statistical data or to survey economic effects of antitrust's application to the American economy or to specific industries. These tasks have in part already 

Indeed, in announcing organization of this Committee, the Co-chairmen said, "For one thing, we are not constituted as a formal fact-finding body. Our function is not to make original factual studies of particular industries or to find the facts in any particular case." 3

been undertaken by others. Undoubtedly they will again be attempted in appraising this report's specific recommendations or in some other context.

Instead, this Committee's task is to mark out as clearly as possible the path antitrust has traveled. In the process, we build of course on the collective experience of prior efforts. Our hope is that from such clarification will emerge more practical guides both for business seeking to live within the antitrust laws and for government officials charged with enforcing their prohibitions. Beyond this task of clarification, our goal is more effectively to achieve antitrust aims by weaving a coherent pattern from the differences and seeming inconsistencies of the legislative and judicial strands that comprise our antitrust fabric. 

I. THE CENTRAL CORE OF LEGAL AND ECONOMIC ANTITRUST CONCEPTS A. Sections 1 and 2 of the Sherman Act: 

A policy against "undue limitations on competitive conditions." 17 

Chief Justice Hughes remarked that the Sherman Antitrust Act,2/ a charter of freedom,...has a generality and adaptability comparable to 

Sections 1 and 2 provided, in substance, that: "1. Every contract, combination in the form of trust or otherwise, or conspiracy, in restraint of trade or commerce among the several States, or with foreign nations, is hereby declared to be illegal....Every person who shall make any contract or engage in any combination or conspiracy hereby declared to be illegal shall be deemed guilty of a misdemeanor.....

that found to be desirable in constitutional provisions...The restrictions the Act imposes are not mechanical or artificial. Its general phrases, interpreted to attain its fundamental objects, set up the essential standard of reasonableness.10/ 

A modern view of the Sherman Act can start with Standard Oil Co. of

New Jersey v. United States.11/ That opinion declared the rule of 

Sherman Act construction to be a "rule of reason," and sketched its scope, its limits, and the main line of its future development. It allayed prior doubts about the relationship between the Sherman Act and the common law, and defined the connection between Sections 1 and 2 offenses. The controversies of an earlier day over the reasoning of Standard Oil have receded into history. If the case is re-examined in the light of modern developments, rather than old debates, its analysis helps restore perspective. 

The government there obtained far-reaching relief against Standard, a holding company controlling stock interests in 37 oil corporations, and the seven individuals chiefly directing Standard. The relief, ordered under both Sections 1 and 2, required Standard to transfer its (cont'd) "2. Every person who shall monopolize, or attempt to monopolize, or combine or conspire with any other person or persons, to monopolize any part of the trade or commerce among the several States, or with foreign nations, shall be deemed guilty of a misdemeanor..." #1 Expen 

10/ Appalachian Coals, Inc. v. United States, 288 U.S. 344, 359-360 (1933). 

11/ 221 U.S. 1 (1911). Many cases are stated and discussed, and some approved or criticized. Discussion of a case without specific criticism is not intended to imply that all members of the Committee necessarily approve the reasoning or result. We do not conceive our function to require a case-by-case critique of all prior antitrust decisions. 5

Standard to bring about further Sherman Act violations.

The Court reviewed Standard's history since 1870 and found that, after 1899, at least, Standard had a dominant position with respect to refined petroleum, its transportation by pipe line and the sale of refined products. Standard's "absolute control" in these phases was considered to bring about, as "an inevitable result" control also over the market for crude oil.12/ Standard's position had been achieved via partnerships, mergers and other combinations with competing and complementary interests as well as through the internal expansion of the Standard group itself. In its growth, Standard had utilized certain practices deemed "unfair" such as, local price cutting, espionage, and the procurement from railroads of preferential rates and rebates.13/ 

These practices helped force some competitors, at least, to either join the combination or abandon the oil business. 

The terms "restraint of trade" and "monopolization" used in Sections 1 and 2 were then analyzed. While they "took their origin," "at least in their rudimentary meaning," from pre-1890 Anglo-American law, 12/ 221 U.S. at 77.

they are by no means confined to a narrow conception of the 15/ common law.

The common law, as the Court restated it, viewed restraints of trade and monopolization as two phases of the same phenomenon. The various early prohibitions dealing with both subjects were directed to the same ends: 16/ to protect the public against those evils "which led to the public outcry against monopolies". These were" "(1) The power which the monopoly gave***to fix***price and thereby to injure the public; (2) [and to impose some]***limitation on production; and (3) The danger of deterioration in quality***which***[is] the inevitable resultant of the monopolistic control over*** [an article's] production and sale."17/ 

Summarizing common law regarding restraints of trade the Chief Justice concludes that it was dominated by "the dread of enhancement of prices and of other wrongs which 15/ The Court's exposition of the common law on these matters is in striking contrast both to the opinion of 

Judge Taft in United States v. Addyston Pipe and Steel 

Co., 85 Fed. 271 (C.C.A. 6th, 1898), and to Justice Holmes' dissenting opinions in Northern Securities Co. v. United 

States, 193 U.S. 197, 400 (1904), and in Dr. Miles Medical 

Co. v. John D. Park & Sons Co., 220 U.S. 373, 409 (1911).

Whether the Supreme Court was right or wrong about the common law as a matter of legal history, its treatment of the subject is the basic statement of the ideas which have become the standard of Sherman Act construction.

This fear, he said, "led, as a matter of public policy, to the prohibition or treating as illegal all contracts or acts which were unreasonably restric- „19/ tive of competitive conditions." Such conclusion, the 

Court felt, might stem either from the nature or character of the contract or act, or from "surrounding circumstances" which "rise to the inference or presumption" that they tended "to bring about the evils, such as enhancement of prices, which were considered to be against public policy."20/ 

The net result was an assimilation of the terms "monopoly" and "restraint of trade." At common law these evils caused such conduct "to be treated as coming within monopoly and sometimes to be called monopoly and the same considerations caused monopoly because of its operation and effect, to be brought within and spoken of generally as impeding the due course of or being in restraint of trade. The statute was to reach all "undue" restraints, whether accomplished by methods new or old, And in determining what restraints of competition are "undue", since Section 1 is universal in form, a rule or standard of construction is needed to guide courts. The Court commented on the absence of a prohibition against monopoly in the concrete, as distinguished from monopolization, attributing this omission ! in the Act to a faith in the working of market forces which may be expected to prevent or cure monopoly, if economic freedom is not restrained by monopolization.27

The statute had not been so interpreted in United States 28/ 

v. Trans-Missouri Freight Association and United States v. 

Joint Traffic Association.29 These cases, however, were correctly decided, the Court said, in view of the nature, character, and "necessary effect" of the agreements there at bar. Both prior cases involved formal agreements among railroads of dominant regional importance. The agreements called for the fixing of rates, the setting of joint rates and rate divisions, and perhaps some division of traffic. 

In neither case was there evidence of tactics deemed "predatory," "abusive" or "unfair". Yet the operation and effect

of these formal agreements, the Court said, were properly held to have brought about by themselves the injuries to competition which the statute forbade. Since this was so, "resort to reason was not permissible in order to allow that "30/ to be done which the statute prohibited. The agreements there were illegal restraints by reason of their necessary effect on competition: accordingly, "they could not be taken out of that category by indulging in general reasoning as to the expediency or non-expediency of having made the contracts, or the wisdom or want of wisdom of the statute which prohibited their being made." The nature and character of the contracts, the Court said, created "a conclusive presumption which brought them within the statute."31/

Applying its view of the law to the facts of Standard's behaviour, the Court upheld the decree below. The holding company was held a combination in restraint of trade, an attempt to monopolize and a monopolization, bringing about a "perennial violation" of Section 2.32/ Two sorts of evidence justified this conclusion. acted, culminating in the transfer to Standard of the stocks of thirty-seven other corporations. This act, the Court said, as its "inevitable result," unified Standard's dominant power over petroleum and its products. Against this corporate history, Standard's resulting market position, "gives rise, in and of itself, in the absence of conntervailing circumstances, to say the least, to the prima facie presumption of intent and purpose to maintain the dominancy over the oil industry."33/ 

This presumption "resulting from the act of expanding the stock of the New Jersey corporation and vesting it with such vast control of the oil industry," was made conclusive by evidence of another class: the way Standard has exerted its market power and the results of such exercise. This evidence, not discussed in detail, was reviewed "not for the purpose of weighing the substantial merit of all the numerous charges of wrongdoing made during such period, but solely as an aid to discovering intent and purpose. 1134/ The Court concluded this mass of evidence showed a course of conduct dominated by a policy of maintaining a monopoly position, "frequently manifested by acts and dealings wholly inconsistent with *** the single conception of advancing the development of business power by usual methods."35/

36/ Some two weeks later the Court applied the same view of the Act in United States v. American Tobacco Company. 

Principal defendants were the American Tobacco Company, a group of its subsidiaries, and companies linked to American by agreements limiting competition. As a result of American's growth and its purchase of control from competitors, often after trade wars and at unusually high prices, it was found to have an almost unchallenged monopoly of the market for leaf tobacco as well as most branches of the trade in tobacco products. 

American violated both Sections 1 and 2, the Court reasoned, "not only because of the dominion and control over the tobacco trade which actually exists, but because we think the conclusion of wrongful purpose and illegal combination is overwhelmingly' i- "37/ established.". This purpose was evidenced by American's forcing competitors either to go under or sell out at prices explicable only by monopolistic intent. In addition the Court noted very large expenditures for the purchase of plant, soon after scrapped, and a policy of requiring covenants not to compete from vendors, stockholders and employees.

Remedying this violation the Court ordered American dissolved in order to recreate "a new condition which shall be honestly in harmony with and not, repugnant to the law. 38/

Such a decre required, in the Circuit Court's view, the division of American into fourteen (later sixteen) corporations, and prohibi ing any connection between these companies and their British rivals. No one of the successor corporations, the Circuit Court found, would have "such preponderating influence n the tobacco industry as to give it power to control the market, either as manufacturer, seller or purch- 39/ The business was so distributed among the successor companies that "no company will have substantially over 40 per cent in volume or value in any particular line."40/ 

In short, the teachings of Standard Oil and American Tobacco can be summarized in these terms: (1) While the words "restraint of trade" and 'monopolize" used in Sections 1 and 2 take their initial meaning from pre- 1890 common and statutory law, these terms are redefined to express a statutory policy of protecting the public against wrongs which the Congress thought might flow from situations, popularly called "monopoly", where competition by whatever was unduly limited. Standard 011 defines the object of the Sherman Act, as well as the common law, as the prohibition of all "contracts or acts which it was considered had a monopolistic tendency, especially those which were thought to unduly diminish competition," and of acts "producing" or tending to aser. means

produce the consequences of monopoly. 1,41/ (2) Construing both sections, the rule of reason requires interpreting the Act in the light of a broad public policy favoring competition, and condemning monopoly. While Standard 

011 gave the courts discretion in interpreting the word "every" in Section 1, that discretion confines each case to the issue of whether the conduct being reviewed under the Act constitutes an undue restraint of competitive conditions, or a monopolization, or an attempt to monopolize. This standard permits the courts to decide whether conduct is significantly anti-competitive in character or effect; it never permits them to consider once prevalent arguments, such as, whether monopoly arrangements would be socially preferable to competition in a particular industry, because of high fixed costs, unusual conditions, or the risks of "cut-throat" competition.

(3) It follows that certain forms of conduct, such as price-fixing, market division or group boycott are "conclusively presumed to be illegal, by reason of their nature or their necessary effect." In such cases, enquiry under the rule of reason is over once it has been decided that the conduct under review in fact constitutes price rigging, production control, or some other form of behavior clearly anti-competitive in character or effect.. 

417 (Standard Oil of New Jersey v. United States, 221 U.S. 1, 57 (1911)). 15

縫い ​that to the 'omela stopo

(4) The treatment of monopolization is closely linked to the Court's analysis in the Rate Bureau cases of conduct "conclusively presumed" to be illegal. In Standard Oil, evidence regarding the market position and power of the alleged monopolizers was segregated from evidence of their business practices and specific purpose to monopolize. The economic, evidence of monopoly power, at least where achieved by acquisitions, was said to create in this case (where the monopoly power was by any economic standard almost complete) "the prima facie presumption...to say the least" of intent to preserve it. That presumption was confirmed by evidence of prior deliberate moves to seek a monopoly position, and of not "usual [business] methods" considered to show a purpose 42/ to achieve or maintain it.

B. Section 1 of the Sherman Act. 

1. Arrangements governing price, either directly or by controlling production. 8. 

Control over market price "conclusively presumed" to be unreasonable.

to be illegal. Most important are those restraints controlling price, directly or by limiting production. Where the conduct involved constitutes market price control, in purpose or in effect, enquiry under Section 1 ends. For the conduct is in itself an undue limitation on competitive conditions. Similar reasoning governs the disposition of certain other cases where the conduct reviewed has no purpose or effect other than unduly to restrict competition. 43/ 

In Dr. Miles Co. v. John D. Park & Sons Co., for example, the court refused a drug manufacturer's request for an injunction against a drug wholesaler. The manufacturer charged the wholesaler with fraudulently inducing the manufacturer's employees and customers to breach their contracts with the manufacturer by selling to the wholesaler below a certain price. 

The contracts were vital to the manufacturer's plan for distributing its secret formula patent medicines. They called for specified wholesale and resale prices, applicable to all dealers to whom the plaintiff sold, The case, decided on demurrer, held the contracts unenforceable both at common law and under the Sherman Act, because of their resale price maintenance provisions. The manufacturer, having sold its product to wholesalers, was deemed to have no right to deprive the public of "whatever advantage may be derived from competition in the

A plan of price fixing through a trade association was 45/ condemned in United States v. Trenton Potteries Co., the trial an court having charged the jury that illegality must follow if it found a combination to fix the market price. Defendants as a group manufactured or distributed 82% of the supply of vitreous pottery bathroom fixtures produced in the United States, and there was no contention on appeal that they were not engaged in market price fixing or that they lacked the economic power In a passage often quoted, the Supreme Court. reto do so. jected the defense that the price fixing agreement was not unreasonable: "It does not follow that agreements to fix or maintain prices are reasonable restraints and therefore permitted by the statute, merely because the prices themselves are reasonable. Reasonableness is not a concept of definite and unchanging content. Its meaning necessarily varies in the different fields of the law, because it is used as a convenient summary of the dominant considerations which control in the application of legal doctrines. Our view of what is a reasonable restraint of commerce is controlled by the recognized purpose of the

Sherman Law itself. Whether this type of restraint is reasonable or not must be judged in part at least, in the light of its effect on competition, for, whatever difference of opinion there may be among economists as to the social and economic desirability of an unrestrained competitive system, it cannot be doubted that the Sherman

Law and the judicial decisions interpreting it are based upon the assumption that the public interest is best protected from the evils of monopoly and price control by the maintenance of competition... "The aim and result of every price-fixing agreement, if effective, is the elimination of one form of competition. 

The power to fix prices, whether reasonably exercised or not, involves power to control the market and to fix arbitrary and unreasonable prices. The reasonable price fixed today may through economic and business changes become the unreasonable price of tomorrow. Once established, it may be maintained unchanged because of the absence of competition secured by the agreement for a price reasonable when fixed. Agreements which create such potential power may well be held to be in themselves unreasonable or unlawful restraints, without the necessity of minute inquiry

Whether a particular price is reasonable or unreasonable as fixed and without placing on the government in enforcing the Sherman Law the burden of ascertaining from day to day whether it has become unreasonable through the mere variation of economic conditions. Moreover, in the absence of express legislation requiring it, we should hesitate to adopt a construction making the difference between legal and illegal conduct in the field of business relations depend upon so uncertain a test as whether prices are reasonable a determination which can be satisfactorily made only after a complete survey of our economic organization and a choice between rival philosophies. b. What Constitutes Price Control? And, absent price control, when is a competitive restraint "undue"?

Difficult questions in these cases are of two types. 

The first is whether the defendants' conduct constitutes price✓ control in purpose or effect. Sometimes, as in simple pricefixing cases, the answer is easy; often, however, where the 

46 273 U.S. at 396-398. 19

Fi ን። Aaracte & Court to pro i.

character or effect of the conduct is equivocal, a broader review of the way the market functions is required before a court can decide whether given behavior in fact amounts to price control. The second question arises when neither control over prices, nor any of the unreasonable per se offenses, such as group boycotts or market divisions, are in issue. Then the court's problem is to determine (1) whether defendants have enough market power to make the restriction of price competition, if proved, an "undue" restraint, and (2) whether they currently exercise the power or intend to do so. 

Board of Trade v. United States, 47/ for example, upheld کیا a rule of the Chicago Board of Trade, prohibiting members who bought after the close of business from making such purchases at any price other than the closing bid at the Exchange's "call" session. After a full review of the available facts, the Court concluded that the rule had no appreciable effect on general market prices, nor on the volume of grain coming to Chicago. On the contrary, the Court found that the regulation served legitimate trade purposes, rather than price-fixing objectives. The Court treated the rule, therefore, not as a species of price-fixing, but as a regulation of the hours of trading, whose effect would be to bring more of the class of transactions affected into the regular and fully competitive sessions of the Exchange. 

47/ 246 0.5. 231 (1918). 20

22for period crasti

On the other hand, in United States v. Socony Vacuum 011 48/ Co., a criminal case, the Court condemned as a price fixing a regional program of stabilization on the part of a group of major oil companies. The programs originated during the N.R.A. period, but were continued after N.R.A. had been declared unconstitutional. The Court found that the program was intended to and did have a significant effect in stabilizing the gasoline prices.

The defendants participated in two programs of buying "surplus" gasoline from small independent refiners who might otherwise have had to sell the gasoline at a price sacrifice. 

The defendants, while primarily interested in the Mid-Continent gasoline market, pursued a parallel buying program in East Texas. For at that time gasoline from there would move north, rather than south to Gulf ports, if the price differential between the East Texas and Mid-Continent areas exceeded transportation cost. Published market price quotations reporting independent refiners' spot sales in certain Mid-Continent cities were the source of the price terms in most of the major companies' distribution contracts. The prices paid by the majors to the independent refiners for the gasoline bought under the program were not high in relation to preexisting market prices; and the purchasing companies did use the gasoline thus purchased. 

48 310 U.S. 150 (1940). 21

www ine

The Court viewed the defendants' activities against the dynamics of gasoline price formation. The majors still in the case on the appeal probably did not have in combination the power to control the market as monopolists. Nonetheless, they were held to have exercised the power to influence materially market prices by buying significant amounts of gasoline on the spot market, a market which at most included less than of the gasoline consumed in the Mid-Continent marketing area. This power, its use pursuant to a common plan to stabilize prices, and the effect it had on prices, brought the program within reach of Section 1, as a scheme "to maintain a floor" under the market

Op a species of price manipulation within the Trenton Potteries rule.

The obverse of Socony Vacuum was the depression case 49/ of Appalachian Coals, Inc. v. United States. That equity proceeding, upheld, at least in advance of actual operations, a plan which united, in a regional sales agency, about half the capacity and about 73% of the commercial production of bituminous coal in the Appalachian district. This coal was sold throughout the east and middle west in competition with coal produced in other regions. In the context of the bituminous coal industry, a most competitive industry by any economic measure, defendants were found

1. Trade Association Programs 

Modern trade associations, of course, "deal with many industry problems other than pricing and production. They may provide a clearing house for information important to the entire industry and to the public as well. They often represent the interests of the industry before Congress, the Executive Department and state legislatures. And they may undertake economic or technical research to improve the quality of the industry's product, to eliminate waste in distribution, and to promote better relations between the industry 

50/ 288 U.S. at 374. 23

I' www # -5... 

YA 

able t Hone 53

on the one hand, and the public, the government and labor, on the other. Some of these activities may be of special benefit to the small business units of the industry less able to undertake services performed by the trade association and which otherwise only the larger firms could afford to perform for themselves.21/

National antitrust policy involves drawing a line between constructive trade association activities operating to promote competition and those which suppress competition among members and with outsiders. This requires preserving individual firms' independence of decision while, at the same time, sanctioning exchange of industry information and other trade association activities which may actually promote, rather than hinder, competition. سرا

In the main, trade associations have come under antitrust surveillance when some of their programs were reviewed as means of price and production control. Most of the cases start with the issue of whether the defendants' plan, in purpose or effect, limits price competition, and whether they have enough market power to carry it out. Where, as 52/ in American Colum and Lumber Company v.

Here, as in

Socony Vacuum, the defendant group need not have enough economic power over price to be classified as "monopolizers", for purposes of Section 2 of the Sherman Act. It suffices if the defendants can be reasonably expected materially to influence price, by reason of their market share, their position in relation to the price policies of other firms, and the way in which prices are in fact formed in the industry. In these cases, the court views the relevant market as the market for the commodity the defendants sell, in the parts of the country where in fact they do business. The competition of substitute commodities, and the possibility of interregional or international competition, as factors limiting the extent to which defendants can pursue an independent price policy, are sometimes relevant in defining the market within which their conduct is evaluated; but, once the relevant market is defined, such elements, representing normal limits on the price power of even a complete monopolist, may not be accepted as part of the market within which restraint on competition must be judged.

In the American Column and Lumber case, for example, the American Hardwood Manufacturers Association represented only 5% of the number of mills in the industry, but produced onethird of the nation's output. Their "Open Competition Plan" was not price fixing in form, It called for frequent meetings, detailed daily sales and shipping reports, monthly production and inventory reports, and the filing of price lists. The members not only reported their past transactions, but gave estimates of future production, and exchanged views as to future market conditions. The Secretary of the Association sent weekly and monthly reports to the members, digesting the information he had received, and thus deriving production programs each member should pursue to avoid industry wide "over-production." The Plan made no provision for fines or other penalties against members deemed to violate the agreement. Its operations covered the sharp price rises of the post-war boom in 1919, to which adverse weather conditions also contributed.

The Court concluded that the plan went beyond the legitimate collection, analysis and distribution of market information, on the basis of which sellers could more intelligently judge the market they confronted. It also reported estimates of future production and future market conditions based on individual plans, included significant suggestions to the members by an expert as to their future prices and productions, 43 26

In the Maple Flooring Case four trade association activities were attacked as elements in a scheme whose necessary effect could be to maintain prices at a non-competitive level. The group disseminated computations of a cost average for its members, "cost" for this purpose including overheads, advertising, and 6% of the book value of plant. The Association, in addition published a freight book, and engaged in a restricted program of weekly and monthly trade reporting of past transactions. There was no open announcement of current prices, or other procedures of price filing.

Nor did the information collected include names or other possible identification of actual past transactions. The statistics distributed to the membership were turned over to the Department of Commerce. The trade association's monthly meetings were confined to a general discussion of market factors and conditions.

A price agreement, express or implied, was not charged. 

The district court found, however, that the direct and necessary tendence of the plan was to destroy competition. 

This finding was reversed by the Supreme Court. The core of the case, as the Supreme Court viewed it, was the computation and dissemination of average cost statistics, "since without that, the other activities complained of could have no material bearing on price levels in the industry. ,,60/ 

These statistics coupled with the published freight book, could have been a

basis for fixing prices. This, if found, would have been illegal. "But, the record is barren of evidence that the published list of costs and the freight rate book have been so used by the present association. 

Consequently, the question which this court must decide is whether the use of this material by members of the association will necessarily have that effect so as to produce that unreasonable restraint of interstate commerce which is condemned by the Sherman Act. 61 The dissemination of market information might, of course, in the play of market forces, have some effect on prices. But this effect would be produced by the individual response of a large number of relatively small sellers, in a market where entry was cheap and easy, and where the defendant had no conceivable power to restrict the competitive opportunity of its rivals. In the presence of these market factors, the case was viewed as concerning the economic effect of the public collection and dissemination of information as to past transactions in a competitive industry. And the Court held that neither the character of the information circulated statistics as to production and transportation costs, aggregate production and inventories, and actual past prices nor the use made of it by the members lead to the inference that its necessary effect would be different than if the same statistics were published by a trade journal or the Department of Commerce.

The Sugar Institute case, the latest comprehensive Supreme Court treatment of a trade association open-price filing plan, held illegal many activities of the Sugar Institute. The defendants refined practically all imported raw sugar processed in the United States, and supplied between 70% and 80% of the sugar consumed here. Their product was found to be thoroughly standardized. consumer preference for brand names permitted some price differences in sales to the public, sugar sold to manufacturers - about one-third of the whole - was sold on price only, and secret price concessions were common in the struggle for large manufacturers' or dealers' contracts. Five refiners, accounting for about 1/4 of defendants' output, never gave concessions from their announced prices. The other ten, identified as "unethical" refiners, did so in greater or less degree. It was estimated that at least 30% of the sugar sold in 1927 carried secret concessions of some kind. 64/ 297 U.S. 553 (1936).

The Sugar Institute Plan evolved out of meetings called to discuss market conditions, and particularly "undesirable practices and secret concessions." The narrow line between legal and illegal refusals to sell, was more fully explored in Federal Trade Commission v. Beech-Nut 78/ Packing Co. There the Court upheld Commission findings that "the 

Beech-Nut system of merchandising" was an unfair method of competition. " 80/ Summary. Section 1's policy against "undue limitations on competitive conditions" raises two problems: what constitutes price control? And, absent price control, what criteria should guide the 

78/ 257 U.S. 441 (1922). 

79/ Id.at 455. 80/ Ibid. (See also, to the same effect, United States v. Bausch & Lomb Optical Co., 321 U.S. 707 (1944). 36

-- competit d price 345 price f Capeti dcc

courts in distinguishing reasonable from "undue" limitations on competitive conditions, where those limitations affect the process of price formation?

Many kinds of devices and arrangements can constitute market price fixing. They are uniformly held to be "undue limitations on competitive conditions," whether the prices thus controlled are "high" or "low", or the program adopted to deal with demoralized or even fraudulent industry practices. 

For this conclusion, two principal reasons are advanced. On the one hand, significant limitations on price competition are, in "character," practices which have no purpose save the elimination of competition, and are therefore prohibited. Indeed, it is often said that where price control is in issue, the amount of commerce affected is immaterial. On the other, decisions refer also to the necessary or actual effect of the practice on price competition. 

Opinions sometimes put differing degrees of emphasis on the relative importance of the "character" and the "effect" of the defendants' conduct as criteria of its illegality. The pattern of decisions suggests, however, that at least where the practice under review is at all ambiguous-- for example, a trade association information program, or a common sales agency, as contrasted with a simple agreement on prices or a resale price maintenance contract--itwwill be held a price control device, within the Trenton Potteries ambit, only if found to be such in actual or probable effect. 37

صوف Whe lural E Floorin being e require ETS 28.

Where price control is not found, but the practicebbeing reviewed may have some effect on price formation, its reasonableness (as in the Chicago Board of Trade, Appalachian Coals or Maple Flooring) turns on the relative insignificance of the competition being eliminated or restrained in the relevant market, as compared with its other purposes or effects. The answer to this question requires an economic analysis of the market affected, and of the practice under scrutiny. 

2. Restraints Affecting Competitive Opportunities and Commercial Freedom. 

Arrangements for limiting price competition are not the only ways in which competition may be "unduly" curtailed and Section 1 violated. a.

(2) Section 7 of the Clayton Act By its 1950 amendment of Section 7, Congress sought statute.85/ an effective anti-merger statute. 85/ Not covered by old

85/ Section 7 now reads as follows: 

"No corporation engaged in commerce shall acquire, directly or indirectly, the whole or any part of the stock or other share capital and no corporation subject to the jurisdiction of the Federal Trade Commission shall acquire the whole or any part of the assets of another corporation engaged also in commerce, where in any line of commerce in any section of the country, the effect of such acquisition may be substantially to lessen competition, or to tend to create a monopoly. "No corporation shall acquire, directly or indirectly, the whole or any part of the stock or other share capital and no corporation subject to the jurisdiction of the Federal Trade Commission shall acquire the whole or any part of the assets of one or more corporations engaged in commerce where in any line of commerce in any section of the country, the effect of such acquisition, of such stocks or assets, or of the use of such stock by the voting or granting of proxies or otherwise, may be substantially to lessen competition, or to tend to create a monopoly. "This section shall not apply to corporations purchasing such stock solely for investment and not using the same by voting or otherwise to bring about, or in attempting to bring about, the substantial lessening of competition. Nor shall anything contained in this section prevent a corporation engaged in commerce from causing the formation of subsidiary corporations for the actual carrying on of their immediate lawful business, or the natural and legitimate branches or extensions thereof, or from owning and holding all or a part of the stock of such subsidiary corporations, when the effect of such formation is not to substantially lessen competition. "Nothing contained in this section shall be held to affect or impair any right heretofore legally acquired: Provided, that nothing in this section shall be held or construed to authorize or make lawful anything heretofore prohibited or made illegal by the antitrust laws, nor to exempt any person, from the penal provisions thereof or the civil remedies therein provided." 40

stock acquisition. In addition, narrowing court interpretations had rendered the former provision hardly more effective than the Sherman Act in halting anti-competitive 86/ mergers. 87/ As a remedy Congress made three major changes. First, it broadened the Section's reach by extending Commission jurisdiction to corporations acquiring the "whole or any part," not only of the stock, but also "of the assets"-. of another company. Second, eliminated from the statute was the unfeasible illegality test of whether the acquisition's effect "may be" to substantially lessen competition between the acquiring and the acquired corporations. Finally, Congress struck the prior test of whether the acquisition might restrain commerce "in any community," and left the gauge of whether the acquisition may substantially lessen competition or tend to monopoly "in any section of the country." 

The intent of Congress in making these changes is in some ways imprecise, For example, the final report of the

Supp. 117 (N.D. Ohio 1935). Cf. International Shoe Co. v.

98/ Such a test, the Commission felt, was established for Section 3 by Standard Oil of California v. United States, 337 U.S. 293 (1949).

In Pillsbury, the Commission viewed Standard Stations as setting for 

Clayton 3 a "simplified test" (mimeo opinion p.8) requiring no "real consideration of the effect upon competition "of the challenged practice." (Id. at 6) Demanded only was "proof that a substantial share of the market was affected" (Id. at 6). Thus viewed, the Commission, properly, we feel, rejected application of Standard Station's gauge to Section 7. (For a like conclusion, see Transamerica Corp. v. 

Federal Reserve Board, 206 F. 2d 163, 169-170, (3rd. Cir. 1953) cert. denied, 346 U.S. 901 (1954). 

We recognize, however, that another, albeit not so prevalent, view of Standard Stations is also plausible. This version emphasizes: (1) The Court's view of the issue Standard posed whether the requisite proof of injury might be "met simply by proof that a substantial portion of commerce is affected or whether it must also be demonstrated that competitive activity has actually diminished or probably will diminish". (337 U.S. 293, 299). Rejecting the latter gauge as requiring comparison of the imponderable exclusionary effects of alternative marketing methods, the Court prescribed as its measure whether exclusive arrangements "foreclosed competition in a substantial share of the line of commerce affected." (Id. at 314). (2) Although Standard reflects no economic analysis of whether competitors were actually "foreclosed" from access to dealers, or consumers from choice of alternative brands, the facts at bar, upon later analyds, might warrant such conclusion. This conclusion points, for example, to adoption of like arrangements by other majors, the majors' refusals to sell to "split-pump" stations, and the difficulty of dealers' switching affiliation. (3) Any implication that Standard required proof that exclusive arrangements, to be illegal under Section 3, must merely involve "a substantial share of the line of commerce affected" is belied, this view suggests, by later Court interpretations of Standard. (See Federal 

Trade Commission v. Motion Picture Advertising Service Co., 344 U.S. 

392, 394, 401-403 (1953). Cf. Richfield Oil Corp. v. United States.

Pillsbury particularly, a general increase in the majors' percentage shares, a decline in the number of mills, a lack of new entries and a movement in the direction of oligopoly. Revealing its concern with the probable future effects of this and other mergers, a consideration 104/ required by Clayton but not Sherman Act doctrine, the Commission noted "There is nothing in the record to indicate that the mergers will at present convert the industry in the southeast from a competitive to a non-competitive pattern. The inference, in fact, must be to the contrary inasmuch as large national distributors, such as, General Mills and Quaker Oats, and large regional distributors remain to furnish effective competition to Pillsbury Mills. However, in the urban markets at least, the mergers lead in the direction of what is sometimes called oligopolistic or 'monopolistic competition, that is, to a situation where the remaining competition in the particular market is between big companies. "If, for example, respondent should continue to acquire competitors at the rate it has since 1940, and other large companies should do the same, the urban markets in the southeast may come to be dominated by a few large milling companies. This, of course, has been the trend in other industries. In some of them, under the policy of the Sherman Act, competition between the big companies continues to protect the consumer interest. But, as we understand it, it was this sort of trend that Congress condemned and desired to halt when it adopted the new Clayton Act anti-merger provision."

Pillsbury's rejection of the Standard Stations test finds support in the last decision under former Section 7. In Transamerica Corp. v. 

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve Board, 105/ the court reversed the Federal Reserve Board's finding of a violation of the Clayton Act's substantial lessening and tendency clauses. Relevant

Reversing the Board, the court reasoned "The Board does not deny that the cases which have been decided under Section 7 have interpreted and applied that section as we do here. But it asserts that these cases have all been overruled by the opinion of the Supreme Court in 

Standard Oil Co. v. United States, *** We do not think that the Standard Oil case has any such effect. Section 3 of the 

Clayton Act deals with exclusive dealing contracts, not with stock acquisitions. The use of exclusive dealing contracts per se lessens competition, however, so that the fact of lessening need not be proved, For one who agrees to purchase all his requirements from a single seller is legally barred from purchasing them from anyone else and is consequently eliminated entirely from the competitive market. In order to establish a substantial lessening of competition in such a case, therefore, it is only necessary in addition to prove that the sales covered by the exclusive dealing contracts amount to a substantial portion of the tdal involved in the competitive market area. This is precisely what is held in the Standard Oil case. "The situation with respect to corporate stock acquisitions the subject matter of Section 7, is wholly different, however. 

For the acquisition of the stock of two or more corporations engaged in interstate commerce is not per se a violation of the section, On the contrary such acquisition is a violation only if its effect may be in fact to substantially lessen competition between such corporations, to restrain commerce or to tend to create a monopoly. Otherwise, the acquisition is entirely lawful, so far as Section 7 is concerned. It necessarily follows that under Section 7, contrary to the rule under Section 3, the lessening of competition and the tendency to monopoly must appear from the circumstances of the particular case and be found as facts before the sanctions of the statute may be invoked. Evidence of mere size and participation in a substantia share of the line of business involved, the 'quantitative substantiality' theory relied on by the Board, is not enough." 2061 106

Pillsbury's primary approach we approve, not only for horizontal, but also for vertical mergers via integration. Section 7 is concerned, not with striking down trade practices with an assumed anticompetitive effect, but rather with the proved probability of an adverse change in a market's competitive pattern. To this end, analysis of "relevant factors" to predict an acquisition's 107/ "probable economic consequences" seems crucial.

At the same time, Pillsbury does not require all evidence be admitted in all proceedings regardless of a market's basic structure. The Act does not demand an exhaustive economic inquiry for its own sake. In the proposed Benrus merger, for example, eliminating one of a few major competitors and leaving the merging companies with twenty percent of the market, evidence of remaining competitors' strength is not necessarily even relevant. 108/

This defense was specifically established in the International Shoe case and is preserved by explicit references in the legis- 109/ lative history.

In approving Pillsbury, we read its reference to proof of the same kind of facts relevant under the Sherman Act against the clear Congressional intent to prevent any substantial lessening of competition or halt, in their incipiency, movements toward monopoly. Thus we stress, as the Commission there did, the need for considering the probable future development of a market and particularly any demonstrable trend toward its domination by a few large companies. "This", the Commission noted, "has been the trend in other industries. In some of them, under the policy of the Sherman Act, competition between the big companies continues to protect the consumer interest. But, as we understand it, it was this sort of trend that Congress condemned and desired to halt when it adopted the new Clayton Act anti-merger 110/ provision. 11.

The tendency clause of Section 7 is peculiarly adapted to this construction. As the court pointed out in Transamerica, the Congressional condemnation of mergers which may "tend to 

109/ House Report No. 1191, 81st Cong., 1st Sess., p.6 (1949); 

Senate Report No. 1775, 81st Cong., 2d Sess., P. 7 (1950. 

110/ Mimeo. Opinion p. 16. 50

-: create a sulting i power. clearly those cu a few re fare sho by even In differe practi ficati 1

create a monopoly must mean a condemnation of any merger resulting in a measurable movement in some market toward monopoly power. Congress, by its disapproval of Columbia Steel's result, clearly intended to halt large companies' growth by acquisitions whose cumulative effect is a movement toward dominance by one or 111/ a few relatively large concerns. The tendency clause therefore should be construed to slow the growth of dominant concerns by even relatively minor acquisitions.

In sum, measuring mergers' competitive effects poses problems different from gauging market consequences of Section 3, marketing: practices. Under Section 3, Congress assumed, with probable justification, ultimate harm to competition once specified practices were shown actually to foreclose competitors from a substantial share of the market. In addition, there can be presumed some degree of market power in the seller, simply from the existence of a "tying clause" or a condition of refusal to deal with the seller's competitors.

Mergers by their mere occurence, on the contrary, are economically and competitively neutral, A common form of growth, they may improve the competitive ability of companies regardless of market size or position. They involve no necessary connotations of coercion, dominance or lack of effective competitive pressures. 

111/ House Report No. 1191, 81st Cong., 1st Sess., pp. 10-11 (1949). 

See also, Senate Rep. No. 1175, 1st Cong., 2d Sess. p. 5 (1950). 51

In additi failed in ruptcies.

En e ​苔

In addition, mergers may ease from the market companies which have failed in the competitive struggle and thus prevent potential bankruptcies. Finally, they may spur operating economies by spreading overhead costs or enabling improved technology.

Similarly, in a vertical acquisition, the fact that competitors of one company are foreclosed from selling to the other need of itself signal no substantial lessening of competition or tendency to monopoly. The integration, on the contrary, may create a company better able to compete with larger rivals, It may, in addition, mean economies which in a competitive market spell consumer savings. 

The question therefore is not whether competitors of one of the merging companies are foreclosed from selling to the other, but whether competitors of either are in fact foreclosed from access to the market. Answering this question generally requires resort, not to a priori assumptions, but market analysis. 112/ Such analysis is no more beyond the competence of the courts than the Federal Trade Commission. Appraising the probable effect of one or a series of acquisitions requires no speculation of what might, absent the merger, have taken place over time.- 

Thus, not for decision is the long-range effect of a particular trade practice, as against possible marketing alternatives, in a current market setting. Instead, gauging a merger's probable effect involves analysis of provable facts of market structure

and operation. For example, the merger of one company with its competitors' sole source of supply involves an obvious potential threat to the existing and future competition in the market. On the other hand, a merger of an ingot producer with a fabricator, in an industry already vertically integrated, may do no more than create a more effective competitor. 113/ Similar market analysis is required in dealing with conglomerate mergers. For example, acquisition of theatres in closed towns by an owner of houses in open towns, might violate Section 7 by creating a tendency to monopoly in the open towns even though, because of the geographic separation of the markets, the merging units do not themselves compete. Also acquisitions in functionally separated markets may afford leverage from one market into the other; for example, an automatic tie-in situation may arise where demand for the acquired company's product would substantially improve the position of the acquiring company as against its competitors who can offer no supply of the acquired product. 

Such situations may also require detailed consideration of business and economic facts. b. Division of the Market: 

by agreement. 

Exclusion of competitors

Beyond mergers, considered largely under Section 7 of the 

Clayton Act, market division is a common practice within the reach of Sherman Act Section 1. Agreements allocating markets are found in plans for the licensing of patents or secret processes, and in programs for distribution through territorial dealerships. They also appear as simple contracts among competitors with no function other than the elimination of competition, either as cartel agreements, or as agreements not to solicit the customers of a rival. 

1. Market Division by Agreement among Competitors. 

Where actual or potential competitors agree not to compete in specified territories, or for specified customers, the consequence is to reduce the number of sellers in each affected market. Thus, competition among the contracting parties is eliminated even more completely than, had they agreed on prices, but still did business in the relevant markets. The contracting parties gain only if the result is to give each a substantial degree of power in his own market; if a large number of strong competitors remain despite the contract, no contracting party profits from elimination of even a significant competitor. Thus where market division agreements among competitors are proved, they are held to be unreasonable, on the analogy of the Trenton Potteries decision, by reason of their character and necessary effect on competition. They are agreements with no purpose 54

other the

Cent sha degree a Mark tars vi market Addysto on mark of mar in Th produ ral ch

other than the elimination of competition; and, where significant shares of the market are involved, their effect on the degree of competition in the market can be severe. 

Market division cases thus far have all concerned competitors who as a group possessed substantial, and often dominant, market power. The conduct struck down as price fixing in

Addyston Pipe & Steel Co. v. United States, 115/ was partly based on market division among manufacturers supplying about two-thirds of markets where they did the bulk of their business. Similarly, 116/ in Timken Roller Bearing Co. v. United States condemned was the allocation of market territories between the dominant American producer of tapered roller bearings and the British and French firms which it controlled jointly with its former British competitor. 

The same result has been reached in several lower-court cases involving territorial market division or agreements not to sell to a rival's customers. 117/ While in no Supreme Court case have the facts been limited exclusively to simple market division

In Columbia Steel, the subsidiary United States Steel processing company was not only an "exclusive" outlet for United States Steel Company's products; but, as a practical matter, it would use no other supplier's steel. In 

Bausch & Lomb, however, the exclusive distributor was described as 126/ "self-restricted" to that field, although the manufacturer's agreement not to compete with it in the distribution of pink-tinted lenses was probably conditional on its remaining an exclusive distributor.

Despite factual differences among the cases, the analogy between mergers and exclusive dealerships suggested by the Columbia Steel case seems for some purposes reasonable. Both can have important and independent business purposes; both may result in a reduction of competition. Where such reduction accomplished by an exclusive dealership forms part of a plan to effect a monopoly, or the lesser degree of market competitive restraint prohibited by Section 1, it will be held a violation. An exclusive dealership, on the other hand, may be merely an ancillary restraint, reasonably necessary to protect the parties' main business purposes. When so supported (for example, it may be impossible to recruit distributors without such a promise), 125

such dealerships should be upheld (1) where the manufacturer does not have so substantial a share of his market as to render competition ineffective in the exclusive dealer's market or (2) where, as a consequence of exclusive dealership competition in either the manufacturer's or dealer's market, is not reasonably restrained.

Forced Use of Standard Contracts, and other Refusals to Deal. 

Apart from the direct elimination of competition among competitors, Section 1 of the Sherman Act has been invoked against restraints "forestalling" or "foreclosing" a market--that is, denying competitive access to a market, whether by contract or integrated ownership br by collective refusals to deal. 

In some cases, such as Paramount Famous Lasky Corp. v. United 128/ States127/ and United States v. First National Pictures, the illegal restraints were imposed by dominant groups on those with whom they dealt, thus further altering the balance of bargaining power and restricting the competitive opportunity of those affected. 

The Paramount case condemned under Section 1 the joint enforcement provisions of the compulsory arbitration clause of a standard motion picture exhibition contract. Defendants were producers and distributors of films, who handled 60% of the motion pictures then displayed throughout the country, as well as their trade association and regional film Boards of Trade. The Boards of Trade consisted of the local managers of the defendant distributors, as well as of practically all other film distributors. Defendant distributors had agreed to contract with exhibitors only according to the terms

Limitation of competitive opportunity through arrangements exploiting a strong position in one market as leverage to improve one's position in another have also been considered under Section 1. These practices involve sellers who provide one commodity or service (the "tying" product) for which the market demand is strong that is, in economic terms, the demand curve that product confronts is relatively inelastic - and at the same time provide other products or services (the tied product) which face more urgent competition. A variety of business policies may argue against pushing the price for the tying product to the limit of what the market would bear. For example, the sellers of the tying product may believe that high short-run profits from its manufacture and sale may spur new

competitors' entry, or result in loss of ground to substitute commodities, or to imports. In other situations, the tying product may be a patented or unpatented machine used for long periods of time with some other commodity; as a practical matter, the best opportunity for making money from its use may therefore be to sell or lease the machine rather cheaply, on condition of supplying all requirements of the tied commodity used with it. 

In some cases, the seller may practice what is in effect a form of price discrimination: to sell or lease the tying product at a differentially low price to customers who aso buy the tied product from it. 135/ Then it may appear that profits can be increased by spreading the market power possessed with regard to the tying product as far as possible to the tied product. 

In all these situations the result may or may not be a less economic allocation of resources than would otherwise obtain. 

But such arrangements do restrain competition with respect to the market for the tied commodity or service. The seller's competitors on the market for the tied product may find access to that market difficult or impossible. As the Supreme Court rightly said in 

Standard Oil Co. of California v. United States. 136/ "tying agreements serve hardly any purpose beyond the suppression of competition

contracts in New Orleans. There were three newspapers in New 

Orleans: the morning Times-Picayune, and two evening journals, the States, owned by Times-Picayune, and the Item. Times-Picayune refused to sell certain classes of advertising unless advertisers also purchased advertising in the

Times-Picayune was thus a "package deal" or "full-lineforcing" case, not involving an obligation not to deal with a competitor of the seller. The suit was brought under Section 1 of the 

Sherman Act, because of doubt that advertising was a "commodity' within the meaning of Section 3 of the Clayton Act. 

The Supreme Court pointed out that the substance of Sherman Act violations may not normally depend upon "so flexible a guide" 143/ as the concept of the "markets" and then said that monopolistic leverage in a market was the essence of tying agreement illegality. 

The relevant market, defendants urged, included not only both morning and evening papers, but also radio and television advertising. 

The government, in contrast, claimed radio and television ads should be excluded and even separate markets for advertising in morning and evening papers. Rejecting both parties' contention, the Court

There a motion picture chain, including theatres which were the sole houses in their towns, as well as theatres which faced local competition, was here violative of Sections 1 and 2 of the Sherman Act. That group, the charge continued, sought to monopolize at least the Chicago taxi-cab market and to eliminate outside competion for the supply of taxi-cabs to its cab-operating affiliates in all three cities. That this is not a permissible construction of Yellow Cab is established by United States v. Columbia Steel Co. 

168/ There the Court upheld the purchase by the United States Steel Corporation of the assets of a small West Coast steel fabricator, Consolidated Steel. Consolidated had purchased its raw materials on the market, and United States Steel then had no West Coast fabricating facilities. The transaction took place shortly after the government had sold its Geneva, Utah, steel plant to United States Steel on the understanding that the purchasing company would enlarge and complete the facility. The Court examined the effect of the purchase of Consolidated in carefully defined subdivisions of the West Coast steel market the market for rolled steel, which Consolidated had formerly purchased; the market for structural fabricated products, and that for pipe, where there had formerly been competition between Consolidated and United States Steel subsidiaries. It concluded that the effect of the purchase on competition in all three markets would be insubstantial and, accordingly, held no violation. 

The references in the opinion to the Paramount case seemed to some to suggest that tests of full market dominance, applicable in cases under Section 2, were relevant in passing on the .. merger's validity under Section 1. But the complaint, the facts and their analysis in the opinion confirm that the case applied

Intra-Corporate Combination or Conspiracy. 

Finally, arising under Section 1 is an issue involving proof of that agreement requisite for violation--the extent to which corporations linked by common ownership and control, or even fellow employees of the same corporation, can "contract," "combine" or "conspire. 11. 169/ The language of Section 1 makes illegal "every contract, combination in the form of trust or otherwise or conspiracy" in restraint of interstate or foreign commerce. The distinction between "combinations" and "conspiracies" for purposes of Section 1 has never been clearly drawn, and the two terms certainly overlap, to say the least, in antitrust usage. Nonetheless, the identification of the term "combination" in the statute with "trusts" suggests the word meant at least to include permanent association of previously separate enterprises, whether through trusts, holding companies, mergers or other legal forms. 169/ The cases so far decided under Section 1 on the problem of combination or conspiracy within a single enterprise include 

Standard Oil Co. of New Jersey v. United States, 221 U.S. 1 (1911); 

United States v. General Motors Acceptance Corp., 121 F. 2d 376 

(7th Cir. 1941); United States v. Yellow Cab Co., 332 U.S. 218 (1947); Timken Roller Bearing Co. v. United States, 341 U.S. 593 (1951); and Kiefer Stewart Co. v. Joseph E. Seagram & Sons, 340 

U.S. 211 (1951). 80

The defined two or Beans circu 

1 as a and 2 or at lede

The word "conspiracy," as used by the statute, was early defined, on the other hand, as referring to a "combination of two or more persons to accomplish an unlawful end by lawful means or a lawful end by unlawful means."- 170/ 

Amplifying the circumstances under which a single person may violate Section 

1 as a "conspiracy" is the disparate structure of Sections 1 and 2. Section 2 specifies that "every person" who monopolizes, or attempts or conspires to monopolize, etc., is guilty of a misdemeanor. Section 1, however, first declares "every contract, combination or conspiracy", etc., to be illegal, and then provides that "every person who shall make any contract or engage in any combination or conspiracy hereby declared to be illegal" is guilty of a misdemeanor. "Contracts" and "conspiracies" clearly involve two or more persons. However, in the case of tying contracts, or resale price maintenance contracts or understandings imposed by a manufacturer, it may be unrealistic to suppose that there is a "contract" "inade" by vendor and vendee with mutual assent to restrain trade. The offense is in reality often committed by the vendor alone. To regard the unwilling vendee, forced by business circumstances to comply with an illegal arrangement, as a violator ignores business practicalities. Much of the common law on the unenforceability of contracts in restraint of trade rests on a recognition of the fact that the restraint may in 

170/ United States v. Addyston Pipe & Steel Co., 85 Fed. 271, 293 (6th Cir. 1898). 81

fact be L

Simil Througho and the States and othe carolus- 1.. A grou mch q Corpor as in

fact be imposed by one party on the other. 

Similarly unclear are the limits of the term "combination". 

Throughout the early cases dealing with mergers, holding companies, and the like, the issue seemed to create no problem. In United 171/ 172/ 

States v. American Tobacco Co. 17 years of mergers, consolidations and other changes in corporate form were unavailing to alter the conclusion that the corporate structure was in fact a "combination". 

A group of defendants all employed by the same corporation, and a group of affiliated or merged corporations, were treated without much question as having "combined" or "conspired" to direct their corporation to commit an illegal act, at least where the corporation, as in Northern Securities or American Tobacco cases, consisted of elements of distinct pre-existing business and functional units. 

Not considered in these cases, however, was whether action by employees of a corporation not formed as a combination could convert their corporation into a "conspiracy", so as to make its conduct, otherwise legal, into a violation of the Sherman Act. 

These cases all concerned companies which combined previously independent competitors, or involved Section 2, which can of course be violated by a single person. It appears clear that the responsible officers of a corporation would be liable as accessories or co-conspirators for directing or authorizing a corporate employee to commit fraud or arson. 

171/ 221 U.S. 106 (1911). 

172/ Id. at 184 : Similarly, since the corporation can 82

alone violate Section 2, it seems likely that its officers and directors would be guilty of conspiring to do so for authorizing 173/ its monopolistic conduct. On the other hand, such action by employees should not make the corporate act illegal, if under the statute defining the offense the conduct, to be illegal, must be committed by two legal persons. The early case of Patterson v. 174/ United States concluded that a group of corporate employees could be held guilty of violating Sections 1 and 2 of the Sherman

Act, the corporations not being a party to the prosecution, where the employees carried out a predatory policy looking to a monopoly 175/ of the cash register business. Later cases in the lower courts have disagreed, at least where Section 1 alone was involved. 176/

The first case in the Supreme Court to consider intra-corporate "conspiracy" or "combination" was United States v. Yellow Cab 

173/ The contrary view would read the words "combine or conspire with any other person or persons to monopolize", in Section 2, as limited to persons of different economic interests, such as competitors. While this is a possible construction of the Act, it would be contrary to the general scope of the conspiracy doctrine in our law, and to long standing usage in antitrust cases, [see e.g., Standard Oil Co. v. United States, 221 U.S. 1 (1911); "Amer 

Ican Tobacco Co. v. United States, 328 U.S. 781, 783 (1946)], where a charge of conspiracy to monopolize is commonly added to the indictment, information or complaint, although both the participating individuals and the corporations are undoubtedly responsible for the monopolization itself.

Co. 177/ Against a motion to dismiss, that case upheld a complaint which charged a combination and conspiracy with specific intent to violate Sections 1 and 2, the defendants being one Markin and six corporations controlled by him through stock ownership. Several of the corporations grew out of mergers of previously independent competitors. The Court upheld the allegation of conspiracy. "The test of illegality under the Act is the presence or absence of an unreasonable restraint on interstate commerce. Such a restraint may result as readily from a conspiracy among those who are affiliated or integrated under common ownership as from a conspiracy among those who are otherwise independent. Similarly, any affiliation or integration flowing from an illegal conspiracy cannot insulate the conspirators from the sanctions which Congress has imposed. The corporate interrelationships of the conspirators, in other words, are not determinative of the applicability of the 

Sherman Act."178/

The most recent case to consider the question is Timken 

Roller Bearing Co. v. United States 

185/ 

a Section 1 case charging arrangements between the American Timken and its chief foreign competitor for limiting their competition in the American and world markets. British Timken had evolved between 

1909 and 1928 as an enterprise jointly controlled by the American Company and a British firm which was American Timken's principal 186/ potential competitor. The French company was organized by the British and American interests controlling British Timken. 

At the time of the trial, the American company owned 30% of the stock of the British Company, one Dewar owned 24%, and the balance was publicly held. Both Dewar and the defendant owned 

50% of the stock of the French Company. Dewar died while the appeal was pending. Under pre-existing contracts, Timken had the right to purchase Dewar's stock from his estate, which the District Court later permitted in this instance, The original arrangements were held unjustified by the patents then owned by Timken, and their continuation after the expiration of the patents was held violative of Section 1. If the British and 

French Timken companies are viewed as a combination or conspiracy between the American company and its chief competitor, in the perspective of the Standard 011 and American Tobacco cases, 185 83 F. Supp. 284 (N.D. Ohio, 1949), 341 U.S. 593 (1951).

there is little difficulty in the Supreme Court's conclusion that the arrangement between the American company and its foreign affiliates was a "combination" or a "conspiracy" which could violate Section 1.

Sometimes on analysis the issue in these cases seems to be more one of "contract" than of "combination" or "conspiracy".

In the GMAC and companion cases, for example, the issue was whether the Big Three motor companies, sued separately, required their dealers to use financing supplied by a subsidiary finance company. And further, did that fact, if proved, violate 

Section 1? To require the automobile dealers to use company sponsored financing was a decision reached by a group of employees of each of the big motor companies, and carried out by them. It was also found to be almost a condition of remaining a dealer 

that is, an unwritton term of the dealers' contracts with their supplier. From another point of view, it represented a combination of functions the sale of cars and of financial services. For General Motors to force the dealers to accept this combination substantially restrained competition for the business of financing the sale of their cars. 

While all of the cases in the Supreme Court, and the GMAC case, where conspiracy has been found among wholly owned subsidiaries and affiliates can be explained on other grounds, the certain language has suggested to some that liability could turn on accidents of corporate form. While the decisions on illegality in both cases may be otherwise justified, we believe that the decree in the Timken case, requiring competition among companies now fully controlled by American Timken, sacrifices substance to form, Summary: What constitutes "Undue Limitations on Competitive 

Conditions"?

Whether conduct is an illegal restraint under Section 1 normally turns on whether it unduly restricts competition in a given market. 

Conduct may also be held to violate Section 1, whatever its economic effect, if defendants intended thereby to reduce competition substantially. Where conduct constitutes in itself an undue restricof tion or competition, the courts put correspondingly less emphasis on a showing of actual market impact. In any case, though the quantum of competition threatened or eliminated need not be so great under Section 1 as under Section 2. Section 1 ordinarily demands more than the lesser showing of injury required by Sec- 188/ tions 2, 3 and 7 of the Clayton Act. 

1887 It is true that in United States v. Socony Vacuum 011 Co., 310 U.S. 150 (1940), the Court said, although the statement was not necessary to its decision, that "power or ability to commit an offense" is not necessary "in order to convict a person of conspiring to commit it." 310 U.S. at 224, fn. 59. While that proposition is no doubt correct as an abstract statement of the law of criminal conspiracy, it has had singularly little development in antitrust jurisprudence. In Socony Vacuum, for example, by reason of the problem of venue in the case, the Court had to find, and did find, that the respondents, with less than the dominant market power of monopolists, carried out a program which materially influenced the level of prices in the District where trial was held. 89
