a. Restraints Unreasonable per se.

From almost the beginning of the Sherman Act, certain kinds of conduct, because of their "character" or "necessary effect" on competition, have been 'conclusively presumed" to violate

Section 1. Such arrangements include market price or production control, organized boycotts, and market divisions. "Where a complaint charges that the defendants have engaged in price-fixing, or have concertedly refused to deal with non-members of an association, or have licensed a patented device on condition that unpatented materials be employed in conjunction with the 189/ then the amount of commerce involved is unpatented device, immaterial because such restraints are illegal per se. 11 190/ This conclusion rests on two practical economic premises: that such programs have no business purpose other than the elimination of competition;' 191/ and second, that they can in fact work only if they are backed by a substantial degree of market power. A price fixing program will be self-defeating if customers can readily turn to alternative sources of supply. The same is true of group boycotts, resale price maintenance plans, divisions of the market, and other familiar devices within the unreasonable 189/ Tying restrictions attached to patents are of course directly covered by Section 3 of the Clayton Act, but they may also constitute violations of Section 1 of the Sherman Act. 

190/ United States v. Columbia Steel Co., 334 U.S. 495, 522-523 (1948).

Because of the character of such conduct, and the economic conditions which can alone make it effective, the courts sometimes treat the amount of commerce affected by the per se 192/ Where, violation as itself a reasonably distinct market. however, as in Appalachian Coals, Inc v. United States, supra, the court found that the defendants' program had a business purpose in addition to the elimination of competition among them in that instance, an effort to increase their collective share of the market and could not have resulted in materially influencing prices in the markets where defendants sold, it was upheld under Section 1. 

Crucial in many Section 1 cases is whether the conduct at bar amounts to price fixing, market division, or some other practice unreasonable per se. This enquiry, as in Sugar 

Institute, Socony Vacuum, Appalachian Coals, the 1948 Cement

Institute case may require an analysis of the market setting and effects of defendants' conduct. Since the imagination of man, and the varieties of market problems, are equally unlimited, enquiries of this kind are often complex. In other cases, such as Trenton Potteries, the task is easier, since no claim is made that defendants were not fixing market prices. In all Section 1 cases, initially for determination is the nature or effect of the conduct under review on present or potential market competition.

Where it is price-control or some other practice within the category of unreasonable per se offenses, illegality follows, Where it is not, any restraints on competition must be judged by the substantiality of their market impact.

In all the cases, except perhaps for some of those concerned with resale price maintenance or tying clauses, the courts are at pains to point out the underlying market position of the defendant or defendants. Upon this position depends the probability that the conduct reviewed will operate as a substantial restraint of competition. See, eg., Paramount Famous

Lasky Corp. v. United States, Fashion Originators Guild v. 

Federal Trade Commission, Sugar Institute, supra. In some of the price-maintenance or tying clause cases, regarded as restrictive of competition in character, no particular showing to this effect may be required, since the conduct might not as a practical matter achieve its purpose unless based on an adequate degree of market influence, All that is required in such cases, International Salt and Columbia Steel suggest, is that the effect of the restraint is not negligible. In 

Times-Picayune, however, at least by way of dictum, this position was apparently modified, by the assertion that tying clauses are invalid under Section 1 of the Sherman Act only if the defendant holds a monopolistic or dominant position in the market for the tying product and a substantial amount of commerce in the tied product is restrained. 92

b. Restraints which are not unreasonable per se offenses.

Apart from offenses unreasonable per se, conduct held to violate Section 1 includes combinations of business units, contractual arrangements which may foreclose competition, especially those which attempt to use a strong position in one market to buttress a weaker position in another, and exclusive or otherwise limited dealerships. Under Section 1, these cases require a factual examination of market effects, in order to answer the issue posed by the rule of reason: has competition been unduly reduced or foreclosed in a given relevant market?

As is indicated in our discussion of the Columbia Steel and Times-Picayune cases, one of the ambiguities left by this group of cases is whether in administering Section 1 the courts apply tests of full market dominance, as under Section 2, or the lesser requirements of Section 1 itself. We believe it is clearly proper and desirable not to require the same, but a lesser, degree of competitive restriction, where that issue is relevant, under Section 17 While both Columbia Steel and Times-Picayune 193/ can be otherwise explained, we believe resolution of this issue is important.

C. Section 2 of the Sherman Act. 

Though Section 2 contemplates a competitive market restraint more complete than Section 1, Standard 011 of 1911 makes clear that 

193/ United States v. Columbia Steel, 334 U.S. 495, 522-523 (1948). 93

the COLD 10890 from Coa BCO OTE

the same forms of conduct controlling price and restricting competitive opportunity covered by Section 1 may constitute essential ingredients of offenses under Section 2. But apart from the elements which Section 2 shares with Section 1, it establishes three separate offenses: (a) to monopolize; (b) to attempt to monopolize; and (c) to combine or conspire with any other persons to monopolize any part of the trade or commerce among the several states, or with foreign nations. 

1. "Monopolize" Monopoly plus an element of deliberateness.

Monopolizing under Section 2 consists of monopoly in the economic sense that is a high, even a dominant degree of power plus a carefully limited ingredient over market price or entry 

of purpose to use or preserve such power. Economic monopòly becomes illegal monopolization not only (1) if it was achieved or preserved by conduct violating Section 1 but also (2) if it was, even by restrictions not banned by Section 1, deliberately obtained or maintained, This element of "deliberateness" or "purpose", distinguishing economic monopoly from the offenses of monopolization, differs from the more demanding concept of "specific intent" relevant where the offense alleged is an attempt to monopolize. 

a The Monopoly Power Required in Section 2 Offenses, The broad outlines of the monopoly concept comprising this first component of the Section 2 offense have been fixed in a series of cases. It begins with the simple idea of monopoly 94

The courts class as "monopolies" under no close substitutes, Section 2 those situations where a single seller, or concert of sellers, have control over the market price. Monopoly power over price may derive from control of a dominant part of a product's available supply in a distinguishable market, or from control of some strategic aspect of the market. Courts have summed up monopoly earmarks as the power to control market price 195/ or the competitive opportunity of rivals. This requirement is satisfied even if there is no showing that prices have been raised or competitors actually excluded from the field. Thus the 

Supreme Court held in American Tobacco Co. v. United States, supra, where certiorari was limited to the question whether actual exclusion of competitors is necessary to the crime of monopolization, "that the material consideration in determining whether a monopoly exists is not that prices are raised and that competition actually is excluded, but that power exists to raise prices or to 196/ exclude competition when it is desired to do so.

Definition of the Market

Whether monopoly power for purposes of Section 2 exists requires first a definition of the market within which it is to be measured. For the distinction between competition and monopoly turns on power. Although the word "market" does not appear in the statute, it is a necessary element of the concepts of monopoly and restraint of trade upon which the statute rests. 

The appropriate market is the "area of effective competition" within which the defendant or defendants operate. 199/ "The problem of defining a market turns on discovering patterns of

Identification of markets required for solution of an antitrust problem is primarily one of fact. The starting point is to ascertain the actual competition to which the defendants are exposed, and the effect on rivals of the conduct they have undertaken. For these purposes, the market is normally identified both in terms of the trade in a product or service affected by the conduct, and the geographical areas within which such trade may be limited. The 

Sherman Act, the Supreme Court said, in Indiana Farmer's Guide 

Publishing Co. v. Prairie Farmer Publishing Co., 201 has "both a geographical and distributive significance and apply to any part of the United States as distinguished from the whole and to any part of the classes of things forming a part of interstate commerce."

There the Court reversed a Court of Appeals decision for the defendants under Sections 1 and 2. The ground for reversal was that the Court of Appeals, by considering the impact of defendants' conduct on the national market for farm journal advertising, erroneously concluded that their program could not materially restrain competition. The defendants, a group of farm journals, offered a reduced combination advertising rate to advertisers who would advertise in all the participating journals. This combination rate allegedly injured plaintiffs, 200

Thus in the Times-Picayune case the Court refused to consider newspaper, radio, and television advertising as part of the same market for Sherman Act purposes, this despite the fact there obviously is some competition among them. 

Section 2 of the Act (unlike Section 1) specifies that it applies to offenses affecting "any part of the trade or commerce among the several States, or with foreign nations." 

In fact, the difference in language between the two sections seems immaterial. Section 1 has been applied to restraints of competition in markets which are defined and identified in exactly the same way as are "markets" for purposes of Section 2. 

It is sometimes suggested that the words "any part" of trade or commerce in Section 2 refer merely to an amount of business sufficient in volume to overcome the objection de minimis non curat lex. But the concept of "the market" is not brought into 211 

Times-Picayune Publishing Co. v. United States, 345 U.S. 

594, 612 n. 31 (1953). 101

integr the en poliza they affed

the antitrust laws by the words "any part" in Section 2; it is integral to the basic concepts of "restraint of trade" and "monopolization", and the ideas of competition and monopoly on which they rest. Thus the first two closely related sections of the 

Sherman Act deal with restraints of trade or monopolizations affecting markets which constitute "any part" of the trade or commerce covered by the Act. To be sure, an appreciable amount of commerce is a "part" of commerce, but control over an appreciable amount of commerce does not necessarily mean control over an identifiable market which constitutes an appreciable "part" of commerce,

It is difficult to determine whether this error was made in 212/ 

United States v. Yellow Cab Co. 

That case can be reconciled with prevailing doctrine on the hypothesis that the defendants' various schemes, although beyond the direct reach of the Sherman Act, isolated the sale of replacements for their taxicabs in Chicago and other cities sufficiently to define a separate "market" in which they were the sole supplier. Thus, the elimination of competition for the replacements for taxicab fleets for which local (and presumably legal) monopoly positions were sought would come within the Griffith case ban on proliferations of legal monopoly. That is an appreciable amount of commerce under any standard. 213/ 

Under Sections 1 or 2, this concept of the market, Columbia

Steel makes clear, cannot be invoked whenever competition is excluded for a substantial volume of business, as in ordinary cases of vertical integration. Their legality does not turn on the intentional monopolization of the business of the company integrated. This would make all vertical integration illegal under Section 2. Instead, integration's legality rests on the extent to which analysis reveals market competition is restrained. 

Sometimes the part of commerce affected by the defendants' conduct will also be a market; but this does not necessarily follow. And without a finding as to the market involved, there is no way of determining whether or not the defendants have a given degree of market power--whether a restraint of 214/ trade stops short of monopoly, or is monopoly itself.

ii. How Much Market Power is Needed to Constitute Monopoly? 

"Monopoly power can be distinguished from the normal freedom of business only in degree. Nothing except death is an absolute. 

Just as the effectiveness of the competition of substitute commodities must be examined in order to define the market for Sherman Act purposes, so the extent to which competition from other market suppliers effectively limits the discretion of defendants will determine whether or not they are "monopolists" with Section 2. 11.

The best and simplest evidence, of course, that defendants have a high degree of power over market price, or over competitors' entry, is its actual use. Thus the Courts have always given great weight to market price increases which they could attribute to a 21.8/ defendant or defendants, and to episodes of actual exclusion 217/

The court must therefore seek to determine the existence of market power, and to measure its degree, in other ways. While the pattern of the cases is now quite clear-cut, the decisions do not provide a simple rule of thumb that enables certainty in doubtful situations. 

In view of the nature of the question, no such automatic rule could possibly be expected. The cases do articulate certain facts to be considered, and the results in the cases provide examples of what are or are not illegal degrees of market power. ff. 220/

The qualification of the Columbia Steel case is clearly correct. 

Where, as in the 1911 Standard Oil and Tobacco cases, the Pullman case and the Alcoa case, the defendants provided 90% or more of the supply of a product or service for which very close substitutes were not generally available, they surely have monopoly power. 

On the other hand, such power may also exist, as a practical matter, where the percentage of supply provided is much less. 

Analysis depends not only on the market structure, which remains under the cases a most important evidentiary fact, but on the way prices are actually formed and output decisions made. 

To determine whether monopoly power exists, courts scan market structure and behavior bearing on control over price and competitive opportunity. With regard to market structure, the factors often considered include the relative size and the strength of remaining competitors; (e.g., that the share occupied by defendant or defendants has been increasing or decreasing); freedom of entry (including reference to such factors as capital requirements, locational advantages, and the importance of advertising); "consumer demands" 223/ 

Appraising conduct allegedly affecting prices, courts examine, for example, prices' course, their

223/ 

See U.S. v. Columbia Steel Co., 334 U.S. 495, 527 (1948). 106

rice in 

*f fled custo

flexibility and relation to price trends in other industries; price competition among firms; the presence or absence of trade customs tending to reduce price competition, Since in defining monopoly the issue is whether there exists something close to a monopolist's power over market price, not the reasonableness of prices actually charged, whether profits are high or low is 224/ Nonetheless, not, strickly speaking relevant under Section 2, evidence as to profits is usually admitted, and may sometimes throw light on other problems, such as the possibility of entry, or even the existence of power over price. Thus in American 

Tobacco Co. v. United States 225/ that defendants raised prices, in a price leadership pattern, during a severe depression, and for a year managed to increase their profits evidenced their monopoly power and the barriers to new competitors' entry. 

Cases defining monopoly power show less variation in actual result than is sometimes assumed. While there have been undoubted changes in economic attitude and even of philosophy in interpreting Section 2, the Court has in no case since 1911 undertaken to recast the essential doctrines of Standard Oil Co. v. United States.

In the Paramount case, the elements of monopoly and combination were similarly related to show that eight defendants together had acquired the dominant market power of monopolists in parts of the movie distribution and exhibition business via concerted programs, many of which violated Section 1. Defendants possessed, not merely monopoly power over the price at which films were exhibited in theatres, but power, based on theatre ownership and control, to deny rivals access to first-run theatres, where films could be shown during a protected time 

243 Id. at 810. 112

int Con deti 1d to

interval at relatively higher admission prices, The Supreme Court reversed not merely because the District Court had wrongly defined the relevant market, but also because it erred in identifying the element of intent under Section 2; and failed to find whether vertical integration in this case violated the Sherman Act by reason either of the power it created or its attendant purpose.

On remand, the District Court found that five of the eight defendants who owned theatres held at least 70% of the firstrun theatres in the 92 largest cities of the country, and 60% of the first-run theatres in cities between 25,000 and 100,000 in population. In addition, they received 73% of domestic flm rentals, except for that apparently distinct product, the "western" film. "These figures certainly indicate, when coupled with the strategic advantages of vertical integration, a power to exclude 244/ competition from these markets when desired. 

While the Court did not find that the companies had agreed to divide markets, the existing pattern of their ownership of theatres meant that in many areas only one or two of the defendants had theatres, so that competition among them was minimized, and each was dependant on the others for outlets in areas where it had not theatres itself. 

In this case, the system of theatre ownership by each of the five major defendants was held to be a means for a variety of restraints 

2447 United States v. Paramount Pictures, Inc., 85 F. Supp. 881, 894 (S.D.N.Y., 1949). 113

brid *: 188 thro posi firs of t CO fix так red

through which the defendants had achieved and kept their monopoly position. For "the ownership of what was generally the best first-run theatres, coupled with the possession by the defendants 245/ of the best pictures, enabled them substantially to control the market" The facts were held to show actual exclusion of competitors made possible by the defendants' collective power to fix clearances and runs and their ownership of theatres. Divorcement of theatre ownership from distribution was therefore required, as "the only adequate means of terminating the conspiracy and preventing any resurgence of [defendants'] monopoly 246/ power"

From all this it seems clear that under Section 2 an illegal degree of power over price is a good deal more than the absence of a perfectly elastic demand curve, i.e., more than the availability of alternatives in pricing policy, which many producers have to some degree. There must be "dominance" on the part of a single firm or a combination. This power, especially when considered as a power to control the competitive opportunity of rivals, need not necessarily be associated with any given or fixed degree of market occupancy, but may stem from some other strategic position in the market.

Measuring monopoly power depends upon a full evaluation of the market and its functioning, to determine whether on balance

While the decisions illuminate the economic theory of the courts in evaluating these facts, they provide no magic formula for simplifying the quest. b The additional element of "deliberateness" required to make monopoly "monopolization".

The existence of monopoly power 

"monopoly in the concrete", 247/ 

Standard Oil Co. of New Jersey v. United States, 

does not by itself prove the offense of monopolization. Under Section 2, the offense is the existence of the power to exclude or to raise prices, coupled with "the purpose or intent to exercise that power" 248/ The requisite intent for this purpose is not a "specific" intent to monopolize, but rather a conclusion based on how the monopoly power was acquired, maintained or used. This purpose, required to distinguish "monopoly" from "monopolizing", is sharply differentiated from the proof of "specific" intent to monopolize required where the charge is an unrealized attempt to monopolize. 249/

evidence considered to show a "specific" intent to monopolize was treated as confirming a presumption, "to say the least" based on the economic position and corporate history of the 250/ defendant company.

Situations of monopoly may arise or be maintained in various ways. They may result, on the one hand, from the supremacy of the monopolist as victor in a race of competition, by reason of his technical superiority, or his ownership of an important patent. Monopoly may develop, on the other, from the absorption of competitors, a series of steps in violation of 

Section 7 of the Clayton Act, or Section 1 of the Sherman Act, or of both. Or monopoly can emerge and be preserved by driving competitors out of business, by putting deliberate business impediments in their path, or by combining or conspiring with competitors, as in the American Tobacco and Paramount cases 251/ 252 The history and business policy of a monopoly have a distinct bearing on the proof of this second element - "deliberateness, 1! 1: as distinguished from "specific intent. Clearly "deliberateness" is shown if monopoly has been established or maintained by means of restraints of trade illegal under Section 1. It is equally satisfied if proof convinces the trier of facts that the defendants'

Often, and perhaps usually, the courts will infer that a monopoly position has been "deliberately" maintained in this sense as a matter of "objective" rather than "subjective" intent, relying on business practice to support the conclusion that men intend the natural consequences of their acts. To hold otherwise, in Section 2 cases where the defendant or defendants have actual monopoly power, would "make nonsense" of the act, Judge Learned Hand said, "for no monopolist monopolizes unconscious of what he is doing". 253 In such cases, therefore, no showing of intent is required beyond 254/ "the mere intent to do the act.' 11The two cases which have given rise to most discussion are those where defendant argues monopoly was "thrust upon it", as the inevitable result of market forces; and those where a group of companies, together possessing dominant market power, have acted in concert to gain, pool or hold it.

For example, consider the recent United Shoe Machinery case, supra. the principal types of machines used in manufacturing shoes. 

Defendant supplied, on lease terms only, all It dealt with approximately 90% of shoe factories, and supplied more than 75% of the demand for the types of shoe machinery which it provided. This position, coupled with United Shoe's business methods, was considered to result in effective control of the shoe machinery market. The company had prevailed in 

1918 against a monopolizing charge, and its leasing methods 258/ had later been revised to conform to the Supreme Court's adverse findings under Section 3 of the Clayton Act. 259/ Thus,

United Shoe's leasing practices were treated as legal. The 

Court nonetheless concluded that the Company's dominance was maintained by its business policy, or, at the least, that the defendant had failed to prove its monopoly position was the "inevitable" consequence of ability, natural forces, or law. 260/ "United's control does not rest solely on its original constitution, its ability, its research, or its economies of scale. There are other barriers to competition, and these barriers were erected by United's own business policies. Much of United's market power is traceable to the magnetic ties inherent in its system of leasing, and not selling, its more important machines. lease-only system of distributing complicated machines The

and dismantling of plants was likewise important evidence of intent to obtain and preserve a monopoly position In like fashion, the Court in the Alcoa case summed up evidence establishing defendants intent to monopolize: 

Alcoa in 1940 was not "the passive beneficiary of a monopoly, following upon an involuntary elimination of competitors by automatically operative economic forces. 

Already in 1909, when its last lawful monopoly ended, it sought to strengthen its position by unlawful practices, and these concededly continued until 1912. 

In that year it had two plants ** at which it produced less than 42 million pounds of ingot; in 1934 it had five plants ** and its production had risen to about 327 million pounds, an increase of almost eight-fold. 

Meanwhile not a pound of ingot had been produced by anyone else in the United States. This increase and this continued and undisturbed control did not fall undesigned into 'Alcoa's' lap; obviously it could not have done so, It could only have resulted * * from a persistent determination to maintain the Alcoa's 1912 control. ** There were at least one or two abortive attempts to enter the industry, but 'Alcoa' effectively anticipated and forestalled all competition, and succeeded in holding the field alone. True, it stimulated demand and opened new uses for the metal, but not without making sure that it could supply what it had evoked. 

There is no dispute as to this: 'Alcoa' avows it as evidence of the skill, energy and initiative with which it has always conducted its business; as a reason why, having won its way by fair means, it should be commended and not dismembered. We need charge it with no moral derelictions after 1912; we may assume that all it claims for itself is true. The only question is whether it falls within the exception established in favor of those who do not seek, but cannot avoid, the control of a market. It seems to us that that question scarcely survives its statement. It was not inevitable that it should always anticipate increases in the demand for ingot and be prepared to supply them. Nothing compelled it to keep doubling and redoubling its capacity before others entered the field. It insists that it never excluded competitors; but we can think of no more effective exclusion than progressively to embrace each new opportunity as it opened, and to face every newcomer with new capacity already geared into a great organization, 121

​ㄢ

having the advantage of experience, trade connections and the elite of personnel. Only in case we interpret 'exclusion as limited to maneuvers not honestly industrial, but actuated solely by a desire to prevent competition, can such a course, indefatigably pursued, be deemed not 'exclusionary'. So to limit it would in our judgment emasculate the Act; would permit just such consolidations as it was designed to prevent. "264/ 265/ This statement, specifically approved by the Supreme Court in the American Tobacco case, is sometimes misconstrued to suggest that "monopoly" may become "monopolization" merely by being active, enterprising and dynamic. This construction implies the safest course for large companies is passive stagnation, with a gradual loss of market share a business policy directly at odds with antitrust aims. Such is not the teaching 

of Alcoa. Defendants' conduct was there held to confirm "monopolization" not merely because Alcoa was progressive, but because it acted with calculation to head off every attempted entry into the field. That history of frustrating potential entrants and the vital fact that no company succeeded in breaking into a basic manufacturing industry, whose technology was widely known, over a period of more than 25 years, while Alcoa's output increased 800%, convinced the Court, as a practical matter, than Alcoa's monopoly position rested on a good deal more than its technical and business skill. ii. Monopoly by Combination: Proof of Combination or Conspiracy. 

As for monopolization through combination rather than by a single company's internal growth, the test is the same. As the Supreme Court said in American Tobacco Co. v. United States: 

"A correct interpretation of the statute and of the authorities makes it the crime of monopolizing, under § 2 of the Sherman Act, for parties, as in these cases, to combine or conspire to acquire or maintain the power to exclude competitors from any part of the trade or commerce among the several states or with foreign nations, provided they also have such a power that they are able, as a group, to exclude actual or potential competition from the field and provided that they have the intent and purpose to exercise that power. $1 266/ Once combination is here proved, however, the requirement of "purpose or intent," needed to establish monopolization, presents no real problem. When several companies achieve monopoly power through combination or conspiracy, it cannot be said that monopoly has been "thrust upon" them. In the Tobacco case, the Court quoted with approval the following language from Judge Hand's opinion in the Alcoa case: 266 "It does not follow because 'Alcoa' had such a monopoly that it 'monopolized' the ingot market; it may not have achieved monopoly; monopoly may have been thrust upon it. 

If it had been a combination of existing smelters which united the whole industry, and controlled the production of all aluminum ingot, it would certainly have 'monopolized' 

328 U.S. 781, at 809 (1948). 123

Thus

CODE 

30 CC pre

the market. In several decisions the Supreme Court has decreed the dissolution of such combinations, although they had engaged in no unlawful trade practices. * * * We may start therefore with the premise that to have combined ninety percent of the producers of ingot would have been to 'monopolize' the ingot market. 267/ Thus, if monopoly power has been achieved through combination or conspiracy, Section 2 has been violated without more. "Intent or purpose to exercise that power" is proved by the fact of 268/ combination or conspiracy.

The basis of the rule is the practical one that companies do not combine or conspire without an intent to make use of the power they thereby obtain. 

Whether there has been a "combination" or "conspiracy" raises problems in common with Section 1. However, the problem tends to be more difficult in Section 2 "monopolization" cases.

The illegal degree of market power requisite for monopolization is greater than that necessary for unreasonable restraints of trade. And a high degree of market power is more likely possessed by a few companies who can readily coordinate their policies without numerous formal meetings, contracts, or other common hallmarks of "conspiracy" present in Section 1 cases.

Not thus far raised has been the issue of combination to monopolize in a case where the record consisted entirely of economic evidence that a small number of companies merely✓ functioned in an interdependent way. Evidence of such inter- 

267/ 148 F. 2d at 429. 

268/ 328 U.S. at 809 124

Mare on vant shor beha Less depend in pro it may

dependent behavior is unquestionably relevant and material in proving combination or conspiracy. Depending on circumstances, it may or may not support inferences of combination. the Initially at issue is the extent such behavior is "conscious." 

More specifically, to what degree is each firm's action dependent on knowledge that like conduct by competitors will ensue? Relevant here, of course, is evidence of business justification showing that, whatever competitors' conduct, individual firm behavior made business sense. Once dependence or "consciousness" - is shown, then significant becomes the nature of the "parallelism" involved. In the case of "parallel" pricing, for example: how closely have each firm's prices paralleled competitor's? How long has the "parallel" pattern endured? Has it involved price rises as well as slumps? How consistent has been the time lag between one firm's pricing and like action by competitors? Is the product involved homc genous or differentiated? 

Upon the answers to these and like questions depends the significance in any case of "conscious parallelism" in pricing. 

The courts have shown a good deal of insight in dealing with organized informational services, or plans for delivered prices, common distribution or buying practices, and the like. 

They have indicated an understanding of what such practices can accomplish, and why they may be economically advantageous to sellers or buyers in reducing their uncertainty as to the policy 125

and res and in verdic Parano parall advant evider firms 

Barke the d out t

and responses of their rivals.

Thus in the recent Tobacco case, and in United States v. Paramount Pictures, Inc. 

269/ the jury verdict (in the Tobacoo case) and the findings of fact (in the Paramount case) rested in considerable part on evidence of parallel behavior designed to secure for the entire group the advantages of organized oligopoly. There was, of course, other evidence in these cases as well, bearing on the knowledge of the firms in adhering to plans, often complex, for organizing the market and establishing its rules. And in the Paramount case, the defendant companies were also closely interdependent throughout the country, both because no one company produced enough films to supply its theatres throughout the year, and because of the geographical pattern of their theatre ownership. 

Perhaps the most inclusive definition of "combination" was given by the Supreme Court in F.T.C. v. Cement Institute, "It is enough to warrant a finding of a 'combination' within the meaning of the Sherman Act, if there is evidence that persons with knowledge that concerted action was contemplated and invited, give adherence to and then participated in a scheme." 

Significant is that the Cement case involved considerable 270/ evidence apart from interdependent business behavior, especially policing practices and punishments, bids identical to a fraction of a cent, showing that the basing point system developed not automatically, but from concert. cited in the Cement opinion. 271/ The reasoning which justified the findings in the Cement case need not be fully applicable where the system of interdependent action is more flexible, and less perfect. Thus in Theatre Enterprises, Inc., v. Paramount Film Distributing Corp. 

the Court distinguished identical individual behavior from interdependence, concluding that here is no coordination of policy. 

That case involved an appeal from a judgment for defendants based on a jury finding. The petitioner's claim was that he should have had a directed verdict. The petitioner owned a modern theatre in a suburban shopping center in the outskirts of Baltimore. 

The defendants, the major movie companies distributing in Baltimore, refused to lease him films on a first-run basis. The claim for damages was for a period from 1949, after the date of the decrees in the Paramount case. Apart from the Paramount case decrees, admitted as rather remote prima facie evidence, there was no evidence of combination among the defendants other than the fact that they all refused to give petitioners first-run treatment. 

On the other hand, the defense introduced elaborate evidence as to the business reason which led them individually not to class the plaintiff's theater as a first-run house, The Court refused the petitioner's claim:

271/ Interstate Circuit v. United States., 306 U.S. 208, 226-27 (1939); United States v. Masonite Corp., 316 U.S. 265, 275 (1942); 

United States v. Bausch & Lomb Co., 321 U.S. 707, 772 (1944); 

United States v. U.S. Gypsum Co., 333 U.S. 364, 393-94 (1948). 

272/ 74 S. Ct. 257, 259-60 (1954). 272/ 127

"The crucial question is whether respondents' conduct toward petitioner stemmed from independent decision or from an agreement, tacit or express. To be sure, business behavior is admissible circumstantial evidence from which the fact finder may infer agreement. [citing cases]. But this Court has never held that proof of parallel business behavior conclusively establishes agreement or, phrased differently that such behavior itself constitutes a Sherman

Act offense. Circumstantial evidence of consciously parallel behavior may have made heavy inroads into the traditional judicial attitude toward conspiracy; but 'conscious parallelism' has not yet read conspiracy out of the Sherman 

Act entirely."

The Court thus concludes that while parallel business behavior is evidence from which agreement may be inferred, it is not sufficient of itself to overcome a jury verdict that the defendants' conduct was not in fact concerted.

Attempts, Combinations and Conspiracies to Monopolize 

Section 2 specifies two offenses attempts to monopolize entirely dis- and combinations or conspiracies to monopolize tinct from monopolization. In neither of these is any showing required that the desired end of monopoly power was attained. 

In all cases of attempt, and in those conspiracy cases where monopoly power has not been achieved, these offenses require proof of something not required where monopolization has succeeded 

proof of a specific or subjective intent to accomplish an unlawful result. 273/ The basic statement of the rule

D. "Trade or Commerce among the several states, or with foreign nations

Underlying all Sherman Act problems is the scope of "trade or commerce among the several States, or with foreign nations." 

Measuring "trade or commerce" limits raises at least three principal issues: (1) The functional scope of the words "trade or commerce"; (2) the territorial distinction between interstate and local commerce; and (3) the breadth of "trade or commerce *** with foreign nations."

The phrase in the statute corresponds closely to the language used in Article 1, section 8 of the Constitution, and has been 277/ construed, except with regard to foreign commerce, to be as inclusive as the constitutional limits of Congress' power to regulate commerce. As the Court put it in United States v. 

South Eastern Underwriters Ass'n., that in passing the Sherman

Act Congress meant "to go to the utmost extent of its constitutional power in restraining trust and monopoly agreements. "278/

​ECONOMIC STANDARDS OF COMPETITION AND MONOPOLY 

The antitrust laws aim to safeguard the maximum freedom of management decision compatible with the maintenance of competitive production and distribution techniques. These laws have, therefore, been designed to prevent any individual company, or group of companies, from exercising its own business options in ways that unduly restrict the range of choice open to other companies and, therefore, unreasonably impair competition. While the different antitrust statutes set different standards for the degree of impairment of competition which will be deemed illegal, all are concerned with the character of competitive opportunity in markets. 

Though antitrust goals are phrased generally, antitrust case law is rooted in facts disclosed in individual cases. 

As a result, the case law applies to limited sets of facts, and presents conceptual gaps which generalizations from the facts in decided cases do not fill. Economic theory, by contrast, is designed to apply to all possible facts within its purview and therefore, has not directly addressed itself to criteria capable of meeting standards of legal evidence.

Nevertheless, the distance between case law and economic theory is not so wide as the separate histories of the two disciplines would suggest. The antitrust laws are, after all, concerned with individual company activities and 139

vith 

Fray 44. 

鼻尖 ​} don to 1 18.. phe 

Both of

AT

with relations among companies as they affect the market freedom of other companies. These laws are, therefore, addressed to business facts and these facts are the same, regardless of the "legal" or "economic" labels on the criteria determining what facts are relevant or how they should be assessed.

The growing complexity of American economic life has presented the courts with an increasing number of cases where the central issue is not the practices of a company, or a group of companies, as such, but the actual or potential effects of these activities on other companies suppliers, competitors or customers. At the same time, economists are coming to view their earlier formulation of the theory of perfect or pure competition as a theoretical model. Such models are of use in suggesting problems and methods of study, but departures do not by themselves have fixed significance. 

Economists have in recent years been giving increasing attention to the study of factual situations and have developed a more complex and many-sided theory which takes account of a wider range of factors manifesting competition and monopoly. The theory of workable or effective competition is the result. As presented in the Report, this theory is designed to permit systematic study of a greater variety of market facts than the earlier theory would have suggested. 

The disparate focus of antitrust case law and the economic theory of competition and monopoly should not, therefore, 140

obsc Mark thet bee le

obscure the fact that, for the courts, the tests of whether the laws have been violated have always been essentially market tests, while similarly for economists, the tests of whether their theories have practical validity have also been market tests.

The section of this Report on legal criteria shows, for example, that unreasonable per se violations can be identified, as such, because their effects on competition are known. The legal and economic problems, in connection with such violations as price fixing and agreements to limit output or divide markets, therefore, revolve around determination of whether the practices, in fact, exist in particular market situations. 

In contrast, practices to which the rule of reason is applied are those from whose occurrence a fixed set of competitive effects do not inevitably follow. Accordingly, where such practices come under antitrust scrutiny, their market consequences must be weighed.

The following section of this Report summarizes the generic meaning of competition and monopoly; explores the relation between pure and perfect competition, on the one hand, and workable competition, on the other; and outlines the the factors bearing on the identification of workable competition.

These factors are proposed as guides for determining whether there have been significant failures of competition. 141

In any case, the relative significance of each factor may . vary. To the bar, enforcement agencies and courts, they suggest means for developing and presenting admissible evidence and for weighing its relevance, materiality and probative value in a particular market context. They are not offered as rigid norms, departure from any one of which mechanically signals antitrust violation. Instead, the criteria of workable competition take on antitrust meaning only as yardsticks for suggesting the economic significance of given facts. 

A. Economic Definitions of "Monopoly", "Competition" and "Workable Competition.' 11 

1. Generic Concepts of Competition and Monopoly 

The essence of full monopoly power resides in being the sole source of a product, so that the buyer must meet the seller's terms or go without. The essence of competition is to free the buyer from this power by access to alternative sources of the product. The same considerations apply where the problem is a buyer's monopoly.296/ 

The basic economic aspect of monopoly is the seller's power over the terms on which he trades: that is, his power to increase profits by giving less and asking more, rather than by giving more and asking less. All the factors and elements which constitute 

296 In this discussion, unless otherwise indicated, what is said of competition among sellers would apply equally where the restraints on competition exist or develop among buyers, and the pressure of these restraints are felt by sellers. 142

this to sen ine pre vis SEI

this idea are summed up by the convenient short-hand reference to monopoly power as power over price. Monopoly power in this sense implies the monopoly seller's discretion to maintain inefficient methods or plants, and his relative freedom from pressure to reduce costs, to develop new products, or otherwise to innovate. The existence of monopoly power, lodged in private hands which are free to pursue their own advantage, is generally to be condemned aside from the question whether it is used "reasonably". It is unsafe power to lodge in private hands, making the monopolist a judge in his own case. On this basic point, the law and the economists are in full accord.297/ 

The idea of competition itself, as distinguished from the many variant technical concepts of competition, is not so easy to define. The literature of economics uses a good many concepts of "competition" for various purposes: perfect and imperfect competition, pure competition, monopolistic and workable competition, duopoly, oligopoly, and several others. 

Competition itself, as a generic concept embracing all its subdivisions, implies two ideas, which sometimes overlap, but do not coincide. In the first place, the word competition denotes only the presence of more than one seller in a market, 297 This does not imply that the presence of monopoly power in all cases constitutes "monopolization" under Section 2 of the Sherman Act. 143

-- and identifies a condition of rivalry among them the selfinterested and independent rivalry of two or more private competitors. This sense of the word "competition" often appears in popular usage. But there is a second generic sense in which the word and the idea of competition are used, both in law and economics, and especially in the law. In this definition, "competition" is contrasted with "monopoly" with respect to the degree of market power a seller (or group of sellers) possesses. This second meaning of competition can be summed up as identifying a market condition in which the rivalry of sellers, of itself, prevents the continued existence of the discretionary market power of monopoly over price and output. Whether competitive rivalry in a given market is of the kind which tends typically to produce "competitive" or "monopolistic" market results in this latter sense depends upon the structure of that market, upon the way in which it conditions and influences the decisions of businessmen as to price and output, and upon the character of market incentives for innovation and cost reduction. To answer this central question, both in economics and in law, more evidence is needed than the simple fact that the market contains two or more sellers or buyers. One must consider the number and relative size of buyers and sellers, conditions affecting the entry of new firms and the growth of existing firms, relations among rivals in the process of making market decisions, 144

as well as other market factors. This second generic meaning of the word "competition", requires further analysis before it can be fully useful either in economics or law, notably on the question of how much market power sellers may have in a given market situation without becoming "monopolists" in one or another sense of that term. At this point, we wish merely to distinguish between two common meanings of the word competition, and to make it clear that in this Report the word "competition" is used in the second sense stated above.

The concept of monopoly, and our definition of competition are used by economics and the law in closely similar ways. 

Both in economics and in law, these are not concepts of all or none. Markets exist in great variety, and they can be classified at many points along a spectrum extending from the complete monopoly of a single seller at one end, to the markets of thousands of small suppliers and purchasers, at the other. 

Economics attempts to divide market situations into categories for various purposes; and legal criteria under different provisions of the antitrust laws differ substantially in the quantum of restraint on competition they proscribe. In utilizing economic concepts of "competition" and "monopoly" in the analysis of legal problems therefore, the task is two-fold: (1) to isolate and define the economic concepts relevant in helping to answer questions posed by the law, and to adapt them to the different standards established by different statutes, on the 145

In dealing with the distinction between "competition" and "monopoly" economics and the law are concerned with the same issues, and emphasize the same factors as vital. Our present purpose is to attempt a clarification of certain economic concepts which may be useful to the law in dealing with ideas of market power appropriate to the solution of many problems arising under the antitrust laws. In the course of that effort, a central preliminary task for economists is to define the circumstances under which competition becomes effective in preventing a concern from having a quantitatively substantial degree of the power that characterizes complete monopoly. For this purpose, the issues center on "effective" or "workable" competition, or its absence. 

2. "Workable" or "Effective" Competition 

The concept of "workable" or "effective" competition can perhaps best be described as the economists' attempt to identify the conditions which could provide appropriate leads for policy in assuring society the substance of the advantages which competition should provide. It is a kind of economist's "rule reason" -- not, of course, to be confused with the legal rule of reason, but analogous to it in the sense that it is also an acknowledgement of the inevitability of human judgment and 146

OPEN BIDANG M 

ག་ག་ག་ bual deny youn

GOČNONTO

discretion in classifying different forms of social behavior. 

The basic characteristic of effective competition is that no one seller, and no organized group of sellers, can hold for long the power to choose its level of profits by giving less and charging more. Where there is workable competition, rival sellers, whether existing competitors or new or potential entrants into the field, would keep this power in check by offering or threatening to offer effective inducements, so long as the profits to be anticipated in the industry are more attractive than those in other employments, when all risks and other deterrents are taken into account. The result would be to force the seller who sought to increase his profits above this level by employing a high-price, limited-output monopoly policy either to give it up, or to lose ground to his rivals at a rate sufficient to reduce his total earnings, thus defeating his policy. In economic theory, this condition is described as one in which the individual firm confronts a relatively flat demand-curve for its output, at least in the moderately long-run, usually accompanied by or based upon high cross-elasticities of demand. 298/ 

The individual seller 298/ The term "demand curve" in economics, as used here, refers to the amounts which a firm can sell in any given period of time at different prices. Thus a "relatively flat demand curve" means that the firm cannot expect to increase its own price much by reducing output, nor need it reduce its price significantly in order to increase its own sales. Put otherwise, a "relatively flat" demand curve means that a small increase (or decrease) in the firm's price will cause a large decrease 147

plynied lefoon to our ovode st slovit ald og houd to wel bedrogadoss vilecou baarsb to nofdiok nh leo kao anti & doka 

Värvide for "

cannot control his rivals' offerings, and those offerings set narrow limits on his discretion as to price and production. 

He must, in the light of his own costs, adjust his offerings to a market scale of prices for offerings of different quality or attractiveness which he knows he cannot much influence, especially in the moderately long run, either by changing his own price or his output. This condition inflicts penalties on high costs or poor services. To bring this result about, it is necessary that rivals be free in fact to compete by lower prices and better service or products, if they can achieve low enough costs to enable them to do so; and that no seller have power to limit this freedom of his rivals, and thus escape the pressures and penalties which effective competition imposes. 

The market pressures which effective competition imposes upon each seller derive from the self-interested rivalry of his competitors. The essential character of this rivalry is to promote the competitor's economic interest by offering buyers inducements attractive enough to cause them to deal with him, 298 (cont'd) (or increase) in the amount it can sell; and that a considerable increase (or decrease) in its output will cause little decrease (or increase) in the prevailing price. "Cross-elasticities of demand" is a term measuring the relationship between the market demand for two commodities, in terms of the relative change in the amount of one which can be sold (assuming its price is unchanged), as a consequence of a change in the price of the other. Thus a high cross-elasticity of demand between commodities means that a relative change in the price of one promptly and directly affects the demand for the other. 148

-- by tim and

The CO

in free bargaining, and in the face of inducements offered by his rivals 299

The inducements consist of quantity, quality, time and place of delivery, incidental services, selling effort and price. The chief enabling condition is efficient operation.

The rivalry may take the form of trying to enlarge one's share of the market by offering something more attractive than one's competitors, or to avoid a reduction of one's share of the business by offsetting the superior attractions of rivals' offers. In other words, competition includes both aggressive and defensive tactics. 

Competitive rivalry in a given business situation may or may not be capable of developing enough force to deny any one seller or group of sellers in the market significant degrees of the power of a monopolist to determine the price he will charge, and other conditions of his sale. Whether this condition is achieved normally depends on the structure and behavior of the market. Active competition, for example, may involve initial moves by one competitor, the responses of the buyers, and the further responses of rival sellers. 

In many cases, the distinction between effective and ineffective competition may depend upon the speed of these 299 The wording is intentionally chosen to avoid stating or implying that the customers prefer to deal with him, or that he offers or tries to offer superior inducements, such as lover prices, The definition should not be read to imply thet A is not competing with B unless he invariably offers a lower price than B's, 149

responses, both the absolute and relative speed, and the certainty or uncertainty of buyers' responses and rivals' responses. The character of these responses may often be a function not only of the aggressiveness and business policies of the rival sellers and buyers, but of their number and relative size and of the nature of their expectations. 

The market pressures of effective competition can be, and should properly be, quite severe. The firm rendering service inferior to that of its rivals would be seriously handicapped; and a firm maintaining superiority over its rivals has a prospect of increasing its volume of trade progressively at their expense so long as it can maintain this superiority. 

The penalties of unsuccessful competition may take the form of either a positive shrinkage or a failure to expand, and may be viewed in a short-term or a long-run perspective. It is ordinarily bad competitive policy, and possibly suicidal, to attempt to squeeze out the utmost profit that can be made in a single year, at least where other firms can enter the field, or where substitute goods or services are at all available. If one seller is short sighted enough to attempt this, others will grow at his expense, in an effectively competitive market. Good practice, viewed from such a standpoint, can seldom be determined very precisely, But this is a matter which those concerned with public policy can leave to the market itself, provided the conditions of market rivalry are sufficiently free, active and healthy. 150

Definition of "the market."

In evaluating the market forces which together would characterize effective competition, the rivalry of close substitutes has the same effect as that of competing producers of differentiated products or services within the same industry. 

Technical advance has often made the rivalry of close substitutes quite effective. But this fact emphatically does not mean that public policy can afford to be indifferent to the elimination of competition within the industry. In the interest of a sufficient number of rivals, and of rivalry that extends to all buyers and all uses, rivalry within the industry is always important.

For purposes of the antitrust laws, and of económic analysis, the "market" is the sphere of competitive rivalry within which the crucial transfer of buyers' patronage from one supplier to another can take place freely. The boundaries of an "industry" or "market" will often be uncertain and controversial, and a definition appropriate in one case may be inappropriate in another. For this purpose, a market is an economic relationship among sellers and buyers, not always defined by geographical area alone. To ascertain whether a firm or group of firms has significant degrees of the market power of monopoly, "the market" should include all firms whose production has so immediate and substantial an effect on the prices and production of the firms in question 151

bull go for whatam reco 13. 1. 

that th direct in a direct all of the p bad b the -1480 prod ind (or Are

that the actions of the one group cannot be explained without direct and constant reference to the other. One should include in a market all firms whose products are in fact good and directly available substitutes for one another, and exclude all others. Thus it is appropriate to consider the coal and the petroleum industries as separate markets, although there had been shift and substitution between their products. Where the products of different industries compete directly as alternatives for the same use, then the market for that class of products should include the rival goods supplied by different industries. One should combine into one market two products (or two areas) if a fall in the price of one product (or one area) will promptly lead to a relatively large diversion of Purchasers from the other product (or area). The appropriate market area may be international, national, regional or local. 

b. Tests of Performance. 

In seeking to distinguish "effective monopoly" from "effective competition", the ultimate question is whether the pressures of the market situation, as distinguished from the motives and policies of competitors (i.e., their adoption of low-price programs), are such as to tend of themselves to bring about the main beneficial effects which constitute the economic reasons why we try to maintain competition in our economy. With respect to the immediate market performance of 152

industry and commerce, these gains include, among other things: (1) the stimulation to progress in the development of new products and in offering customers an adequate and constantlyrenewed assortment to choose from; (2) reduction of costs of production; (3) diffusion of the benefits of cost-reduction, in lower prices and in returns to all factors of production in accordance with their productivity; and (4) the attraction of capital to prospectively more profitable uses, and the gradual withdrawal of capital from prospectively less profitable or unprofitable uses, whether by direct shifts of resources, or by the weeding-out of inefficient enterprises, ownership groups, or managements.

The process of adjusting the employment of resources to the pressures and demands of a workably competitive market is one of the chief means through which the diffusion of the benefits of competition takes place. As to profits, these competitive pressures mean that in the long run investors can keep no more than the minimum amount of profit necessary as an incentive to bring about their production, and to invest the effort and capital, and to incur the risks which it requires, Over time, effective competition, implying the expansion (or contraction or withdrawal) of existing firms, or the entry of new ones, would reduce (or raise) the profit for a representative firm with respect to any particular product or service towards the point where bringing forth the supply earns no more than 1.53

coul labo thro to en Be

could be earned in alternative employments of the capital, labor and management involved. Effective competition's longrun tendency to equalize the attractiveness of investments throughout the economy at minimum levels closely corresponding to the marginal productivity of capital in alternative uses may be viewed from the standpoint of the over-all profits in different industries, the relative profits of different firms in an industry, and the relative profitability of different products, where one firm may make a number of products. 

Beyond the broadest identification of trends, industry-wide profit averages have significance only in measuring the comparative pull of different industries in their competition for capital that is, the comparative profit and risk prospects of different industries. For firms, not industries, are the units through which economic decisions are made. The pressure of competition to drive rates of profit throughout the economy towards minimum levels is consistent with continuingly high rates of return for firms with better-than-average location, resources, efficiency or rate of innovation. Where firms produce more than one product, cost allocations can at best afford only a crude and imperfect guide to the relative profitableness of different products, and business policy may diverge from such indications as it does give. But a considerable check on such divergence may come from the competition of rival firms which differ in their product-mix. And the general force of competition in this direction, even if deferred by imperfect knowledge and foresight, is of unquestioned importance in multi-product industries.. 154

Kowth one mo -kila-Sambong rhoud et verib keldy wonde fake 30 the цве 18 ind pro ti

The impact of effective competition on profits throughout the economy, and thus on the flow of capital to alternative uses, is one of its most important functions 

However, it is altogether normal in a competitive economy that a given industry or group of firms can earn extremely high (or low) profits at any given time, and over considerable periods of time. For such profits are the signals through which a market economy attracts capital and other resources to more productive uses, and drives them out of less productive uses. 

For these reasons, reduction of costs and moderation of profits, or other tests of "progressive performance" in industry, do not as such prove the existence of effective competition. 

The rate of technical progress, and the level of profit in a firm or an industry can reflect many forces other than the presence of effective competition. In some doubtful cases, in conjunction with other more material facts, they may be considered to furnish collateral or corroborative evidence of the nature and significance of competitive forces in the industry, In terms of progress, a young industry, or one in process of exploiting recent basic scientific discoveries, might make 155

54digas do peit me ne sand by these dar a b alt of dem dec

a better showing than an older and more mature industry, although the latter might be more competitive. In terms of a low rate of profit, an industry with declining Our demand might show a very low rate in spite of being decidedly monopolistic. On the other hand, a monopolistic industry, with dynamic management and active research, might perform better than a fairly competitive branch of industry which is inferior in these characteristics. public policy, however, is founded on the assumption that competition will on the average result in much more progressiveness and efficiency than monopoly. We also desire to have, so far as possible, a government of laws rather than of men, and to avoid placing an unbearably heavy burden on administrative expertise and judicial discretion. For these reasons, economic and non-economic alike, the antitrust laws have properly placed their emphasis on developing and abiding by the rules of a competitive system, and do not permit exceptions based on an apparently good performance which might have been better (or, indeed worse) under more competitive conditions. 156

Summary of Factors Bearing on Identification of Workable. 

Competition. L 

If the level of profits and the progressiveness of market. performance at any moment of time are not in themselves evidence of the presence or absence of "workable competition", what aspects of the market situation can guide the courts in determining whether or not they confront a situation of effective monopoly, or lesser situations of competitive restraint defined by the antitrust laws? The short-hand legal definition of monopoly-- "power over price or the power to exclude competitors"--focusses directly on the ultimate and closely related economic elements of the problem. The economic definition of workable competition-a relatively flat demand curve for the individual firm, especially in the moderately long-run--concentrates on the seller's power over his own price. But that power cannot normally be retained for long without natural or imposed limitations on the entry or growth or rivals. Restriction on the entry of rival firms is an integral part of the economic as it is of the legal definition of monopoly power, for competitive results can hardly be rexpected in a market where entry is not free. One may sum up the major economic aspects of workable competition with reference to the factors listed below. Of these factors, the first three are the most general, normally constituting the key strategic factors distinguishing situations of workable competition from those of effective monopoly. But all these factors are elements of market situations which bear directly on the presence or absence of effective competition, in the sense in which that term is used in this Report, 1.e., a seller's power over his own price. 157

(1) A sufficient number of effectively competitive sellers: the issue of relative size.

For effective competition to exist, there should be a sufficient number of independent firms to give assurance that no one firm will have alone a considerable degree of monopoly power, as defined above. 

The number of firms is large or small relative to a particular market: twenty large sellers in a national market have far less market power than one or two small sellers in a well-defined local market. From this standpoint, economists would regard as the most desirable condition the largest number of units compatible with efficient size. 

Short of this condition, in order to be "sufficient", the number of sellers should be large enough to give those who deal with them a considerable number and range of available alternatives, and to afford fair assurance that informal understandings and agreements will be so unstable as to be ineffectual. Unless numbers are already large in a given market, a reduction of numbers presumptively involves some lessening of competition, although not necessarily a lessening to the point of "effective" monopoly. Where genuine economies of scale, or other considerations of efficiency (including the capacity V to innovate), permit only very small numbers of sellers, added vigilance is indicated as to other requisites of effective competition. 

The presence or absence of workable competition is affected not only by the number of sellers, but by their relative size and strength, which has a bearing on whether a sufficient number of them are able to 158

d nimwih avent delu w begin a na pestor ticqonon: To

Wissen & 5 

MILANO 

tes et per 63% ap in low to amends go ahajan 20 28 be effec close ed that mo st the of beal Affers sarily charac to 1 30

be effective competitors. This condition implies not necessarily close equality between the various firms, but at least it implies that most of them should not be so weak or inefficient as to exist at the sufferance of a few strong firms. Thus one of the conditions of healthy rivalry is that there should not be too great a degree of difference between firms in competitive strength, which does not necessarily depend upon size, but upon costs and efficiency. The presence of any units in a market very much larger than the others is a factor to be examined closely for its bearing on the workably competitive character of the market.

The number and relative strength of firms necessary to effectively competitive behavior cannot be compressed into a formula: the answer to the question in any given market depends also on the factors discussed below, so that it would be quite possible to conclude that a given group of, say, twelve firms would be compatible with the presence of effective competition in one industry, but not in another. The interrelation and relative importance in different situations of the various factors bearing on the presence or absence of effective competition have not yet been fully isolated and measured by economics. 

But economists are almost universally agreed that if an industry has only a few firms, or a few firms of overwhelming size relative to the remainder, special study would be needed to determine whether the industry is workably competitive. Where firms are few, each producing a significant share of market supply, each seller knows that by alter-¨ ing output, he and his rivals can change market supply, and thus price; 159

and each seller knows also that changes in his own price policy are likely to be followed by his rivals. The few firms cannot disregard the effects of their own policies on each other, and on the market as a whole. They soon realize that many types of competitive moves, such as open price reductions, especially on homogeneous or closely similar products, will be promptly countered, so that a company has little or nothing to gain from such tactics. Competition of this kind being futile, the resulting pattern of market behavior may be one of avoiding price competition and concentrating on product differentiation, salesmanship, advertising and other cost-increasing means of gaining ground in the market. Product differentiation may take the form of genuine product improvement, or of minor alterations in appearance, packaging, and the like. Whether the level of prices, costs and output resulting from such behavior would be more or less favorable to society than that of complete monopoly in a given case would depend on many factors, and cannot be stated as an invariable rule. Where the number of firms is sufficiently small, however, and where the industry is sufficiently insulated from competitive pressures, even in the absence of overt collusion, the market itself is unlikely to show very many of the characteristics of effective competition. 

(2) Freedom of Entry. 

The cost of entry into the competitive area should not be impracticably high. This does not imply an absolute criterion for ease of entry in terms of a given number of dollars. Nor does it deny recognition to the fact that as a practical matter geographical and other 160

ON SC

factors may make the entry of new firms into even a competitive industry a relatively slow process, But it does mean that under prevailing conditions as to the availability of capital, an attempt by existing firms to raise prices considerably above the competitive norm would make it profitable and practicable for new firms or existing borderline firms to invade the field, In economic terms, this means that conditions of cost for a new firm should not be notably higher, at least after a reasonable period of initial development, than conditions of cost for an existing member of the industry. In many cases, of course, the new firm may start with the cost advantage of being able to use the most advanced available techniques. The importance of entry as a condition distinguishing effective competition from effective monopoly, and the cost barriers to entry in many industries, may justify public policies for aiding small business units to be more efficient, although such policies are not part of antitrust policy proper. From this standpoint, freedom of entry needs to be a feasible reality. 

Freedom of outsiders with requisite skill to enter the market may appear dispensable, for if there are many companies, and they compete vigorously, what purpose would be served by additional numbers? If the number of companies in the industry is strictly limited (say, by license), however, the industry may not produce sufficient output to meet social standards. And if energetic and imaginative rivals cannot enter, the boldest and most rewarding innovations may be excluded. New firms entering an industry may not all survive. Some may be weeded out in the competitive struggle, sometimes indeed after making their contribution 161

either to pricing or to business methods. But freedom of entry is needed if competition is to maintain a sufficient number of sellers in a market, and to prevent markets from evolving gradually into situations of oligopoly or monopoly. From the economic point of view, the freedom of new rivals is, indeed, the most fundamental requisite for effective competition in the long run. Without this condition, it is idle to expect competition. The entry and withdrawal of firms is the basic mechanism of the market for achieving its economic results. 

The exclusion of new rivals is a major impairment of competition in itself, and the power to exclude rivals is usually associated with the power to eliminate rivalry between those already in the industry. (3) Independence of rivals. 

A primary condition of workable competition is that there be genuine independence on the part of the business units in an industry, so that each firm pursues its own individual advantage and not the advantage of the industry as a whole. In industries with many sellers, concerted action is difficult to achieve without relatively visible machinery of cooperation. While most of the day-to-day activities of modern trade associations are neutral in their effects on competition, and some may contribute to making competition more effective, the question often arises as to whether their programs permit or lead to collusion on price or output, either directly or through the exchange of certain types of information. The chief problem is to distinguish those arrangements for the interchange of cost or other trade information which are likely to restrain, or to be used to restrain competitive 162

policies which would otherwise have been to the advantage of the individual firm.

Where there are only a few sellers, however, concerted action can be subtle and informal, and often difficult to detect. (The problem of so-called "conscious parallel action" is an aspect of this problem, and will be discussed separately below.) In such industries, it is normal for sellers to try to take the reactions of rivals into account in determining their own competitive policies. Fewness of sellers does not necessarily lead to mutual interdependence of policies, but it may well do so. In addition, it permits more subtle forms of collusion than are possible where sellers are more numerous. Where collusion exists, therefore, it may be more difficult to detect where firms are few than where they are many.

These first three criteria of workable competition are the most central in their significance and scope. They delineate the problem in its most general terms. The issues they raise, however, often require evaluation in relation to other forces. The aspects of the concept of workable competition discussed below are of importance in many situations, and may be useful especially in studying uncertain cases. 

(4) Rate of Growth of the Industry. 

The rate of growth of an industry can, for example, strongly color the significance to competition in the industry of the number and relative size of the firms, and alter the effectiveness of barriers to entry. The expected rate of growth of the industry affects the attitudes and expectations of firms in the industry, the attractiveness 163
