of the industry to outside firms, and the possibility of maintaining positions of market power without severe restrictions on entry. In a new and rapidly expanding industry, characterized by a high rate of growth, the opening of new markets and the high rate of technical progress usually characteristic of such situations make for uncertainty as to the most profitable policies. The entrance of new firms, if it takes place, may lead to further unsettlement of industry policies and under these circumstances, firms may grow without imposing losses on their rivals. Insofar as a few firms find it possible to dominate a stable industry, they can hold their position more readily than the same number of firms in an industry that is open to rapid expansion. 

These relationships are sometimes reciprocal. If a few firms have a tight hold in an industry and are not disposed to engage in competitive activity, they may, by their passivity, not only discourage the entry of rivals, but of new techniques which might, in turn, permit the industry to reduce costs and to expand. (5) Predatory practices.

There should be no predatory or preclusive tactics, such that their natural effect would be to enable the user to eliminate rivals without regard to their efficiency, or at least to place them under serious handicaps irrelevant to their efficiency. It should be noted as a practical matter that predatory or unfair competition in this sense can only be waged where a considerable degree of market power already exists. Thus the presence of such conduct is properly regarded as evidence symptomatic of monopoly, although it is not necessary for 164

Conversely, the accusation of "predatory", "cutthroat" or "unfair" practices often turns out on examination not to stem from the abuse of significant degrees of market power, but from the uncomfortably active pressures of competition itself. (6) Time-lags in Responding to Competitive Moves.

In general, effective competition (outside the area of organized markets, such as exchanges, and markets of comparable character) seems to hinge on a condition such that the intitiator of a competitive move can expect to gain in volume of business for a while, even though he may not gain a permanent differential advantage from any one such move, since rival imitators will seek to cancel his advantage in one way or another. Or, to put it in the terms already used, in an effectively competitive market the customers' response to an inducement offered by a competitor is quicker than his rivals' responses which attempt to neutralize the superiority of the inducement he has offered. This aspect of effective competition is implicit in points (1) and (2) above regarding the appropriate number and relative size of sellers and freedom of entry. The magnitude of the time-intervals involved may differ greatly according to the nature of the industry or trade, and of the competitive move in question. But the essence of the competitive market's incentive to innovation, to price changes, or other directly competitive moves is typically an interval which an innovator may reasonably expect to have, under effectively competitive conditions, 165

during J 2 be met especie that ar by his 

Are fem

Innova which

INCAL 

fact .4

during which he may hope to gain an advantage because his moves cannot be met and offset promptly by his rivals. Where there are few sellers, especially where a small number are of dominant size, each may believe that any competitive move on his part will be neutralized so promptly by his rivals as to make the move not worth making. Thus where there are few sellers in a market, imitation of the competitive moves of an innovator is likely to be more prompt than in a market of many, a factor which often tends to discourage innovation by reducing the advantages which may be expected from it, although it also means the prompt spread of innovations when they are undertaken. It is for this reason that uncertainty as to the pattern of rival responses is such an important factor in the process of market rivalry where sellers are few. Where uncertainty prevails--perhaps because of the presence in the market of a strong maverick company which refuses to follow prevailing patterns-sellers may be more willing to conclude that they have a chance to make a gain through some competitive innovation, than if the system of market responses has been securely built up by past habits, agreements, or experience. Thus the absence of competitive conditions, or well-grounded convictions as to the market position and responses of rivals, may fail to put sellers under the pressure of market stimuli--the hope of gain, and the fear of loss in the event that others move-- which could give them sufficient incentives to innovate or make other competitive moves. 

One circumstance that favors this time-interval for gain through innovation in an effectively competitive market is the fact that the ✓ initiator of a competitive move may gain business at the expense of all 166

This is a further reason why a number of competitors and ease of entry are important conditions of effective competition. 

(7) Product Differentiation and Product Homogeneity. 

Thus

The definition of the word "market", and that of workable competition itself, both turn on the actual and direct competition a seller confronts from the closely related products of others. An important factor in determining the boundaries of the market is the knowledge of buyers as to the alternatives open to them. If other conditions are equal, it would seem for this reason to follow that the more homogeneous the product of rival sellers, the more easily buyers could switch from the output of one competitor to that of others; and therefore the wider the market, and the greater the degree of competition in it. standardized classification and quality-grading systems in many industries, serve to permit buyers a greater range of alternative choices than were previously available, and to increase the number of sellers in direct rivalry with each other. Other conditions are not always equal, however. 

The apparent conclusion of the preceding sentences may turn out to be illusory in many situations. Systems of standardized classification do not as such necessarily affect the commodity structure, and therefore the boundaries of the market, although they may do so in several ways, both favorable and unfavorable to more effective competition. Such systems may give the buyer a better identified assortment of alterna- 1 Y སད་༩\r 

A 20 167

tives the reduce t sellers

Classifi the stam Low: so that before 

Lead to zare s also t breadt mact

tives than he had before. In some cases, however, they could also reduce the number of possible sources from which he could buy, if sellers altered their production policy to specialize in certain classifications or grades of the product. This would be the case if the standardized classification system split the market into segments, so that within one grade, there were fewer suppliers competing than before. Under other circumstances, a classification system could lead to there being fewer differentiated products in the market, and more sellers of each. Classification and standardization plans may also tend generally to discourage innovation that amoujnted to a breach in the lines of a rigid system of product grading. And such practices could be used as devices to cloak programs for market division or even the exclusion of rivals. 

Where sellers are relatively few, however, product differentiation within the same market may have different effects. Extreme product differentiation, by tending to insulate the demand for one product against that for rival products, may allow real positions of monopoly to develop. Relatively mild differentiation of products within a market otherwise effectively competitive, however, may be a factor favorable to the intensiveness of competition, including price competition and competition in quality. This will tend to be most forcibly the case if the product differentiation reflects product rivalry, that is, product improvement, rather than mere heterogeneity of closely similar products. For product differentiation, especially if it constitutes or embodies a genuine innovation, may be a means whereby 168

the seller can take advantage of the time interval the market allows within which he can expect to gain from a competitive move. If the number of sellers and their relative position are such as to justify uncertainty as to the speed and completeness with which rivals will counter the initial move, such a move, in the form of product differentiation, may contribute to the competitiveness of market behavior. 

This would not be the case if the innovation is fortified by obstacles to imitation by rival producers, or obstacles to the sale of cheaper and simpler models. Thus the impact of product differentiation on the effectiveness of competition will have to be judged in each case in its market setting, in relation to more central indicia of effective competition. It is not, as such, evidence either way. 

The fact that product homogeneity is the rule in a given market may be a significant element in determining the kind of market structure needed for effective competition. Where the number of sellers is large, product homogeneity increases the zone of competition. 

The active and effective competition on organized exchanges and like markets depends upon product homogeneity within grades. On the other hand, if the number of sellers is small, product homogeneity tends to reduce any one seller's chance to gain through a competitive move. An open reduction of price is almost certain to be met instantly. The result is that the initiator of the competitive move has little or no chance to gain in volume of business at his rival's expense--although they may jointly gain by discouraging sales by other rivals segregated either by distance or by product differences. Thus it may be that in a market for a homo- 169

geneous product, effective competition may require a larger number of sellers than would be necessary when the product sold is quality-differentiated to a point permitting significant uncertainty about what price-differential is called for to meet a rival's offer. Of course, in many instances, stable or accepted quality relations among somewhat differentiated products in a market may come to develop in such ways as also to prevent the appearance of genuine uncertainty about the responses of rivals to a competitive move, especially to a price reduction. In such situations, there would be little difference between the conditions of effective competition in the market for a homogeneous and a non-homogeneous product. In effect, the existing pattern of price differentials for product differences, if stable for a time, becomes one of effective product homogeneity for this purpose. If the number of sellers in the market for a homogeneous product, though limited, is large enough to permit effective price competition to prevail at all, such competition is likely to depend on the secret shading of list prices, or variations in other terms of the bargain. In such an industry the initiator of a competitive price reduction may need secrecy if he is to make any competitive gains from the step; but the secrecy would have to be short-lived if it were to eventuate in effective competition. Secrecy, however, virtually implies discrimination, with the result that in cases where secrecy is necessary to competitive incentives, it involves a choice of market imperfections, and an opportunity for buyers who have bargaining power to gain undesirable advantages over their rivals. This disadvantage is mitigated or minimized 170

in proportion as the secrecy, and the discrimination, are shortlived. 

But a stream of short-life discriminatory advantages to a few buyers may have the same preclusive effect as a small but significant number of permanent discriminatory advantages, and would be evidence of the ineffectiveness of competition. 

(8) Meeting or Matching the Prices of Rivals 

The above analysis of the varying effects which product homogeneity and differentiation may have on competition in different market settings has a bearing also on the vexed question of meeting or matching the prices of competitors. This problem arises most acutely where the number of sellers is small enough to raise a serious question about the potential effectiveness of competition, and the sellers act, from habit, from self-interest, or through a plan, in interdependent ways, which may minimize competitive moves, by reducing or eliminating the possibility that they can bring a seller any advantage. It is of the essence of effective competition that competitors should try to meet, or offer an equivalent for, any superior inducement which one of them offers. 

Meeting a rival's inducements is the means whereby competition diffuses the gains of productive efficiency. But, as we have seen, the process of meeting a competitor's moves may preclude the possibility of "effective competition" if it is too prompt and too precise, as it is likely to be in the case of a homogeneous product, sold by a limited number of sellers. Even here, however, merely to forbid a seller to meet his rival's price would involve a reductio ad absurdum, so long as the market structure itself is untouched. If A only part-way meets B's کہا 171

Under these circumstances, in the absence of a change in demand, there is no place where competitive price can level off, and no adjustment permitting a number of competitors to remain in the market in question. This is the trouble with holding that a firm is not competing unless it is exceeding its rivals' offers. 

There are two possible ways in which effective competition could develop in such situations, depending upon circumstances: (1) a change in the structure of the market, by increasing the number of sellers; or (2) where the number of sellers is not unduly limited in the first instance, more workable competition might emerge if nothing is done to prevent a good deal of price discrimination. This course, naturally, has its dangers, and this comment should not be interpreted as a general recommendation of such a policy. It is meant merely to point out the available alternatives in situations of this kind. Perhaps something could be accomplished by not preventing--and certainly by forbidding private groups the power to prevent--a reasonable variety and variability in pricing practices. Thus in cases like Sugar Institute, supra, the

(9) Excess Capacity, "Excess capacity" is a term difficult to define satisfactorily, and even more difficult to identify. The term is commonly used to describe capacity unused during a general depression, as well as to temporary "excesses" of capacity which may be generated by investment booms in competitive industries. Both these senses should be distin guished from the excess of capacity confronted by a declining industry. 

In a period of generally good business, for a growing or stable business, the existence of unused capacity, which could be utilized at or near prevailing costs, may help to demonstrate the presence of either effective monopoly or effective competition in connection with other facts. If the industry pursues policies of making more money by charging high prices and restricting production, it may have chronic excess capacity as a result. The practice of purchasing and dismantling unused capacity in this sense--that is, capacity which could be utilized at normal costs--has always and rightly been considered strong evidence of monopoly power and monopolistic intent, even when such programs are dignified in the name of "rationalization." On the other hand, a moderate and varying amount of excess capacity naturally tends to develop from time to time as a result of expansion in a competitive industry, And its presence is favorable to the effectiveness of competition, if other criteria of competition are present. It permits pro- 173

And, as business approaches conditions in which efficient capacity is fully utilized at high profit, a failure on the part of the industry to expand in response to high levels of demend and profit would usually suggest the presence of some restrictive arrangement to prevent the normal response of a competitive market. (10) Price Discrimination

From the economic point of view, a discrimination in price is the sale of the same product or service to different buuers at approximately the same time, but at different prices. In defining a price discrimination, the products sold need not be identical, but they should be closely similar.300 A price differential between buyers 

300/ Sometimes goods which are physically the same are sold under different brand names, or minor differences in the product are made the basis of significant grade and price differences. Where consumers do not know the relevant facts, such differences may permit the continuation of extensive price differentials, especially if one of the brand names or grades commands consumer support. In one sense, products which consumers think are different are not the same economically, even if they are physically identical. This is not, however, to suggest that in administering the Robinson-Patman Act, the Courts and the Federal Trade Commission can ignore the physical identity, or close physical similarity, of goods sold at different prices. Determining whether a price discrimination exists is a question of fact, and whether the goods sold at different prices are substantially alike should be determined in the light of all elements of the problem, including both their physical character and the way they are labelled. Among the factors relevant to such a determination are: whether the goods can be sold under one label at a higher price than another because of consumer preferences created by advertising, or other selling costs; whether the differences in the product, though 174

is not

and rec ing amo or diff 

To cons the di

IT ser selle

is not necessarily a discrimination in price, if the two buyers seek and receive different grades or qualities of the commodity, or differing amounts of related services, such as credit extensions, deliveries or differences in packaging, storage, risk of default, and the like. 

To constitute a discrimination, the similar goods and services sold at different prices should be produced and provided at the same cost: the discrimination in price, that is to say, consists in selling goods or services which cost the same at different prices. For this purpose, the relevant differences in cost are differences in average costs: the seller can hardly claim that any one buyer is the marginal buyer, to be favored with a lower price because the transaction with him should not bear the burden of the firm's overhead. And such cost differences as are relevant are those consistently characteristic of the categories of business being compared, not transitory or incidental differences, such as the fact that one order is produced on overtime, or delivered under bad weather conditions. On the other hand, quantity sales, or sales to customers who take large volumes over time, may permit cost distinctions: if marginal costs are less than average costs, such sales would reduce average costs for all units. That effect of the additional sales on costs would be irrelevant, however, for the purpose of deter- 300/ (cont.) minor, are significant in terms of consumer preferences, etc. To conclude that sales of similar products at different prices may be discriminatory from the economic point of view does not, of course, imply any conclusion as to their legality.

On the other hand, such sales may also involve lower costs in salesmanship, handling, packaging, delivery and advertising, which would be relevant in justifying an apparent discrimination. Similarly, sales to customers who perform their own storage or delivery functions clearly involve lower costs than sales to customers for whom these services must be provided by the seller. 

In such cases the buyers are in effect seeking and buying different combinations of goods and services: one buyer,who provides his own storage, is buying only the commodity, whereas another may be buying both the product and storage service. Under these circumstances it would be discriminatory to charge the buyers the same price, since the first buyer would be paying more for the commodity itself than the second. 

In a purely competitive market, no seller could or would discriminate in price. For in such a market, by definition, he could sell all his output at prevailing market prices. The departure from conditions of pure and perfect competition need not be very substantial in order to permit some discrimination in price to take place. The impetus to discrimination may arise as a result of considerable imperfections of competition on the buyers' side of the market; but sellers would have no interest in yielding to such pressures by discriminating in price if they could market their output at the prevailing price. Thus even the largest buyers pay the market price for wheat, cotton, and other products sold under such highly competitive conditions. Under such circumstances, the seller would have no interest in selling any 176

The presence of price discrimination is therefore evidence that to a greater or lesser degree the seller is functioning in a market which is not purely or perfectly competitive. The presence of some element in the market which causes or permits a departure from perfect competition does not of itself prove anything of legal significance, nor does it, as such, define a problem in public policy. A market sufficiently imperfect to permit occasional, transitory or hap- ✓ hazard price discrimination may still be "effectively competitive" by the standards suggested here. 

An especially important case of discrimination is that of a firm with considerable market power, which may have strong motives to discriminate in price. Dealing with segregated markets (a tariff protected home-market and a more competitive foreign market, for example), he may be able to add to his total profits by "dumping" at lower prices abroad without threatening higher prices and profit margins at home. 

The seller with such a market position may be able to gain the same type of advantage in dealing with functionally distinct markets at home, if the product cannot flow from one market or customer to others after being sold. By price discrimination, such a seller may be able to adapt himself to the different market demands for his product more profitably than if he pursued a one-price policy to all buyers.

Competition among spatially separated producers, where transportation cost is a substantial item, often presents such problems in discrimination. The issue has been much discussed in relation to basing-point or other systems of delivered price quotation. These problems have two aspects, which are closely related: How prices are determined in each separate geographical market; and whether, and in what sense, sellers are discriminating in price by selling in more than one such market, at prices which yield them different returns from each market. In such situations, it may safely be assumed that the markets are sufficiently distant, and transportation costs sufficiently high, that there would be no arbitraging between markets on the part of buyers, except for price differentials greater than the cost of transportation. Under conditions of pure competition, no seller would ship to two such markets if his return were different from each; wheat or cotton farmers may sell through different markets, but only if the prices they expect to receive, net of transport tòsts, are the same from each market. If this were not so, the product would move towards the higher-priced market until the producers' net receipts for sales in each market were equalized. Therefore, the fact that the seller will accept different returns from two markets marks the presence of a market imperfection, which in each case may or may not be significant from the point of view of analysis and policy. 179

Where sellers "absorb" freight to sell in distant market areas at net receipts below those earned on sales nearer home, the seller engages in the practice because he believes that it will pay him to He calculates that he can secure added sales, at some return above out-of-pocket costs, without reducing his net receipts from his previous volume of sales in other spatially distinct market areas, where his market position is stronger. Such a calculation on the seller's part has two principal elements: his estimate of the limits of demand (either seasonal or absolute), at the prices he believes most advantageous, in the "home" market areas where he has been selling in the past; and his estimate of the risk that rivals will penetrate his home markets by "absorbing" freight, either on their own initiative, or by way of reprisal for his penetration of their "home" markets. The risks of such reprisals and the losses for all parties to which they could lead, are among the factors leading to the development of basing-point and similar systems of price quotation. 

One of the principal aspects of many such systems is that they serve as a means for limiting or eliminating price competition, by establishing predictable patterns of price leadership and price formation, through which concerted action to restrict price competition take place.

Thus the presence of price discrimination is an important fact to consider in examining the forces influencing price formation in the market. For when discriminations in price are sustained and substantial, they may have far reaching effects at several levels of competition, and measure or lead to significant departures from standards of workable competition.

Price discrimination is not only a symptom of some imperfection in the market, more or less significant in evaluating the competitiveness of the market; it may also become a means of making existing

Such discriminations may injure or prevent effective competition between them, and perhaps even promote tendencies to monopoly. 

On the other hand, price discrimination may also be the force, through private negotiation, which can weaken or disrupt an otherwise effective one-price system. Ragged, ineffective and uncertain oligopoly pricing would obviously be preferable in many cases to the perfectly disciplined variety. Its result may be lower average prices and higher output than would otherwise prevail. Where a small enough group of sellers functions as the dominant market influence in an industry, therefore, a rigid and undiscriminating policy against price discrimination could lead to closely policed and concerted pricing programs obviously illegal under Section 1. 

In the Sugar Institute 306/ case, for example, it was apparent that price discrimination was the alternative to a price program of higher and more rigid prices, and of better controlled monopoly, so far as the sellers of sugar were concerned; their trade association was rightly denied the power to require its members to adhere to announced prices. On the other hand, conventional functional discounts, while often discriminatory, in that they may not correspond to cost differences in dealing with different classes of customers, ! have less impact on competition, unless they collide with the challenge of integrated distribution systems, combining the retail and wholesale function. In such situations, attempts to prevent discriminations in price may work against development of more efficient modes of distribution--unless the courts are willing to consider all the relevant cost savings which may be involved in dealing with different classes of customers.

Thus price discrimination offers many difficult problems both for the policy maker and for the analyst. The capacity to employ serious and persistent price discrimination as a trade policy may, and often does, derive from the ineffectiveness of prevailing competition. And price discrimination is always a fact of importance, to be considered in relation to other facts, in determining whether the market is or is not workably competitive.

In judging the effectiveness of competition, various further, and more specialized, applications of the foregoing analysis are required in dealing with certain problems. One such problem is V that of vertical integration. One one hand, this may be a means of extending a monopoly position from one stage of production to another, undermining competition along the way. On the other, it may be a form of defensive competition, where a producer employs 183

his al vradná „cititegate was tungel angi, dení tion ness goo cri of ca 08

his own power to produce as a protection against monopoly exploitation by others. The impact of vertical integration on the effectiveness of competition in the markets affected will therefore vary a good deal, depending on other factors in the situation. L These examples illustrate the relation between the crucial criteria of effective competition--numbers, entry and independence of sellers--and their application to the analysis of particular cases. Many cases are easily classified. But the complexity of actual departures from ideal competition makes it impossible to draw a clean line between the degrees of monopoly power which do and do not carry with them the potentiality of "undue" harm. Where a

Judgment as to the relative significance of competitive and monopolistic elements in a market situation requires a separate examination of several variables, each of which will probably have a different weight in each case, as well as an over-all judgment as to their effect in combination, it is obvious that the economist can never expect to tell a court that a market of three sellers is effective monopolistic, but one of four is workably competitive. This is not to say that competent and disinterested economists cannot agree on many cases. 

An industry of well-known technique; where three or four firms share 75% of capacity in a stable relationship; where the remaining firms constitute a satellite fringe without strong competitive potential; where prices are formed in a pattern of prompt and precise price leadership, involving a basing point or comparable system of predictable pricing; and where new entries are rare; would not be 184

dof

classified as effectively competitive by most economists, unless the perhaps there was strong overriding evidence of competition, for example, progressive betterment of product accompanied by lowering of prices. Even in such a case, however, the reasons for the rarity of entries would have to be examined further. 

The example, like any other hypothetical, merely underlines the fact that assessment of the workability of competition is complex. If any element of the example were shifted, the conclusion might vary also. This means, of course, that it is necessary to consider the features of each case in realistic detail, in order to determine whether the monopoly or the competitive elements of market behavior are the more important. 

3. Workable Competition Contrasted with Pure or Perfect 

Competition. "Workable" or "effective" competition supplies no formula which can substitute for judgment. It suggests leads to data of significance, and a means of organizing the data bearing on the question whether a given market of itself is sufficiently competitive in its structure and behavior to be classified as workably competitive. And it provides some bench-marks or criteria, representing somewhat different points of vantage, for the process of making that judgment. 

The criteria it offers are not, of course, the criteria of pure or perfect competition. For the benefit of noneconomists, it may be useful to clarify the role of these concepts in economics, and to distinguish them from the concepts used here. 185

OPTOUT CABANE 

theo many or a len de a dong m the

AMA 4. As Ou fo Intr

The concepts of pure and perfect competition are tools of theoretical analysis of great utility to economic science. Like many useful scientific concepts--the concept of homogeneous fluids or a rigid body, for example, or the hypothesis used in predicting the weather that barometric pressure tends everywhere to equality-of pure and perfect competition are not intended to be immediately applicable to reality, or to constitute a description of reality. 

As a scientific model, these ideas give economists means for rigorously exploring the interrelationships of certain specified market forces. And they define rigidly the conditions necessary to a form of static long-run equilibrium in which prices would equal costs, including the minimum economically necessary supply price of capital. 

Historically, this concept developed first as a series of statements about the cost-price relationships which competitive market pressures tended to produce and later, as the cost-price results of competition were more exactly defined, economics produced a far more precise formulation of the conditions necessary to those results. 

These conditions are: (1) that the products of all the rival sellers in the market are precise substitutes for each other, and that all buyers and sellers have perfect knowledge of the market and complete indifference as to their customers or sources of supply; (2) that the number of sellers be so large that no one seller produces more than a négligible share of market supply; and (3) that new firms can enter the industry with the same costs as existing firms. As theoretical models, these concepts have facilitated analysis, and 186

thia no signes the that fe bey

the study of real situations, by directing attention to key forces which lead actual markets to deviate in their results from those that would emerge under perfectly competitive conditions. 

The concepts of pure and perfect competition have also clarified and helped to make more precise the results which economists have long concluded should flow from competition. They thus served to define, in theoretical terms, ideal or optimum conditions in the utilization of resources and the distribution of income. It should be emphasized that these are theoretical standards, in that they are not intended to be immediate and direct guides to policy. 

The concepts of pure and perfect competition are not by any means the sole basis for economists' recommendations as to anti-monopoly policy in specific situations. They define cost-price relations under conditions of equilibrium towards which certain markets tend to move, although markets, like pendulums, can never be expected to reach equilibrium. Thus they provide standards for comparison with the equilibria towards which other markets are driven by competitive forces. When taken out of context, the very precision of the theoretical standards of pure and perfect competition can be misleading. Nonetheless, these concepts, used in connection. with the study of other factors outside their terms, have helped to orient economists' studies of actual situations, and have contributed, along with other influences, to the elaboration of the . theory of workable competition, as an instrument for the direct study of market conditions. 187

1. and two

COL 

COM to

The theory of workable competition differs from that of pure and perfect competition in several ways. In the first place, the two theories have different purposes. The theory of pure and perfect competition is an instrument of theoretical analysis; the theory of workable competition seeks a method for making necessarily less exact judgments in studies of actual market situations. Secondly, to the extent that the two theories are concerned with the same elements--the definition of the product and market, the number and relative size of sellers, and conditions of entry--the concept of workable competition posits a lesser degree of "perfection". Thus perfect competition would require an extremely large number of sellers--a condition that could be met only in some agricultural and textile industries, bituminous coal mining, etc. Criteria of workable competition could be satisfied by a number of sellers, some of whom might well produce significant fractions of total supply. Pure and perfect competition would require that all sellers produce goods which were ready substitutes for each other, and that no buyers have preferences among sellers. The concept of workable competition may be consistent with far less complete homogeneity of the product. As to entry, too, perfect competition contemplates complete freedom of entry by new firms, whereas workable competition is compatible with practical but not serious barriers to entry, such as considerable capital and even advertising requirements, and a period of higher costs while production is organized and management trained and shaken down. Thirdly, apart 188

Joolzog fry ATUL from thes Forkable certain 

Labod to ertain te lite compe op wenpepje nominates compy p dty.Jastukweo ed ye pspol ​al Boldewborn ditu od ten niitd 28 Boltbpa fun pality Cof to ac 2 Foble ​at i 红花 ​寫 ​E 281

from these factors in market analysis which the two theories share, workable competition goes beyond the theory of perfect competition in certain respects. While the theory of perfect competition examines certain implications of the seller's quest for profit in great detail, the literature of workable competition seeks to identify those aspects of competition which provide market incentives for innovation, including quality innovation as well as cost reduction, and for taking directly into account other elements of the market situation which change through time. It is of importance, therefore, in using economic analysis of problems of market organization and behavior for antitrust purposes, that in each case the premises of analysis be made clear, and that economic theories not be applied beyond the limits of their own propositional base. Thus it does not necessarily follow that "elements of monopoly" or "market imperfections" which imply merely the absence of some of the conditions of pure or perfect competition are for that reason alone monopoly situations in the antitrust sense, or even that they necessarily lessen the effectiveness of competition. 

4. Comparison of Legal and Economic Concepts of Competition and Monopoly.

From this it seems clear that there are striking similarities between the legal and economic conceptions of monopoly and competition, and some striking dissimilarities as well. Chief common ground lies where legality hinges on economic concepts such as monopoly or substantial lessening of competition, or on judgment as to the economic meaning of practices classed as unreasonable per se. 189

abendua ', '1, minist 43 rabey ove

tile cmter heory nd mon 2 perf So 973

Some of the apparent differences arise from the particular language of statutes, and from the fact that, especially in the administration of a criminal statute, the law must give weight to concepts of intent, and purposefulness of action, which have no counterpart in economic analysis.

Thus it is natural that economic theory has given little attention to the distinction between monopoly and monopolization, which has preoccupied the law. 

The law is concerned with conduct deemed harmful, economics with conditions, including "imperfections" of competition, which may or may not have resulted from anyone's actions. Again, the law cannot, save in the most obvious cases, assume that actual behavior in the market-place corresponds to the pattern of competitive behavior that would normally be expected in a market of a given structure. The economic analysis of market theory, while it suggests important leads to relevant evidence, does not and cannot relieve the law of the necessity for searching out the evidence as to the kind of competitive behavior that actually occurs in a given instance. The law can learn from generalized studies of normal behavior, but it must always be on the alert for the exceptional case as well. The antitrust laws establish criminal guilt or civil responsibility; here as in all other phases of the law, legal responsibility is individual. 

The problem of "conscious parallelism" is a case in point. This v term has gained wide currency as identifying the kind of pricing practices which develop in industries dominated by a few sellers and buyers. 

It is a misleading phrase. A seller must meet his rivals' prices in any kind of a market, however competitive or monopolistic, and this 1,90

eting 

Brepe 70st. OPAJONE 

testion Minstit. e sell d sell is cost Large | tside espons Heir anda

meeting of prices is of course always a "conscious" act. The economic question is whether the price and the meeting of price result from competitive or monopolistic forces. In a perfectly competitive market, the seller meets a market price, and must determine what he will produce and sell, how much and in what forms, in terms of the relation between his costs and market price. Where sellers are relatively few, with large shares of the market, they begin to confront the necessity of considering the effects of their own policies, and their rivals' responses to those policies, on market supply and price. In short, their business policies begin to approach a condition of interdependence where market structure and organization come close to the boundary line of workable competition. Where the number of sellers is small enough, and other factors working against effective competition are present, the economists examine the consequences of such interdependence, finding that in certain conditions such interdependent business policies may lead to levels of price or output higher than they might have been if the number of sellers were larger: Where such situations can be identified, the economist would conclude that levels of price or output were effectively monopolistic, or more broadly, that the enumerated conditions tended to lessen or balk competitive incentives to price reductions. From the economic point of view, the issue here would be whether the sellers' price policies were independent or interdependent.

The legal problem, by contrast, would be to find whether there were combination or conspiracy within the meaning of the Sherman 191

Act. Whether economic interdependence constitutes combination or conspiracy, however, is primarily a legal question, not an economic one. The legal problem, then, is to determine the conditions under which different kinds of evidence of interdependent behavior would be considered proof of combination or conspiracy. This remains true, regardless of the quantum or character of the economic evidence. the one hand, for example, there may be no more evidence of interdependence than the fewness of the sellers and the relative immunity of the products that they sell from competition by other products. At the other extreme, there may be explicit agreements to fix prices, divide markets, or exclude competitors. In between, there may be any number of combinations of price, product, entry, and innovation behavior and effects. In all such cases, the legal question of what constitutes proof of combination or conspiracy becomes paramount for antitrust purposes.

The legal answer to that question is often facilitated, of course, by the fact that sellers in markets where they are few may take steps to make the pattern of their non-competitive behavior more clear-cut. They will naturally understand the lessening of competitive pressures which would result from foreknowledge of their rivals' responses, and from the prompt meeting of prices. They may, therefore, take deliberate concerted action to make the system of price announcements more predictable and the meeting of prices more prompt and precise than they would have been if their actions were purely independent, or even interdependent without some further steps in 192

concert. Such arrangements may not in themselves amount to "agreement" on what prices should be charged, but might serve either to implement such agreements if made, or to obviate the need for them. 

On the question of "bigness", economists would agree that absolute size, the number of employees, or dollars of assets, etc., has no significance for monopoly or competition. But relative size, i.e., share of the market, has great significance indeed. And few economists would concur in occasional judicial dicta that an industry with two or three firms behaves a good deal like an industry with ten or more in a given market.

One of the major interests of economics as a study is to reach conclusions about the respective social performance of different industries, and to draw conclusions as to whether a given market, whether competitive or monopolistic, is doing its social job well or not. The antitrust laws, however, are concerned with undue restraints of competition, with monopolization, and with specific practices deemed especially dangerous to competition. Whether conduct violates these antitrust standards, as undue restraints of competition, cannot, and has never turned on whether it also represented "progressive" or "unprogressive" managerial policy. The antitrust laws do not discriminate between "good" and "bad" monopolies, undue restraints of trade or violations of the Clayton Act. As has been indicated above, behavioral evidence may sometimes be helpful in demonstrating the presence or absence of monopoly or undue restraint of competition. Its relevance in antitrust law, however, does not go beyond its possible bearing on 193

these two issues. The economic study of the social performance of business can, on the other hand, make significant contributions to the antitrust laws, even though it is not directly germane to the trial of antitrust cases. It can help to clarify and improve the theory on the basis of which statutes are drawn, and assist in the long-run improvement of the statutory system. And it can offer useful advice to antitrust officials on the selection of cases, and to courts on the formulation of effective decrees.

Both the law and economics emphasize the same aspects of market structure and market behavior as relevant in seeking to measure the extent of monopoly power in a given market: the number and relative size of sellers and buyers, and their respective shares of the market; the way in which prices are formed and output determined; conditions affecting the possibility of new entries into the field; the relations among sellers, that is, their independence, dependence or interdependence in policy, and the presence or absence of other types of evidence bearing on the issue of collusion. This latter factor is of special importance where the number of sellers or buyers is sufficiently large to make close price leadership or other indicia of monopolistic pricing uncertain and unstable without the aid of informational services, or well understood procedures of price quotation, such as a basing point system, or trade association rules struck down in Sugar Institute v. United States308/

True, in one sense all commodities and services compete with each other for the consumer's dollar, so that houses and cars and education and trips to Florida are all in the same "market", for one sort of economic analysis. Nonetheless economists recognize the propriety of defining the market for purposes of anti-monopoly policy far more closely. Indeed, the chief economic analyses of definitions of the market used in legal cases, such as that in the recent Times- Picayune 309/ case, have criticized the courts for defining the relevant market too broadly. The closeness of agreement between lawyers and economists on this crucial issue is brought out by a recent comment in United States v. DuPont310/that "if prices, quantity of production and sale, and the quality of a seller's products are determined by pressures exerted on him by buyers and sellers of another's product, the product and the sellers must, for purposes of realistic analysis, be in the same 311/ 'market' and must be in competition with each other. This statement is a useful test of the concept, for if the rigid conditions set forth are not met, the products are not in the same market. Because some products compete for some uses, more or less directly, is not sufficient to justify classifying them for antitrust purposes as in the same market. Instead there must be proof of the particular use as to which there is direct competition between the products, in the sense of proven "shifts of 312/ business. ??

5. Economic Measures of Market Power. 

Economics has not yet produced a measure of monopoly power which can supplant the Courts' often laborious evaluation of a market's competitive character. Where there is only one supplier of a commodity or service for which there are no very close substitutes, the problem generally is easy, both in law and in economics although it is easier in economics than in law. At the other extreme, where there are many relatively small sellers; ready substitution among them on the part of buyers; many buyers; and easy entry, the chief problems that may arise are openly collusive attempts to restrict competition.313/ There, more likely is disagreement among lawyers and economists. 

In the case of many markets in between, however, where a good deal of monopoly power in the economic sense may be enhanced or maintained by various business policies, the relative importance of monopolistic and competitive elements, to both economists and lawyers, must depend upon a careful weighing of particular relevant factors. 

313/ See e.g. Fashion Originators Guild, F. 313 U.S. 457 (1941); Sugar Institute Inc. v. United States, 299 U.S. 

553 (1936); and Federal Trade Commission v. Cement 

Institute, 333 U.S. 683 (1948). 196

GTUP of Qu de

One approach to devising a statistical measure or test of monopoly has been to compare marginal costs and prices. 

Quite apart from the theoretical problems, the task of devising meaningful marginal cost statistics on a large scale has made this approach academic so far. Other studies have compared profit levels in one industry with those in others. Such comparisons, while interesting, offer no rules of thumb to the antitrust lawyer. High or low profits of themselves neither prove nor disprove the presence of effective monopoly or effective competition. But the fact, to take one example, that an industry earned a positive rate of profit even with huge excess capacity is proof that something was restraining competition. The economic inference from this fact would certainly not suffice to justify a legal judgment, but it would be important evidence. In American Tobacco v. United 

States, for example, that the defendants were able to increase their prices and profits for a time in the face of a general depression was relevant evidence of their market power and ability to act in concert, though it did not in itself prove their conspiracy.314,

Comparisons of price movements in one industry with those in others, while long accepted as relevant evidence in antitrust cases, can hardly be regarded as conclusive of 

314 See e.g. American Tobacco v. United States, 328 U.S. 781 (1946). 197

Best, dok

Ou On pe

monopoly, and have not been so considered by the courts. 

Only extreme cases of price inflexibility during extended periods which include general changes in economic conditions can properly be considered evidence of the market power of effective monopoly. Monopolies may charge flexible prices in their own interests, and competitive industries may have periods of stable supply and demand conditions, Nor are the classifications of industries by concentration ratios sufficiently precise to do more for this purpose than serve to start the analysis.

Again, a monopolistic market may be characterized by what seems to be a high rate of innovation and progressiveness.

The legal issue, of course, is whether competition is restrained, not whether the market is progressive. The law regards competition as the most reliable general force inducing progressiveness over extended periods of time. But a rapid rate of innovation in an industry where product and process improvements could be proved part of a continual thrust-and-parry of winning and losing business might be persuasive evidence of strong competitive pressures. may be overbalanced by other evidence--e,g., that competitors agreed to fix prices and compete only in quality. But it should often, though not necessarily, be relevant evidence. It 198

"A

Thus in United States v. DuPont, the District Court considered the process of innovation in the industry from this point of view, and found it evidence of the existence of real competitive pressures, especially in determining the extent to which various products were direct substitutes for each other, and could therefore be considered in the same market.315/ 

Yet the court also deemed it necessary to consider evidence as to the existence of an alleged price conspiracy. Had such a conspiracy been found to exist, there is no doubt from the opinion that it would have determined the decision despite the other evidence in the case.

Workable competition similarly offers the law an important tool of analysis, but not a new standard of legality; it directs attention to facts and factors of strategic significance, and helps to explain the economic behavior with which the courts have long grappled, as well as, permits more certain prediction of the market consequences of particular conduct. But it provides no short-hand substitute for careful judgment, in the light of the purposes and history of the antitrust laws. 

315/ See e.g., United States v. DuPont, 118 F. Supp. 41 (D. Del., 1953) (appeal noted April 23, 1954). 199

6. External Conditions. 

Severe depressions and inflations alike generate a demand that the competitive market be superseded as society's chief institution for determining prices and the scale of production, either in general, or in certain branches of production that either are relatively hard hit. In the long run, the preservation of free markets, and the achievement of the social and economic values served by the free market, depend upon maintaining, through appropriate fiscal and monetary policies, a balance between aggregate demand and the output of the economy at sufficiently high and stable levels of employment and of growth. While fiscal and monetary policies are outside the terms of reference of this Committee, we deem it appropriate to call attention to their vital importance as fundamental elements of effective economic policy, closely linked to the problems we have been asked to review.

In periods of violent inflation, price rises themselves become socially destructive, and resort to price-controls and other restrictive arrangement. Equally, in severe and extended depressions governments have adopted programs for restricing Competition either by permitting direct private cartellization or government supervised bodies on the N. R. A. model. They have experimented, too, with various depressioninspired scheres for controlling the world market for rubber, sugar, tin and other commodities, or have adopted increased 200

ta ea

tariffs and related barriers to competition. While it is easy to understand these depression-inspired demands to thwart the competitive process, it is clear such restrictive measures do nothing to cure depressions; they may aggravate them; and their presence makes recovery otherwise stimulated slower and more expensive to achieve, To the extent that they can reduce the burden of depression on certain groups, they shift it to others.

This is not to suggest, that various forms of industry cooperation in the past have not accomplished useful purposes, in influencing opinion helping programs of mobilization or even mitigating inflation's effects. However, unless, as in war-time, inflation goes to the point at which the government is unwilling or unable to impose adequate fiscal and monetary restrictions, neither inflation nor depression indicate any need to modify the policies of the anti-trust laws. The presence of depression or inflation calls primarily for corrective action by the Federal Reserve System and the government to balance aggregate demand with full-employment supply, not measures to reduce competition in particular markets. 

Conditions of general depression imply that in many sectors, especially capital goods industries and those dependent upon them, much productive capacity stands idle. Under these circumstances, producers have low incremental costs, and 201

f:

at any prices above these costs, they can profitably accept large amounts of business in order to earn something towards their overheads. Where competition prevails, and demand is falling, this condition results in a strong downward pressure on price. For each seller has an incentive to bid lower in order to obtain more volume. Under these circumstances, it is good business for any seller to produce and sell so long as each sale yields something over the out-of-pocket costs associated with it. Indeed, it may be economic for some firms to accept orders even below their average variable costs, if their shut-down losses would outweigh operating losses. In some branches of production prices do not fall so much as physical volume during depressions, while in others the reverse is true. Relative rigidity of prices may occur where prices are "administered", or where the chief element in costs consists of wage-rates which are themselves inflexible, and which could alone prevent the industry from pursuing policies of much price flexibility. Profits, of course, suffer during a general depression in both classes of industries, and may suffer worse where prices are flexible and demand largely unresponsive at that time to price changes, Other conditions external to the market can vitally affect the setting in which markets function and the effectiveness of competition within them. The organization of the labor market upon which a firm or an industry depends obviously 202

Yodo!

WOW YOU d'acqué gr. » sqq WE

influences its costs and its capacity to increase productivity, While trade-union activities, and the laws relating to them, are outside the terms of reference of our Committee, we call attention to their improtance as elements of economic organization which may effect both the competitive potential of a firm or an industry and its ability to innovate through cost reduction. 

Similarly, situations of undue market power have depended in the past, and may now depend, on tariff protection. Equivalent protection against competition is obtained by a variety of state, local and national laws requiring governmental units to deal only with restricted classes of suppliers. Many such enactments unreasonably limit competition and create or perpetuate positions of great economic advantage. In the nature of the tariff problem, the bearing of tariffs on the maintenance of high degrees of market power in domestic commerce must be studied case by case. We recommend that the Department of Justice and the Federal Trade Commission, in carrying out their enforcement and investigatory responsibilities, systematically examine the domestic market effects of tariffs and appropriately transmit their findings to the President,

Tarrif Commission, or the Congress. We recommend also that tariff legislation expressly recognize the purposes of the antitrust laws as one of the justifications for and objectives of both unilateral and negotiated tariff reductions. 203

.ܐ ser bis adres ast www/05: CO M... CO

In the international sphere, arrangements for limiting competition, either by dividing world markets or restricting competition within markets, are not uncommon. Generically known as international cartels, such plans are often legal under the laws of other countries. Several countries have recently adopted new laws, or revised older laws, dealing with monopolies, restraints of trade and restrictive business practices. These laws differ widely in theory and in approach. 

One common feature seems to be that they do not apply to international cartels, except insofar as such organizations may exercise a restrictive influence on competition within their respective home markets. International cartels and business practices restricting competition may have farreaching effects on the economy of the United States. In addition, such restraints may cramp the business freedom of American firms operating abroad. In most instances, American antitrust laws, no matter how vigorously enforced, cannot deal with such conduct. While some of the more obvious cases may come within reach, the Sherman Act enforcement can touch only a small percentage of international restrictive arrangements adversely affecting Americans, We believe that the best interests of the United States, and of American business at home and abroad, would be served by the adoption of an international treaty or convention translating Sherman Act philosophy into effective international law. We recognize that other countries, with 204

1.

differing views, are unlikely at this time to go so far. 

Nonetheless, such a treaty, adequately safeguarding American interests, could do much to encourage foreign laws to preserve free enterprise.

Finally, tax laws of the United States also offer certain incentives for combination, and disadvantages for smaller growing firms as well as for companies which might otherwise sell off part of their properties to separate enterprises. 

While recent studies have brought forward relevant and suggestive data there is need for a systematic review to isolate possible tax changes in the interest of furthering the general antitrust goals. We are not prepared now to indicate which features of the tax system would be of greatest importance in such a study. Quite possibly, different tax problems arise in this connection for different industries. 

We do believe, however, that the tax aspect of the problem is of importance, and that a comprehensive and thorough evaluation of antitrust policy requires further study of its tax elements. 

7. Conclusion. 

What economics can do for law in the antitrust field has two aspects. First, it can document and support the public policy favoring competition. Generally speaking, the majority of Anglo-American economists support competition for four series of reasons, which are of coordinate importance: 205

(ܐ܂

(1) because the actual level of prices in competitive markets should in the short run more accurately reflect the influence of demand and of cost, and thus in the long run help guide the flow of capital towards the most productive possible uses; (2) because the goad of competition provides the most powerful and pervasive incentives for product innovations and product development, and for long-run cost-reduction, both through improved technology and improved management; these forces make themselves felt in the constant process of product variation, and through the pressures implicit in the fact that competitive conditions offer an open opportunity to new entrants in a particular industry; (3) because competitive conditions in business should lead to a more equitable distribution of income among the factors of production; and (4) a view held with somewhat less unanimity than the others, because the more flexible prices of competitive markets should make it easier and cheaper for the economy to adjust to industrial fluctuations, and for the Federal Reserve System and the government to carry through effective contracyclical programs of stabilization, primarily utilizing methods of monetary and fiscal policy. 

Beyond this rationalization of antitrust goals, economics can contribute step-by-step to the procedures by which conduct is scrutinized under the standards of the law. It can assist 206

in ܀܆ Of

in the selection of evidence as relevant; in the analysis of arguments and the display of inconsistency or fallacy both in particular cases, and in considering the relation among statutes; in demonstrating the effect of market prices in their background; and above all in helping to devise remedies which can help to achieve the basic purposes of the law.

Thus far, the teachings of economics support the premise of the law 

that effective competition is worth striving for in the organization of those industries and markets where it is practical possibility. Economic justifications for antitrust policy is not its sole support. But an economic rationale complements the social and political motives which also underpin those: laws as crucial to our program for economic freedom. 

There is no reason for discouragement with the prospect either of competition or of the antitrust laws. Studies have been made of the trend of monopoly over the past 50 odd years. 

Despite the severe technical limitations of the data, it is significant that they lend no support to the idea that monopoly has increased. In manufacturing, the largest and most important segment of the antitrust area, several comparisons have been made of the degree of concentration in the largest corporations over different periods of time. It is striking that the result of these studies is always the same; a slight 207

”། dem fnd ce 18 

Den vreed CO 197173

L decrease, if anything, in the degree of concentration, and certainly no increase since the turn of the Century. There is no factual basis for a widespread impression that overall concentration increased during World War II, or was increased by a merger movement during 1940-47, although there were significant increases in concentration in several important industries. There is probably more competition in wholesale and retail trade than in 1900. In general, we no longer have industries dominated by single large firms, as we often did then; nor isolated local markets dominated by local monopolists. Alternatives to the consumer, whether families or business firms, have increased and not decreased. The progress of science and technology, the decline in transportation costs, and the development of new forms of production and distribution, have been both result and cause of competition, and the antitrust laws have provided them with a favoring climate. These developments, it should be noted, took place despite intervals when the antitrust remedies were not vigorously pursued, nor consistently applied. They are evidence of the immense competitive potential of the American economy, and of American society. And they give reason for sober hope that the record in the future could be much better, if antitrust enforcement is maintained and developed. 208

in 1.

For none would deny that restraints of competition still trouble our economy, nor that the degree of concentration in American economic life is still high. And the gain of monopoly restrictions must always be tempting under a competitive system. From the record of antitrust success in helping to maintain a competitive economy, it would be strange indeed to conclude that we need less, not more of a set of laws that has served us so well in the past. We conclude that major long-term governmental effort is justified in seeking to prevent the influence of monopoly on prices, on progress, and on opportunity. On problems of genuine importance, the main concepts of the antitrust laws, as they have developed in sixty-four years of trial and error, can do as much in the future to improve the competitive functioning of the national economy.

III. Recommendations. 

1. In light of our Sherman Act review, as well as the perspective of economic knowledge, we conclude that, with the exceptions noted, the central core of antitrust concepts is sound. Preservation of this core we deem of crucial import to the political, social and economic well being of the people of the United States. We do not believe these essentials should be altered by legislation. Instead they should continue to develop through the tested historical processes of administrative and judicial constructions.

209

ار 

To conclude that the basic corpus of the antitrust laws is satisfactory, however, is not to conclude that - beyond the core no change is needed. Peripheral parts of the antitrust statutory framework require reinterpretation and, in some instances, amendment or repeal. These and other recommendations are later detailed in the Patents, Distribution, Foreign Commerce and Exemptions sections of this Report. Beyond substantive changes, present is the need for reform in antitrust administration and enforcement. 

This, too, we treat in detail. Only made here are the additional general recommendations which flow from our evaluation of the crux of antitrust concepts. 

2. We do not believe that relaxation of antitrust standards are desirable in situations of boom or depression. Stabilization of employment at high levels is primarily a task for monetary and fiscal policy. Effective competition can facilitate that task and reduce its costs. Indeed, in the long run, we believe that such stabilization is an indispensable condition to the maintenance of a free market economy, and to all the values, social and economic, which that economy serves. 

3. The role of American business in the world market is increasingly important, To protect American business interests in that market, and to assure the American consumer that American economic activities abroad are carried on in 210

ways that should serve the nation's long-run interests, we recommend that in the interpretation of the Sherman Act the concept of "commerce with foreign nations" correspond in breadth to the scope of the idea of "Commerce among the several states". Thus antitrust would not restrict competitive American investments abroad. 

4. Since the enforcement of the Sherman Act with respect to foreign trade can deal at best with few of the restraints which international cartels impose upon the American economy, we recommend that the government support, negotiate and sign, and the Congress by appropriate procedures ratify an international treaty or convention against restraints of trade and monopolistic practices. 211

II PATENT-ANTITRUST PROBLEMS/ 

Corollary to antitrust problems in production and sale are questions stemming from the protection for innovation the patent system provides. "To promote the progress of Science and useful Arts" is the objective of Article I, Clause 8, of the Federal Constitution. From this charter all patent laws derive. To serve this Constitutional end, Congress has enacted patent laws stemming from the original Act of 1790 and now consolidated in the 1952 Patent Code 2/ Our task requires no full-scale analysis or evaluation of the Patent System. Consideration of myriad aspects of the patent laws encompasses interacting technological, economic and social factors of which no balanced public policy appraisal can be made solely within antitrust boundaries. Instead, we set forth that system's announced goals relevant to patentantitrust policies.. Then we examine the doctrines developed to curb patent abuses resulting in departures from these aims. 

Paramount is the question whether these two policies conflict or whether one may not be accommodated to the other. The starting point is understanding of the basic nature of the patent grant.3/ 

1/ Foreign Commerce aspects are treated in that section of the Report. 2/ 35 U.S.c. (1952) §§ 1 293. 

3/ From Pennock v. Dialogue, 2 Pet. 1 (U.S. 1829) to date virtually every Supreme Court opinion on patent policy analyzes 212

dorit wɔ ablution, ens 

---

To effectuate the Constitutional objective of promoting technological growth, issuance of a valid patent grant goes beyond common law to give the inventor a statutory seventeen years' right to exclude all others from manufacture, use and sale of the patented invention. This limited-time monopoly exclusiveness creates property rights entitled to all Constitutional protections accorded to other forms of property.5/ 

Primary to this concept of exclusiveness are the public purposes of fostering early disclosure of patentable inventions and their unlimited availability upon patent expiration. Corollary is incentive to invent by holding out to the inventor economic reward ancillary to his patent's exclusiveness. 

Co-equal is incentive to invest entrepreneurial risk capital in research and development vital to discovery as well as the early period of commercial utilization. 

3/ (cont.) the fundamental characteristics of the patent grant. These are recapitulated in historical context in 

United States v. Line Material Co., 333 U.S. 287, 321 (1948).

The Supreme Court has frequently referred to the patent as a "privilege conditioned by a public purpose. The Court has, nevertheless, repeatedly affirmed the property concept of the patent, going back to Wilson v. Rousseau, Howard 646, 674 (U.S. 1846).

poll mea At pr

Patent rights must, of course, be geared to other public policies, among which antitrust is the Nation's predominant means of assuring competition as the general rule. At heart, patent theory aims through a limited-time monopoly to further 

a prime goal of competition innovation and improved technology.

At the same time, patent policy hopes to improve the competitive process itself by, first, encouraging marketability through shortrun commercial protection and, second, by disclosing a protected invention around which others may invent potentially competing products.

Carving out a temporary legal monopoly is deemed an intermediate step toward achieving a creative and dynamic economy 

also an antitrust end. First, the temporary monopoly exclusiveness of the patent grant embraces only something new and useful which is the product of inventive faculties, thus adding to the economy's store of published inventions later generally accessible to competitive enterprise. So the public receives consideration in exchange for the temporary legal monopoly of the grant, Second, within the seventeen years' exclusivity, the patentee's conduct earns for him only those pecuniary rewards within the claims of his disclosed invention and its utilization circumscribed by the patent or general laws.

Inventions patentable and conditions for patentability are governed by 35 U.S.c. (1952), §§ 101-104. 215

1... duter EM pat sti laz res cl ве to

Indeed, the exclusion of others from the practice of the patented invention without consent of the owner itself may stimulate "inventing around" the patent. This is why a single invention, disclosed by the patent claims, may set off a chain reaction sparking other patentable ideas. Absent patent disclosure, protection might be sought via trade secret law, thus sealing off competitors' opportunity to conceive new products to compete with the patented invention.

The temporary patent sanctuary may further encourage expenditures for improved development and marketing of patented articles or processes. The result may be to promote more competition in patented commerce than would prevail without patent protection. For monopoly of the patent grant is then checked by availability of patented substitutes. 

These are the Patent System's generally sanctioned goals. To assure each patent is employed consistent with these ends, two general limitations are imposed. First, the patent law requires the patentee to exercise his right to exclude strictly within the claims of his invention and regulates certain uses of the patent. Second, conduct not sanctioned by patent law may run afoul of the antitrust prohibitions. And even when a patentee stays nominally within the patent law, his patent, like other property, may be used to violate the antitrust laws. 216

Initially, a patent may be acquired by government grant, by purchase, or by grant-back provision in a patent-license agreement. The patent acquired, the patentee must decide whether utilization of the patent is, for the present, commercially feasible. If use is practicable, he may use it himself, pool it with patents of others, or license it or a package of patents. The decision to license made, he may seek to impose license restrictions, These may include territorial, quantity, price, field of use or other limitations,

Or he may seek to tie unpatented to patented goods or impose restrictions on purchases from him or his licensee. Each of these decisions may pose problems of patent policy, antitrust laws or both. 1. Acquisition 

The patent laws set forth requirements for securing a patent grant and proscribe how it may be assigned, 10/ These procedures and requirements concern us only since issuance of patents later held invalid may generate antitrust problems. "Weak" patents may offer an undesired cloak of immunity and leverage for antitrust abuses. Improving Patent Office issuance procedures is, therefore, to this extent a matter for antitrust concern.

2. Nonuse of Patented Inventions

Viewed either from the standpoint of patent or antitrust policy, nonuse should be considered in the context of particular factual situations. Mere nonuse is equivocal. 

An invention, though patentable, may not be commercially feasible. Alternative products may be cheaper and of better quality. Materials required for production may not be available at practical cost. Or time may be needed to build up market acceptance for the new product. Fatents may be obtained as a legitimate protection against appropriation by others of the part of a patented combination of greater commercial value 

Nonuse, on the other hand, may than some of its elements. 28/ be part of a scheme by individual or conspiratorial conduct to foreclose competitors or may evidence intent to monopolize. 

Recognizing that nonuse may not transgress patent policy, 29/ that the Supreme Court held in the Paper Bag case that the mere showing that the patentee had not used or licensed his invention was no defense to an equity suit to enjoin infringement.

Referring to record evidence of business reasons for nonuse, the Court observed that "*** It is certainly disputable that 

28/ Special Equipment Co. v. Coe, 324 U.S. 370, 382 (1945). 

29/ Continental Paper Bag Co. v. Eastern Paper Bag Co., 210 U.S. 

405 (1908). Prior to Paper Bag one court refused to enjoin infringement of a valid patent where there had been nonuse for an unreasonably long time, Hoe v. Knap, 27 Fed. 204 (C.C.,N.D. 111. 

1886). This decision was repudiated in Heaton-Peninsular Button 

Fastener Co. v. Eureka Specialty Co., 77 Fed. 288, 294 (6th Cir. 

1896) and quoted with approval on this point by the Supreme Court in Bement v. National Harrow Co., 186 U.S. 70 (1902). 224
