There was no question of a diminished supply or of increase in prices, and can it be said, as a matter of law, that a non-use was unreasonable which had for its motive the saving of expenses that would have been involved by changing the equipment of a factory from one set of machines to another? 

And even if the old machines could have been altered, the expense would have been considerable.30/ 

Granting relief by injunction, the Court concluded: "Whether, however, a case cannot arise where, regarding the situation of the parties in view of the public interest, a court of equity might be justified in withholding relief by injunction, we do not decide."31/ 

The Paper Bag case thus implies that reasonable nonuse may be distinguished from suppression where the patentee neither uses nor makes available to others the use of a technologically meritorious invention at the time capable of being marketed on a commercially practicable scale. Paper Bag further implies that patent law objectives are realized only when patented inventions are utilized to the fullest extent commercially feasible. To achieve its ends, the patent system, as does free enterprise, relies on the incentive to profit from using any patented invention for which there is public demand and

3. Patent Pools 

The decision to use a patent made, then at hand is a question whether or not to "pool." "Patent Pool" is not a term of art.35/ It has described situations ranging from an elaborate patent holding and research arrangement36/ to a simple cross-license. 

For our purpose, "patent pool" is defined as any arrangement for the interchange of patent rights where either one or more of the patent owners, or some separate 

34/ Cf. United States v. Aluminum Co. of America, 148 F. 2d 416 

(2d Cir. 1945); United States v. General Electric Co. (Carboly), supra, n. 20; United States v. duPont de Nemours & Co., supra, a. 12. 

35/ See United States v. Line Material Co., 333 U.S.287, 312 (1948). 

36/ Hartford-Empire v. United States, 323 U.S. 386 (1945), 227

entity, has the right to license others under the pooled patents.37/ 

This definition, consistent with most cases involving patent pools, excludes simple cross-license agreements. 

The need for patent pools, and conversely the dangers inherent in their use, has been judicially recognized. The technological interdependence of a vast number of patents and their legal mutual exclusiveness frequently necessitate exchange or waiver of patent rights.38/ In summary form, patent pools are beneficial in resolving patent conflicts and making the pooled patents available to others.39/ They may facilitate patent licensing of a multiplicity of patents; or make possible the use of mutually dependent or blocking patents. Patent pooling may thus promote rather than restrain competition. 40/

On the other hand, patent pooling necessarily involves agreement and cooperation. If created with illicit purpose, or misused, it may lead to unreasonable restraints of trade and monopolization. Adjudicated cases demonstrate the manner

In sum, then, patent pools may, on the one hand, be crucial to feasible patent utilization or, on the other, facilitate competitive restraint. Determination of legality in a given case may require examination of the formation purpose, the power pooling brings, or the pool's operating patterns. In any one case, some or all of these factors may be determinative. 

4. License Limitations. 

Whether patents are utilized individually or pooled, pivotal are the limitations that may be imposed on their license. A patent license is no more than a waiver of the patentee's right to exclude the licensee from making, using or selling 62/ the patented invention. Initially, of course, patents may be licensed in package or separately. a.

Package Licensing 

A package license may include more than one or all of the licensor's patents. Packaging is frequently used to avoid troublesome questions of infringement, complex bookkeeping, the difficulty of determining which patents cover the present and future needs of the license, cost differences, and similar practical considerations.

A possible vice of this practice lies in its potential use to coerce the licensee to accept a license under one patent on condition of acceptance of a license under another, or the 62/ 

DeForest Co. v. United States, 273 U.S. 236, 242 (1927). ​235

noki kat

entire package. This practice, the Supreme Court, by analogy to "tying clause" cases, condemned in Ethyl Gasoline v. United States.63/ "The patent monopoly of one invention may no more be enlarged for the exploitation of another ** than for the exploitation of an unpatented article * * or for the -、 exploitation of a business not embraced within the patent.

" 

And in United States v. Paramount Pictures, 64/ this disapproval was extended to the analogous "block booking" of copyrighted films. There the Supreme Court held illegal a refusal to license one or more copyrights unless another copyright is accepted. But the Court was quick to add: "We do not suggest that films may not be sold in blocks or groups, when there is no requirement, express or implied, for the purchase of more than one film." 

In Automatic Radio Mfg. Co. v. 

Hazeltone Research, Inc., 65/ absent a requirement to accept a license on all patents or none, and absent the condition of acceptance of another and different license, 63/ 309 U.S. 436, 459 (1940).

the Supreme Court sanctioned a license of all of Hazeltine's patents and developments for a royalty measured by a percentage of the licensee's sales.

Strictures against package licensing should not be applied to frustrate good faith license offers. Package licensing should be prohibited only where there is refusal to license less than a complete package after a request for an identified less-than-package license. Additionally, the licensor should not be required to justify on any proportional basis the royalty rate for less than the complete package, so long as the rate set is not so disproportionate as to amount to a refusal to license less than the complete package. For example, where a substantial group of patents are offered at a flat royalty rate, the deletion of one or several specified patents will not affect the rate. Rather the benefit to the licensee will lie in exclusion of products subject to the several patents from the royalty computation, and in his freedom to challenge their validity if asserted against him. Moreover, where several "per piece" licenses are requested and offered, the mere fact that the sum of the per piece license royalties exceeds the package royalty rate should not of itself be considered a condition that all or no patents be taken. 237

B. Individual License Limitations 

Of prime importance is the yardstick announced in United

States v. General Electric 66 

for measuring the remuneration. to which the patentee is entitled. The Court formulated, as the test of determining whether or not the patentee's conduct is within the ambit of his grant, the standard that he may license "for any royalty, or upon any condition the performance of which is reasonably within the reward which the patentee by the grant of the patent is entitled to 67/ secure."

This makes permissible all restrictions "normally and reasonably adapted" to the patent policy of securing to the inventor rewards ancillary to his patent grant.

Applying the General Electric standard involves delicate balance of private incentive with public benefit the dominant aim of patent policy. The basic criterion generally applied by the courts was enunciated in the Motion Picture patents case: 68/ 

66/ United States v. General Electric Co., 272 U.S. 476, 490 (1926), later considered on facts and holding. 

67/ Royalty payment is the common form of consideration for a patent license and is governed by general law. 

Royalties may be based on any standard which does not restrict the sale of unpatented goods. Illustrative are a lump sum; a fixed annual fee or percentage payment; or a percentage payment; or a percentage of the licensee's total sales, including in part non-patented articles. 

Unless so limited by antitrust decree, the amount of royalty is entirely a matter of agreement between the parties. .. 

68/ Motion Picture Patents Co. v. Universal Film Mfg. Co., 

243 U.S. 502, 510 (1917). -238

· * H₂ 7'1

"The scope of every patent is limited to the invention described in the claims contained in it, read in the light of the specification. These so mark where the progress claimed by the patent begins and where it ends that they have been aptly likened to the description in a deed, which sets the bounds to the grant which it contains. It is to the claims of every patent, therefore, that we must turn when we are seeking to determine what the invention is, the exclusive use of which is given to the inventor by the grant provided for by the statute, 'He can claim nothing beyond them.'

Thus "normally and reasonably within the reward" encompasses the rights of exclusion only as disclosed by the claims of the patented invention. This limiting policy aims to prevent monopolistic extention beyond the discovery and resulting discouragement rather than promotion of invention.

Principal problems arise in demarking license limitations which patent policy permits. These boundaries are usually marked out in the context of private equity suits to enjoin infringement. There courts deny equitable relief when the patentee subverts patent law policy by seeking to restrain commerce in unpatented or patented articles not within the monopoly granted by the patent in suit. Typical of the 

Supreme Court's rationale are these observations in the 

Morton Salt case: .69/ "* * * the public policy which includes inventions within the granted monopoly excludes from it all that is not embraced in the invention, It equally forbids the use of the patent to secure an exclusive right or limited monopoly not granted by the Patent Office and which it is contrary to public policy to grant *** Equity may rightly withhold its assistance from such a use of the patent by declining to entertain a suit for infringement, and should do so at least until it is made to appear 

69/ Morton Salt Co. v. Suffiger, 314 V.s. 488, 492, 493 (1941). 239

TAGG CO 20!

that the improper practice has been abandoned and that the consequences of the misuse of the patent have been dissipated."

Any extra-patent restraint on competition is invalidated without inquiry into the amount or substantiality of the commerce outside the grant. Thus, relief against infringement is withheld by regarding the extra-patent control as a per se transgression of patent public policy. 

A patent license may include a variety of restrictions on the patentee or his licensee: 

1. Limitations on patentee. Any restraint imposed upon the patentee himself such as limitations on his price, quantity, type of products, or territory is beyond the grant. Nor can a patentee covenant not to compete in the production or sale of competing goods,70/ or give to the licensee a veto power over licenses or assignments of the patent to others.11/ Distinguishable are the legal consequences ancillary to an exclusive license. It is well settled that an exclusive license. as such is valid under the patent law.72/ By definition 

70/ McCullough v. Kammerer Corp., 166 F. 2d 759 (9th Cir. 1948). 

71/ See Besser Mfg. Co. v. United States, 343 U.S. 444 (1952). 

72/ Waterman v. Mackenzie, 138 U.S. 252 (1891). 240

and implication, an exclusive license excludes all others, including the patentee-licensor, from practice of the license in the matters covered by it. We believe that the exclusive license is in legal effect nothing more or less than an assignment of the patent rights. It follows that it should have the same status as an assignment specifically authorized by the patent law.73/. 

Limitations on the licenco may include: 

2. Price. License limitations on price are crucial and have evoked both approval and criticism. Supreme 

Court sanction was given in the 1926 General Electric case.74/ There General Electric licensed Westinghouse to manufacture and sell its lamps at prices fixed by General Electric on its own sales. The Court upheld this limitation as a condition of sale "normally and reasonably adapted to secure pecuniary reward for the patentee's monopoly." Stressing profit as one of the valuable elements of the patentee's exclusive rights, the Court observed that "The higher the price, the greater the profit, unless it is prohibitory. "When the patentee licenses another to make and vend and retains the right to continue to make and vend on his own account," the Court continued, "the price at which 

73/ Ibid.

United States v. General Electric Co., 272 U.S. 

476 71926). 11 އ 241

!.

his licensee will sell will necessarily affect the price at which he can sell his own patented goods." To the 

Court, therefore, it seemed "entirely reasonable that he should say to the licensee, 'Yes, you may make and sell articles under my patent but not as to destroy the profit that I wish to obtain by making and selling them myself."75/ 

General Electric's apparently sweeping scope does not legitimate all contracts of a patentee designed to increase earnings on his patents.16/ Thus, General Electric itself reaffirmed the previously settled rule that sale of a patented article puts control of the purchaser's resale price beyond the patentee's power. 5. Scope of Misuse. 

Still in the process of formulation is the outer reach of the patent misuse doctrine. In Russell Company v. Comfort 

Equipment Corporation91/ the Court denied injunctive relief for infringement on the grounds that an exclusive distributor franchise covering the patented product, coupled with an exclusive dealing provision and a warranty agreement for purchase of assembly materials from the patentee, constituted patent misuse, even though the patent itself was not misused. 

We believe this approach confuses conduct of the patentee. related to misuse of the patent in suit, and limitations upon the manner of sale or other business dealings in articles resulting from the use of the patent. Realistic analysis should determine whether the patent itself contributes to the marketing practice attacked. Where it does, infringement relief should be denied. 

6. Contributory Infringement and Patent Misuse. 

Another question not yet fully clarified is the status of contributory infringement suits in patent misuse cases. 

Contributory infringement is in essence the application of the law of joint torts to patent infringement. This doctrine provides that "where one makes and sells one 

91/ 194 F 2d 592 (7th Cir. 1952). Cf. Cardox Corp. v. Armstrong 

Coal-Break Co., 194 F 2d 376 (7th Cir. 1952). 248

ساحلية 300

element of a combination covered by a patent with the intention and for the purpose of bringing about its use in such a combination, he is guilty of contributory infringement and is equally liable to the patentee with him who, in 192/ fact, organizes and completes the combination. 

It is evident that the defendant in a contributory infringement action does not practice the invention.

Infringement occurs only when the complete combination is assembled or the process is performed. Hence, the plaintiff in a contributory infringement suit is in reality seeking to enjoin conduct not covered by the patent and thus to control the sale of something outside its scope. 

We refrain from exploring all of the technical patent law refinements of this doctrine. The apparent inherent conflict between contributory infringement and misuse stressed the need for placing some limitations upon the scope of the patent misuse doctrine in order to place contributory infringement in the proper context of the joint tort rules generally. 

In many instances the unpatented element may be the heart of the patented combination. To render the patentee powerless to sue for contributory infringement may thus denude him of his patent rights, 92/ Thomson-Houston Electric Co. v. Ohio Brass Co., 80 F. 

712, 721 (6th Cir. 1997). བ་བ 249

The most normal means for enforcement of patent rights One or numerous infringeis the direct infringement suit. 27 ment suits,98/ by themselves, constitute no antitrust violation. The effect of Kobe v. Dempsey is not clear. That case was based on the finding that the infringement suit and associated activities were intended to further plaintiff's monopolistic practices and to eliminate defendant as a competitor. Treble damages were awarded to the infringing defendant on the ground that the court should not be a "vehicle for maintaining and carrying out an unlawful monopoly."102/ To deny recovery, the court said, "would permit a monopolizer to smother every potential competitor with litigation" and "leave the competitor without a remedy. 1,103/

We believe there is obvious merit in the rationale of the Kobe case where the evidence clearly established damages resulting from an infringement suit brought in furtherance of an agreement or plan violative of the antitrust laws. 

But it is equally important not to imperil free access to the courts for determination and protection of patent rights. 

Accordingly, we make the following recommendations : 

1, Where it is shown that an infringement suit has, in fact, been brought in furtherance of an agreement or plan 

101/ Kobe v. Dempsey Pump Co., 198 F. 2d 416 (10th Cir. 1952) cert. denied, 334 U.S. 837 (1952). 

102/ Id. at 424.. 

103/ Id. at 425. 253

stant res whe TA pe

to violate the antitrust laws and that the defendant sustained resulting damages, treble damages should be recoverable, whether or not there was a colorable claim of infringement. 

2. On the other hand, there may be misuse of the defense of antitrust violation as, for example, where the defense is without probable cause and for the purpose of frustrating the patentee's right to relief. This is basically a problem of judicial control. We therefore recommend that the courts should make full use of their powers to curb such attempts to defeat justice. Useful methods include pre-trial procedures to ascertain whether a valid antitrust defense is presented. Similarly, the court might give weight to the bona fides of the defense or counterclaim in exercising power to award costs or treble damages and attorneys' fees to the patentee.

Closely related are problems arising where an antitrust defense is pleaded as a defense to patent infringement suit, Here courts have recognized that not every antitrust violation by the patentee-plaintiff should be a defense to a suit on contract or on some other tort 105/ 104/ 

Where the patent infringement suit and the conduct in the antitrust violation are wholly unrelated, the defense has been rejected.106/ These decisions properly recognize that misuse of the defense would

: subject the patentee to a severe loss of rights merely because he is a patentee and often provide an undeserved shelter for a patent infringer 107/

Customary injunctive and monetary relief for patent infringement may of course directly implement an antitrust violation. To illustrate, the patent in suit may be such an essential part of an illegal monopolization that injunctive relief for patent infringement exercises the very monopoly power illegally 108/ acquired in violation of Section 2 of the Sherman Act.. In auch instances courts have wisely limited patent infringement relief to avoid prejudice to antitrust objectives. 

In sum, we believe, antitrust violation should be considered a defense to a patent infringement action only when it is shown that the patent in suit is integral to the violation and that grant of customary patent relief conflicts with antitrust goals. 

Further, in any patent infringement suit in which antitrust violation is the basis of defense, or counterclaims, the court pursuant to Rule 42(b) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, should order separate trials of the issues of patent validity and infringement. Such separation may be essential not only "in furtherance of convenience and to avoid prejudice," but also "to serve the ends of justice." Trial of the infringement issue should precede trial of the antitrust issues except where, in the discretion of the court, pleadings, pre-trial orders and discovery on balance favor prior trial of the antitrust issues.

8. Patent Misuse as Antitrust Violation 

Arising here is the question whether patent misuse is in every case a per se antitrust vidation. Uncertainty stems from the Mercoid case, where the factual situation presented some elements of antitrust violation, But these were not relied upon in the decision. Instead, Mr. Justice Douglas' majority opinion coalesced patent with antitrust violation: "The legality of any attempt to bring unpatented goods within the protection of the patent is measured by the antitrust laws and not by the patent law. * * * the effort here made to control competition in [the] unpatented device plainly violates the antitrust laws, even apart from the price-fixing provisions of the license agreements."109/ 

109 Mercoid Corp. v. Minneapolis-Honeywell Regulator Co., 320 U.S. 680, 684 (1944). Mr. Justice Douglas, on the other hand, in Transparent-Wrap Mach. Corp. v. Stokes & Smith Co., 329 U.S. 637, 641 (1947), distinguished between patent and antitrust policies in these words: "The requirement that a licensee under a patent use unpatented material or device with the patent might violate the antitrust laws but for the attempted protection of the patent. The condemnation of the practice, however, does not depend on such a showing. Though control of the unpatented article or device falls short of a prohibited restraint of trade or monopoly, it will not be sanctioned." 

And in the Mercoid case, Mr. Justice Roberts regarded the issue as "a pure question of the extent of the right of exclusion conferred by the patent statute. It no wise involved the antitrust acts." 320 U.S. 662 at 675. 256

MCGODOTA'

We disapprove this confusion of patent and antitrust policies. This does not deny that the self-same conduct of the patentee may violate both sets of laws. Yet in no other 

Supreme Court opinion prior to Mercoid has there been any suggestion that antitrust violation automatically results from any patent misuse, regardless of proof of the elements of violation required under the Sherman, Federal Trade and Clayton Acts.

We reject the view that any violation of patent law necessarily violates the antitrust laws, From some abuses of patent policy may flow consequences not drastic enough to meet antitrust prerequisites of effect on competition. 

In addition, many patent abuses are more effectively curbed by simply denying the patentee equitable relief as a matter of patent policy, thus avoiding necessity for a complex adjudication to determine whether the same conduct also violates the antitrust laws. Holding every patent law violation at the same time an antitrust violation would, moreover, treat the patent owner different from all other owners of property subject to antitrust. For antitrust has its own measure of permissive and wrongful conduct. To say that action beyond the borders of the patent grant is a per se antitrust violation is to ignore the Supreme Court's distinctions between the variant statutory standards of the 

Sherman, Federal Trade Commission and Clayton Acts as well as 257

9. Remedies 

Where antitrust violation is found, remedies aim to cure the consequences of unlawful conduct and thus restore 

Patents may, of course, be 110/ "effective competition". used to violate the antitrust laws and, like any form 111/ of property, may then be regulated by antitrust decree. 

Requiring analysis are questions arising from three possible remedial provisions: (1) compulsory licensing at reasonable rates; (2) compulsory licensing royalty-free, and (3) dedication to the public of patent rights.

The Supreme Court has held compulsory licensing at reasonable rates within the range of remedies which may be necessary to correct past and prevent future violations. 112/ By compulsory licensing, the exclusive nature of the patent grant is destroyed at least until competitive conditions

Trade-Mark-Antitrust 

Trade-mark rights in the United States, unlike patents or statutory copyright, are not the outgrowth of a statutory monopoly expressly sanctioned by Constitutional provision. 

Rather they find their basic legal protection in the common law doctrine of priority of adoption and use. The federal registration statutes, including the Trade-Mark Act of 1946 (Lanham Act), 146/ 145/ merely serve to strengthen these common law rights. These provisions give the owner of a registered trade-mark certain procedural and substantive safeguards for more effective protection against infringement or unfair competition.

As far as domestic trade is concerned, not until recently was it suggested that the rather limited substantive protection afforded trade-mark owners under the previous Trade-Mark Acts of 1881 and 1905 might conflict with the antitrust laws. Some fifteen years after Congressional attempts toward a more effective 147/ registration statute had been initiated, trade-mark-antitrust relationships were considered for the first time. Several staff members of the Antitrust Division testified before a

Alfred Bell & Co. v. Catalda 

Fine Arts, Inc., 191 F. 2d 99, 106 (2d Cir. 1951). Judge 

Frank there said: "We have here a conflict of policies: (a) that of preventing piracy of copyrighted matter and (b) that of enforcing the antitrust laws. We must balance the two, taking into account the comparative innocence or guilt of the parties, the moral character of their respective acts, the extent of the harm to the public interest, the penalty inflicted on the plaintiff if we deny it relief. As the defendants' piracy is unmistakably clear, while the plaintiffs' infraction of the antitrust laws is doubtful and at most marginal, we think the enforcement of the first policy should outweigh enforcement of the second." 270

The general equity power of the courts to deny relief to prevent furtherance of an illegal act is sufficient protection, and should be applied to contestable and incontestable marks.

In recommending repeal of Section 33(b)(7), we further recommend the following amendment of Section 46(a) to remove any doubt that incontestable trade-marks are within antitrust reach: "The benefit of incontestability under Section 15 of this Act shall not be deemed to affect the jurisdiction of any Government agency to institute proceedings under the antitrust laws of the United States against the owner of such registered mark in any case in which the mark is alleged to have been used or is being used as an instrumentality of a violation of the antitrust laws." 271

III. ANTITRUST POLICY IN DISTRIBUTION 

Connecting the producer with the ultimate consumer, distributive services guide the traffic from plant to market. Capital invested in fabrication, storage, transportation, financing, and sales promotion, for example, transforms an industry's output into finished consumers' goods. Traditionally, each function was performed by an independent business firm which specialized in one form of distributive service and dealt only with others equally specialized. Manufacturers typically utilized brokers as middlemen, or sold through jobbers to wholesalers who sold directly to the consuming public. In time, however, competing channels of distribution emerged. Retailors cooperative buying agencies, chain and department stores, and mail-order houses acquired additional facilities so as to combine successive stages of distribution. And successful retailers frequently plowed back their earnings to buy plant and equipment to enable them to deal directly with manufacturers and, at the same time, to earn a profit from reselling to other retailers as well. To the extent these rival channels compete, consumers benefit, not alone from competition among manufacturers, but also from the gains of modernized distribution.

The distribution process is the target of the Clayton Act's specific antitrust provisions. Supplementing the Sherman Act's general bans on monopolies and restraints of trade, Congress in 1914 expressly outlawed certain practices deemed steps in achieving those monopolistic ends proscribed by the Sherman Act, and created the Federal Trade Commis- 272

sion a $ 

Ge.. AV ་སཡ preser of the sive stant new 28287 deng the 73

sion as an auxiliary expert organ of antitrust enforcement. Directed to preserve free competition in the distribution process, Sections 2 and 3 of the Clayton Act prohibited predatory price-cutting as well as exclusive and "tying" arrangements whenever they tended to monopoly or substantial lessening of competition in any line of commerce. 

The general business collapse of the thirties, however, inspired a new trend of legislation, this time directed at mitigating the business hazards of aggressive competition during a period of declining consumer demand. These drives for antitrust "rationalization" culminated in an extensive legislative program succeeding the short-lived NRA "codes of fair competition." In addition, the Miller-Tydings Act, exempting resale-price fixing of branded items from antitrust prohibitions, and the Robinson-Patman amendments, reorienting the Clayton Act's price discrimination bans, recast the legal framework within which distribution evolved.

The imprecise impact of these enactments has produced questions of statutory interpretation. For example, the Congressional dispensation for "Fair Trade" nevertheless withheld sanction from "horizontal" agreements between firms in competition or operating at the same level of distribution--leaving unresolved issues in the classification of firms which span several distributive functions. In addition, the Fair 

Trade exemption extended only to commodities "in free and open competi-

And nearly two decades after enactment of a supplementary criminal ban on some price discriminations, courts still confess serious doubts as to whether the prohibition is sufficiently comprehensible to meet the Constitution's guarantees of fair play and adequate notice.3/ other tribunals disagree on whether this section is really a part of the "antitrust laws" which authorize parties injured by violations to seek private treble damage redress.4/

At the same time, statutes woven from such diverse historical fabric soon revealed incongruities. On the one hand, courts have viewed the 

Clayton Act's specific prohibitions as auxiliary to the Sherman Act, designed to abort anti-competitive business practices which would develop into substantial restraints. Consequently, the legal issues here concern largely matters of degree: how far back the law will reach

Yet the judicial process has evolved one fundamental accommodation to protect competitive distribution--resolution of every statutory doubt in favor of the Sherman Act's basic antitrust directives. In the Automatic Canteen decision of 1953, the Court acknowledged the danger that strict enforcement of the Robinson-Patman Act might foster a "price uniformity and rigidity in open conflict with the purposes of other antitrust legislation." The Court then stated a basic formula to encourage rivalry in distribution. "In the light of congressional policy as expressed in other antitrust legislation," the Court asserted a "duty to reconcile [interpretations of the Robinson-Patman Act] with the broader antitrust policies that have been laid down by Congress. 11/ We agree that the "heart of our national economic policy has long been faith in the value of competition."12/ For the better part of a century the Sherman Act has moulded our industrial organization and permeated our political and economic thought. Sherman Act policies. The Federal Trade Commission through its processes of interpretation and administration can help reconcile distribution controls with "broader antitrust policies." demonstrated a renewed intention to justify its role as an expert tribunal in which Congress has confided special responsibilities. 

Statutory provisions affecting distribution should, therefore, be applied to reflect not only reasonable clarity and internal consistency but, above all, to remove market barriers to efficient distribution and thus serve basic antitrust objectives. 

Distribution involves business transactions among buyers and sellers to which a variety of antitrust consequences may attach. At the outset, a businessman must determine whether he will deal at all with particular buyers or customer classes. Such refusals to deal, while not in terms controlled by specific statutory prohibitions, may nevertheless encounter a range of implicit antitrust commands. Once the goods enter the stream of distribution, the antitrust focus shifts 277

to the terms of the bargain between the negotiating parties, Governed by express antitrust provisions defining their permissible scope, these terms may involve exclusive arrangements, control by the seller of the buyer's resale price, and direct or indirect price discriminations among various customers. All these problems we consider. 

REFUSALS TO DEAL 

Unlike the actual terms in a sale of goods, refusals to deal are not expressly governed by any particular antitrust provision. Instead, the validity of any refusal to deal may depend on the Sherman Act's prohibitions on monopolization and combinations in restraint of trade; the Clayton Act's controls on price discriminations and exclusive arrangements; as well as the general ban on "unfair methods of competition" codified in Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act. 

Boycotts, like other group action to coerce, have¨ long been condemned as unreasonable restraints under Section 1 of the Sherman Act.

In the Eastern States Retail Lumber Dealers Ass'n case, for example, the Supreme Court invalidated an arrangement by which members of a retailers' association boycotted all suppliers competing with the participants by selling directly to the retail trade.13/ In like fashion, the Supreme Court condemned a multilateral arrangement among a dominant group of motion picture distributors fixing the terms on which any one distributor acting singly would consent to deal with film exhibitors.14/ The Supreme Court had earlier indicated some

In the Court's view, a concerted restraint by a powerful combination was legally unjustifiable even if intended to inhibit only admittedly invidious trade conduct or acknowledged commercial torts. From this and later Supreme Court pronouncements emerges the rule that group action coercing outside parties is deemed an undue restraint of trade and--whatever its purpose--is likely 17/ to fall as unreasonable per se. 16/

Absent conspiracy or combination, refusals to deal by individual businessmen have enjoyed a qualified antitrust dispensation. The free market ideal long presupposed a private merchant could freely develop business relationships with whom he pleased. This principle the Supreme 18/ 

Court proclaimed in the Colgate case. There a Sherman Act criminal indictment charged a seller with employing various commercial tactics designed to coerce his distributors' adherence to a "suggested" resale price. The Supreme Court rejected the indictment as not alleging the element of combination of conspiracy essential to a Sherman Act Section 1 offense, and broadly countenanced the private trader's discretion to select his customers. By analogy, an individual wholesaler's refusal to continue purchases from his supplier--even though designed to halt the flow of supplies to a rival wholesaler--was subsequently exonerated as not constituting an "unfair method of competition" under the Federal 

Trade Commission Act.19/.

But Colgate's blanket sanction for individual discretion in dealing did not long withstand limitations. 

Its holding, the Court quickly determined, did not extend to protect a course of dealing which inferentially spelled out the factor of agreement that Colgate lacked.20/ 

More important, the Court's landmark decision in Federal Trade Commission

v, Beech-Nut Packing Co.21/ inaugurated a broad counterrule which, though acknowledging the abstraction of untrammeled discretion, places any exercise of this right in its business perspective, and then scrutinizes all pertinent antitrust prohibitions the trade pattern suggests. In this way the legal status of refusals to deal derives indirectly from the Sherman, Clayton, and Federal Trade Commission

Acts. 

Viewed in the larger business setting, even individually conceived refusals to deal may violate Section 1 of the Sherman Act. Thus, enforcement of a resale price policy against resourceful price-cutting dealers may invite joint policing efforts between the seller and distributors willing to adhere to his resale price. From this mutual aid in resale-price enforcement may evolve a concerted pattern vitiating the immunity the seller's autonomous refusal to deal with price cutters 22/ otherwise enjoys.

AYT to

Refusals to deal are factually equivocal and legally neutral, relying for significance on their market context. This discerning market analysis of refusals to deal seems essential to reconciling the preservation of individual business discretion with protection from monopolistic practices and undue restraints. Refusals to deal, standing alone, lack significance. For example, they may evince aggressive efforts to develop an enterprise intelligently through cultivating productive while quitting barren business channels. Reluctance to acquire additional suppliers or outlets may reflect merely an appropriate accommodation to adverse market conditions through business retrenchment or a halt to aggrandizement of operational scope. And failure to consummate trade relations may certainly inhere in unfavorable past experience, unsatisfactory credit record, or even from a clash of incompatible personalities. Yet it is equally undeniable that a refusal to deal may easily lend itself to abuse as an instrument of monopoly power, and may symptomize a broader course of conduct detrimental to competition. Hence we believe that only scrutiny of surrounding business context can characterize a refusal as a legitimate exercise of individual business judgment, or a restrictive practice incompatible with antitrust objectives.

We subscribe, however, to vigorous condemnation of conspiratórial refusals to deal. In our view, individual freedom to trade does not comprehend license to combine with others for coercing outsiders' 

31 (cont.) Service Co., 344 U.S. 392, 395 (1953); see Times-Picayune 

Publishing Co. v. United States, 345 U.S. 594, 609 (1953). 285

busine establ or sup of the condu in wh ions 

CUT 

ual' That

Cla

business policies or patterns of commerce. We therefore approve the established legal doctrines which condemn trade boycotts of customers or suppliers as routine unreasonable restraints forbidden by Section 1 of the Sherman Act.

In sum, we consider a refusal to deal an instance of commercial conduct that relies for antitrust significance on the course of business in which it plays part. Although not ruled by express antitrust provisions, refusals to deal are today subject to legal doctrines which, in our view, achieve an appropriate balance between preserving the individual's freedom to deal and the protection from undue trade restraints. 

That being so, the Committee recommends no revision of the Sherman,

Clayton, or Federal Trade Commission Acts designed to modify their impact on the antitrust validity of refusals to deal. 286

EXCLUSIVE DEALING 

Congress sought to embrace common business practices within Section 3.32/ 

The typical "tying" arrangement conditioned the sale or lease of a machine on its use only with supplies or services furnished by the seller or lessor.33/ "Full-line forcing," a variant of the customary tying agreement, bound the distributor of one item to accept added products in the seller's line. In both types of "tying" arrangements, however, the supplier of the particular commodity which the purchaser desired foisted on him a further product that he might not wish to buy at all. Outright exclusive dealing arrangements, on the other hand, may concern the terms of sale for a single product. a condition of doing business, a seller might demand that 32/ Section 3 in relevant part forbids any person "to lease or make a sale or contract for sale of goods, wares, merchandise, machinery, supplies, or other commodities, whether patented or unpatented...or fix a price charged therefor, or discount from, or rebate upon, such price, on the condition, agreement, or understanding that the lessee or purchaser thereof shall not use or deal in the goods, wares, merchandise, machinery, supplies, or other commodity of a competitor or competitors of the lessor or seller, where the effect of such lease, sale, or contract for sale or such condition, agreement, or understanding may be to substantially lessen competition or tend to create a monopoly in any line of commerce. 11 

33/ See e.g., 51 Cong, Rec. 9061, 9090, 9410, 14,093, 14,200; 

Sen. Rep. No. 698, 634 Cong., 2d Sess, 8 (1914). 287

".. 59. 123,

his customers shun all rivals' merchandise.34/ In addition, identical consequences might temporarily inhere in a supplier's insistence on a term requirements contract that obligated the buyer to fill all his needs from the single source for a specified time. But the element common to all practices reached by the statute was exclusion of rival sellers either by the terms of the arrangement or its necessary effects. 

The Supreme Court has consistently applied Section 3 

to invalidate restrictive "tying" arrangements. Exclusionary leasing practices, previously upheld under the Sherman Act, readily fell before the Clayton Act's specific prohibitions. 

In United Shoe Machinery Co. v. United States, "tying" clauses requiring lessees of machinery to utilize only the lessor's supplies and services were condemned under Section 3 as obviously excluding any purchases from competitors; the requisite probability of adverse effect on competition or tendency to monopoly was inferred from United's "dominant position in supplying shoe machinery of the classes involved. 1135/

United Shoe Machinery Co., 247 U.S. 32 (1918). The Supreme Court in Federal Trade Commission v. Gratz, 253 U.S. 421 (1920), had exonerated from Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act a seller charged with conditioning his sale of steel ties on purchases of jute bagging; that case, however, turned entirely on a point of pleading. See Times-Picayune Publishing Co. v. 

United States, 345 U.S. 594, 606 n. 21 (1953). United's leasing arrangements recently played a significant part in decisions adjudging a broader monopolization offense under Section 2 of the Sherman Act. 110 F. Supp. 295 (D. Mass. 1953), aff'd per curiam, 347 U.S. 521 (1954), 288

The strict application of Section 3 to "tying" arrangements culminated in the 1947 International Salt case.39/ The Supreme

Court affirmed a summary judgment invalidating under Section 3 

of the Clayton Act machinery leases conditioned on exclusive utilization of the as well as Section 1 of the Sherman Act 

lessor's supplies. Without investigation of the lessor's market "dominance" which was the crux of illegality in the Shoe Machinery decision, the International Salt decision condemned the tying arrangement under Section 3 as embracing a not "insignificant or insubstantial" volume of commerce in the "tied" And because for even a lawful patent monopolist it was "unreasonable, per se to foreclose competitors from any substantial market, supplies.40/ 11 the tying arrangement could equally not survive scrutiny under Section 1 of the Sherman Act. 41/

As recently summarized by the Supreme Court in the Times- Picayune opinion, the Court's course of adjudications in "tying" cases discloses a "perceptible pattern of illegality: ་་ When- ever the supplier "enjoys a monopolistic position in the market for the tying product, or if a substantial volume of commerce in the 'tied' product is restrained, a tying agreement violates

Exclusive dealership and requirements contracts, however, have engendered more complex and less settled issues. In 

Standard Fashion Co. v. Magrane Houston Co., the Supreme 

Court's first interpretation of Section 3 invalidated a system of distribution by which dress patterns were sold only to retailers which obligated themselves not to handle the patterns of rivals.43/ The system, binding 40% of pattern agencies in the United States to a single supplier, was viewed as imparing competition and tending to monopoly essentially because it shut out competitors from certain prime retail outlets. That principle underlay the later 

Fashion Originators Guild decision which deemed Section 3 

as well as the Federal Trade Commission Act violated by concerted arrangements enjoining buyers of textiles and garments from dealing in any merchandise copied from Guild members' designs.44/

The legal status of exclusive arrangements was highlighted by Standard Oil of California, or Standard Stations

At issue were contracts obligating service stations to take full gasoline requirements as well 

and in some cases accessories from Standard. The contracts covered 16% of the existing retail gasoline outlets in the "Western area," where Standard, the largest marketer in that region, accounted for 23% of total gallonage. The District Court, conceding that 

Standard enjoyed no "dominant" position, invalidated the arrangements under Section 3 as well as under Section 1 of the Sherman Act by presuming the requisite competitive 

effects simply from the contracts' coverage of "a substantial number of outlets and a substantial amount of products whether 

In the court's view, considered comparatively or not. 1,46/ the "quantitative substantiality" of trade flowing through exclusive marketing channels by itself signaled the "substantial lessening of competition" which the Clayton Act sought to prevent.

The District Court was affirmed in a five to four decision by the Supreme Court, in a perplexing opinion whose rationale is not clear. As framed by the Court, the legal issue was whether the statutory prerequisite of potential competitive injury might be "met simply by proof that a substantial portion of commerce is affected or whether it 

46 See id. at 298. 292

-- mua act to th 8

must also be demonstrated that competitive activity has actually diminished or probably will diminish."47/ Limited to these alternatives, the Court had no difficulty in choosing the narrower test. Since alternative exclusionary marketing devices, such as supplier-operated service stations or agency arrangements, might legally supplant the existing distribution system, any firm prediction of its restrictive effects presupposed a comparison among a host of economic imponderables that might well exceed the competence of an adjudicatory tribunal. Moreover, exclusive arrangements were a marketing practice singled out by Congress in the Clayton Act as a special object of antitrust concern. Avoiding such feared speculation, the Court construed the statute to prescribe a simpler, incisive test: If the factor of market dominance could not furnish the requisite inference of adverse market effects, exclusive arrangements nevertheless transgressed Section 3 whenever they "foreclosed competition in a substantial share of the line of commerce affected," since such 48/ "foreclosure" made probable economic harm.

While postulating "foreclosure" of competitors from a significant market as the index of illegality, the Court never analyzed the factual arrangements at bar. Without explaining what "foreclosure" meant, the Court assumed

In sum, from the early Standard Fashion case until the most recent Standard Stations and Times-Picayune decisions, the Supreme Court has defined the ultimate issue under Section 3 as whether the challenged arrangement "would under the circumstances disclosed probably lessen competition or create an actual tendency to monopoly. "56/ That ultimate legal conclusion, the cases suggest, derives from factual analysis according clear recognition that tying arrangements and exclusive agreements are disparate marketing techniques, both in conception and result.

Tying arrangements, or full-line forcing, in the Court's view, generate the requisite probability of economic harm whenever the seller occupies a dominant market position for the "tying" commodity or if his arrangements cover a substantial volume of trade in the "tied" product.57/ Exclusive dealerships or requirements contracts, however, transgress

Section 3 if employed by suppliers dominant in the relevant market, but otherwise only whenever they in fact "foreclose" rivals from access to "substantial share of the line of 58/ commerce affected."- In either event, the requisite effect on competition is presumed to follow once these indicia of illegality emerge. 

56/ 258 U.S. at 357. 

57/ Times-Picayune Publishing Co. v. United States, 345 U.S. 

594, 608 (1953). 

58/ Standard Oil of California v. United States, 337 U.S. 

293, 314 (1949). 297

We think a narrow inquiry for testing the validity of tying arrangements is fully compatible with business facts. 

As forcefully emphasized by the Supreme Court in the Standard Stations and Times-Picayune opinions, tying arrangements tion. 'serve hardly any purpose beyond the suppression of competi- „59/ Ordinarily, the manufacturer goodwill is adequately safeguarded by close specification of the supplies his product requires. Thus, obviated is the need for a "tying" contract automatically disqualifying all his competitors' goods.

Apart from goodwill, tying arrangements sole apparent rationale is to exploit a power position in the market for the "tying" commodity by artificially extending the market for the "tied" product. We agree with the Court's observation that "in the usual case only the prospect of reducing competition would persuade a seller to adopt such a contract and only his control of the supply of the tied device, whether conferred by patent monopoly or otherwise obtained, could induce a buyer to enter one. By coercing the buyer's freedom of 1,60/ choice, a tying arrangement prima facie "forecloses" competitors in a separate market from offering up their goods to a free competitive judgment and, as a result, almost invariably threatens competition to a degree warranting Clayton Act condemnation.61/ 

59 337 U.S. at 305; 345 U.S. at 605. 

60/ 337 U.S. at 306; 345 U.S. at 605. 

61/ 345 U.S. at 606. From this "tying-clause, analysis, Edward R. 

Johnston dissents in his words, "the existence of a dominant market 298

Amor

Requirements contracts and exclusive dealerships, however, present more complex market problems. Unlike tying arrangements, exclusive arrangements may in fact promote vigorous competition, and do not signal substantial market power in the seller. Requirements contracts may, indeed, be preferred by customers as assuring a steady source of supply, affording protection against price fluctuations, and consequently 61 (cont'd) for the tying' product is essential. This dominance may result from monopoly, patent or otherwise, or from market acceptance of the commodity, but to say that the tying together of two products, both of which may be purchased freely in the open market, is a violation of Section 3 if the tied product happens to represent a substantial volume of trade is to adopt the "quantitative substantiality" test. The reasoning in the Times-Picayune case, albeit by way of dicta, makes it clear that the existence of a dominant product is an essential requisite of a Section 3 tying case. While most of the Section 3 tying cases have involved a dominant market product, I have in mind a pending case where the Department is relying on Section 3 and where no market dominance whatever exists as to either of the products which are tied together by the sales contracts. In short, I do not think the sharp distinction exists between a tying case and an exclusive dealing case, so far as the doctrine of "quantitative substantiality" is concerned, which the Group makes in its report.' 299

---

L facilitating long-term business plans.62/ For the seller, an exclusive arrangement may reduce selling and promotional expenses, as well as permit evenly balanced and consequently more economic production. Above all, exclusive arrangements can fortify the market position of weaker competitors against more powerfully entrenched rivals, and enable new entrants to gain a foothold by assuring a set volume of business for a crucial starting period.

Exclusive arrangements, on the other hand, may seriously clog competition whenever a seller through preempting of access to consuming markets unduly constricts his rivals' opportunities to compete, the potential impairment of the competitive process or tendency to monopoly readily appears. 

Central to inquiry is whether a system of challenged exclusive arrangements in fact "forecloses" competitors from a substantial market. It is, of course, elementary that every bilateral contract for the sale of goods correspondingly prevents third parties from getting that business, and to this extent excludes a rival's trade. Hence, an exclusive arrangement may be no more restrictive than an ordinary mercantile agreement; a readily cancellable short-term requirements contract, for example, may leave greater opportunities to rivals than an absolute sale of a large quantity 

62/ 337 U.S. at 306-307. 300

"'. 

---

which would fill the buyer's needs for a longer time. Any legal significance of an exclusive dealing arrangement, we think, springs from terms which so tightly hem in actual or potential rivals that antitrust policy must interfere. 

A series of exclusive arrangements exacted by the dominant supplier with the relevant product's ultimate consumers, necessarily confines competitors to an intolerable degree and hence creates a probability of economic harm sufficient to bring the Act into play.63/ In all other cases, however, coverage and duration 64/ of the challenged agreement must... be measured against the pattern and practices of a particular industry to determine whether competitors are genuinely "foreclosed" from access to the market, That investigation might well reveal that an exclusive arrangement preempting wholesalers or intermediate distributors rather than consumers, 65/ for example, need not "foreclose"

The inquiry goes beyond a "quantitative substantiality test" but stops short of the full-scale analysis appropriate for measuring a trade practice against the Sherman Act's

Rule of Reason. Any "quantitative substantiality" rule virtually condemns exclusive dealing arrangements to per se illegality, in disregard of significant economic and business facts. The criterion of "actual foreclosure" we derive from the Supreme Court's Standard Stations opinion, however, is incompatible with a mechanical application of the law to practices not inherently detrimental to competition. In our view, the mere coverage of a substantial volume of commerce by exclusive dealing arrangements is not tantamount to "foreclosure" of rivals from access to a substantial market, so that some analysis of particular distributive patterns is essential to any determination of actual foreclosure.

However, foreclosure even short of tangible damage to business rivals must satisfy the prerequisite of injury established by Section 3. Congress designed the Clayton 

Act to abort business practices with a dangerous potential of economic harm. That law was clearly intended to strike before real injury was done. Nor should an exclusive agreement shown to foreclose competition escape invalidation by broader explanations or harmless intent. We understand 

Section 3 as an express condemnation of business practices 303

7 17 de 3. A. 29cube va ch 202

demonstrably detrimental to competition regardless of countervailing considerations. Thus, while a business practice challenged as an unreasonable restraint under the Sherman

Act might be absolved upon a showing of reasonable business purpose adapted to cope with peculiar economic conditions, 70/ or for want of adequate proof of accomplished harm,71/ ex- clusive arrangements were marked for illegality under the Clayton Act once the test of potential detriment to competition is met.

The standard of "actual foreclosure", moreover, is well adapted to implementation in judicial as well as administrative proceedings. We do not understand Standard Stations to suggest that Congress envisaged the parallel enforcement of the statute by the Department of Justice and the Federal Trade Commission to rely on disparate legal principles, depending solely on whether the initial adjudication is rendered by a judicial or an administrative tribunal. We agree, rather, that the "actual foreclosure" criterion effectively implements the Clayton Act which "must have contemplated standards of proof capable of administration by the courts as well as by the Federal Trade Commission; "72/ 

70/ Appalachian Coals, Inc. v. United States, 288 U.S. 344 (1933); Chicago Board of Trade v. United States, 246 U.S. 

231 (1918). 

71/ E.g., Times-Picayune Publishing Co. v. United States, 345

and we believe that the Commission's subsequent applications of Section 3 agree. 

73/ The identical indicia of illegality should govern, moreover, whenever the Commission chooses to pursue exclusive arrangements as "unfair methods of competition" 

forbidden by Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act. 

The essence of "unfairness" in an exclusive arrangement as a marketing tactic is the actual foreclosure of business rivals from consuming markets, thereby stymying their opportunity to compete on even terms. Hence we consider the Congressional mandate in the Clayton Act best executed by according to exclusive arrangements a uniform method of market analysis, whether adjudicated by courts or the Commission, under the Clayton or the Federal Trade Commission Act.74/ 

73/ We do not read the Federal Trade Commission's opinion in the Maico case, F.T.C. Dkt. 5822 (Dec. 7, 1953), as launching that agency into economic investigations beyond the inquiry' authorized by the governing interpretations of Section 3 that bind courts and the Commission alike. Chaotic consequences might attend the anomaly of subjecting identical trade practices to different legal principles due to fortuitous circumstances determining whether a district court or the Commission sits as the initial arbiter, in either event subject to appellate judicial review. 

74/ E.g., Federal Trade Commission v. Motion Picture Adver tising Services Co., 344 U.S. 392 (1953). Because the technical prerequisites of Section 3, demanding, for example, the presence of a "commodity" and a "sale or lease," might permit some economically equivalent though anti-competitive practices to escape, we consider the Commission legitimately entitled to pursue such activities under its general authorization to proceed against "unfair methods of competition" under Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act. On the other hand, proceedings thus brought under Section 5 ought not, in our opinion, finesse the essential criteria of illegality prescribed by the express prohibitions of the Clayton Act. 205

RESALE-PRICE MAINTENANCE AND "FAIR TRADE" 

The Miller-Tydings Act, passed in 1937 as an amendment to Section 1 of the Sherman Act, exempts from the antitrust laws 'contracts or agreements prescribing minimum prices for the resale of a commodity which bears, or the label or container of which bears, the trade mark, brand, or name of the producer or distributor of such commodity and which is in free and open competition with commodities of the same general class produced or distributed by others, when contracts or agreements of that description are lawful [in the state of resale]."

An express proviso, however, excludes "horizontal" arrangements between "corporations in competition with each other". Not expressly included, moreover, were "non-signer" state provisions which bound all distributors with notice to observe the resale price agreed upon by any one buyer. Because the

Supreme Court in 1951 invalidated the "non-signer" practice as unprotected by the Miller-Tydings amendment, the McGuire amendment, enacted in 1952 as an additional provision of the Federal Trade Commission Act, specified within the exemption resale-price maintenance effective against all distributors signers and "non-signers" as well. 

Before Miller-Tydings, agreements by distributors to maintain a resale price were consistently struck down, Beginning with the Dr. Miles decision in 1911, the Court 306

held that "vertical" resale-price agreements by a seller with several distributors actually restrain competition among the dealers, and extinguish price competition on that level as effectively as any "horizontal" price-fixing agreement.75/ Attempts to justify such arrangements as indispensable to preservation of the supplier's trade-name or business goodwill have failed. Instead, resale-price setting has been condemned as no less offensive to antitrust policy than any other price-fixing agreement.76/

75/"If there be an advantage to the manufacturer in the maintenance of fixed retail prices, the question remains whether it is one which he is entitled to secure by agreements restricting the freedom of trade on the part of dealers who own what they sell. As to this, the complainant can fare no better with its plan of identical contracts than could the dealers themselves if they formed a combination and endeavored to establish the same restrictions, and thus to achieve the same result, by agreement with each other," Dr. Miles Medical 

Co. v. John D. Park & Sons Co., 220 U.S. 373, 407-408 (1911). 

76/ E.g., Dr. Miles Medical Co. v. John D. Park & Sons Co., 220 U.S. 373 (1911); United States v. A. Schrader's Son, Inc. 

252 U.S. 85 (1920); Frey & Son, Inc. v. Cudahy Packing Co., 

256 U.S. 208 (1921); Schwegmann Bros. v. Calvert Distillers 

Corp., 341 U.S. 384 (1951); cf. Federal Trade Commission v. 

Beech-Nut Packing Co., 257 U.S. 441 (1922). "It is clear from our decisions under the Sherman Act....that this interstate marketing arrangement would be illegal, that it would be enjoined, that it would draw civil and criminal penalties, and that no court would enforce it. Fixing minimum prices, like other types of price-fixing, is illegal per se. 

Schwegmann Bros. v. Calvert Distillers Corp., 341 U.S. at 386. 11 307

* Antrutero bar tik net 1 י. 3

Since the depression of the thirties, however, numerous states have enacted legislation legalizing local resale-price setting agreements. These "Fair Trade" statutes typically authorized suppliers of trade-marked or branded commodities sold in "free and open" competition with merchandise of the same "general class" to bind buyers not to resell below a minimum resale price. Since the early "Fair Trade" statutes reached only contracting parties, price cutting by "non-signers" often thwarted the supplier's price stabilization effort. 

Later and improved laws therefore bound all distributors not to undercut knowingly the "Fair Trade" price set in a contract with any dealer. These "non-signer" clauses, today part of every operative state enactment, are crucial for enforcing adherence to "Fair Trade" prices. Under the typical "Fair 

Trade" statute, any price-cutting distributor becomes subject to injunctive or damage proceedings at the instance of the supplier, "cooperating" dealers or "any person damaged" by the departure from the "Fair Trade" price, State "Fair Trade" pricing subsequently received express federal sanction. The constitutionality of the state enactments, as well as their "non-signer" provisions, was sustained by the Supreme Court in 1936. In the Old Dearborn case, the Supreme 

Ccurt viewed the Illinois "Fair Trade" law as designed solely "to afford a legitimate remedy for an injury to the good will 308

by

"} which results from the use of trade-marks, brands or names by price cutters on the retail level, and hence not transgressing the "due process" and "equal protection" clauses of the Fourteenth Amendment. Rather, the Court viewed "Fair 

Trade" legislation effective against signatories and informed dealers alike as an appropriate exercise of the state's police powers for safeguarding the supplier's proprietary interests from impairment through price cutting and "loss-leader" selling. One year later, Congress passed the Miller-Tydings amendment as an express exemption from the Sherman Act, thereby legitimizing any "Fair Trading" authorized by the state in which resale took place. By virtue of the Old Dearbor case and an Act of Congress, state authorized resale price maintenance was for the time at least, immunized from Constitutional attack as well as federal antitrust prohibitions. 

The Supreme Court's Schwegmann decision in 1951, however, once again jeopardized effective "Fair Trade". There, the 

Court construed the Miller-Tydings exemption as shielding from the federal antitrust laws only consensual "contracts or agreements", and placed enforcement against "non-signing" distributors beyond the pale.18/ .78 Since the federal exemption 

17 Old Dearborn Distributing Co. v. Seagram Distillers Corp., 299 U.S. 183, 198 (1936). 

78/ Schwegmann Bros. v. Calvert Distillers Corp., 341 U.S. 

384 (1951). 309

could not protect "non-signer" enforcement, effective "Fair

Trade" pricing was again exposed to the statutory prohibitions on restraints of trade. And the Supreme Court's opinion reaffirmed its historic position that, unless expressly 

exempted by Act of Congress, all maintenance or fixing of resale prices whether consensual by agreement or coercive through "non-signer" controls

is per se illegal as inherently unreasonable. To repair this damage, the McGuire Act in 1952 expressly added "non-signer" enforcement to the federal antitrust dispensation Miller-Tydings had earlier conferred.19/ 

As a result of local enabling and federal exemptive legislation, resale-price fixing, otherwise a clear antitrust violation, is today lawful in most American states. While some state courts have invalidated the "Fair Trade" statutes as defective under state constitutions, 80/ numerous state 79 In addition, the McGuire Act expressly authorized 'stipulated" as well as "minimum" resale prices, and further exempted "Fair Trade" arrangements binding buyers in turn to obtain "Fair Trade" agreements from persons to whom they resold. In fact, the statutory recognition of "Fair Trade" itself remains highly controversial. On the one hand, protection of "Fair Trade" pricing through state and federal enactments stands vigorously backed by powerful organized retailers' 85/ and manufacturers' groups. On the other hand, the Department of Justice and the Federal Trade Commission in numerous official policy pronouncements have registered adamant opposition to "Fair Trade" as incompatible with the Nation's antitrust policy of fostering free competition. 86/

"Fair Trade" enactments may reflect some legitimate commercial aims. Nationally advertised and branded consumer commodities readily lend themselves to loss-leader and cutrate merchandising that can impair substantial investments in business goodwill. Such marketing tactics may alienate established distribution channels whose appeal to consumers emphasizes attractions other than price reductions. "Fair 

Trade" pricing enables manufacturers and other brand or trade mark owners to invoke prompt legal sanctions to check unwelcome promotional selling, thereby protecting "quality" items from debasement in the consumer's eye. In fact the objective of preserving valuable investments in trade-marks and business goodwill from destructive marketing tactics underlay Old Dearborn's upholding of state "Fair Trade" enactments.

The Committee, however, does not consider "Fair Trade" pricing an appropriate instrumentality for such protection. 

Since state enactments vest absolute discretion in suppliers for determining the level of a "Fair Trade" price, the legislative price-setting authorization extends far beyond the essential guarantees of "loss-leader" control, An effective "Fair Trade" system, moreover, strikes not only at promotional price cutting, but at all price reductions which pass to the consumer the economies of competitive distribution. 313

In no event can the Committee condone another consequence of "Fair Trade" pricing. Apart from manufacturers' interest in preserving trade symbols and commercial goodwill, "Fair 

Trade" pricing may enable distributors to extinguish price competition. A distributor whose inventory consists largely of "Fair Traded" products can carry on business freed from the pressures and tribulations of price competition. Accordingly, distributors in some segments of the economy concertedly espouse "Fair Trade" pricing by suppliers as a legal means for obtaining the benefits of a horizontal price-fixing arrangement 87/ that the antitrust law forbids

It is the Committee's view that "Fair Trading" used as a device for relieving distributors from the rigors of price competition is at odds with the most elementary principles of a dynamic free enterprise system. 

On balance, we regard the federal statutory exemption of "Fair Trade" pricing as an unwarranted compromise of the basic tenets of National antitrust policy. We recognize that the legislatures of forty-five states have at some time accorded official sanction to "Fair Trade" pricing; that the Congress twice deferred to state enactments by creating federal "Fair Trade" exemptions from antitrust prohibitions; and that without federal immunization "Fair Trade" pricing, as a practical 

87 United States v. Frankfort Distilleries, 324 U.S. 293 (1945); cf. Kiefer-Stewart v. Seagram & Sons, 340 U.S. 211 (1951); Spencer v. Sun Oil Co., 94 F. Supp. 408 (D. Conn. 

1950); United States v. National Wholesale Druggists Association, Crim No. 618-C (D.N.J. Jan. 4, 1946); United States v. National Association of Retail Druggists, Crim. No. 683-C 

(D.N.J. Jan. 29, 1947). 314

matter, cannot survive. Nevertheless, the throttling of price competition in the process of distribution that attends "Fair Trade" pricing is, in our opinion, a deplorable but inevitable concomitant of federal exemptive laws. Moreover, whatever the underlying legislative intent, any operative "Fair Trade" authorization cloaks price-fixing efforts on the manufacturing and each succeeding distributive level. And the prominent existence of a federal price-fixing exemption not only symbolizes a radical departure from National antitrust policy without commensurate gains, but extends an invitation for further encroachment on the free-market philosophy that the antitrust laws subserve. 

We therefore recommend Congressional repeal both of the Miller-Tydings amendment to the Sherman Act and the

McGuire amendment to the Federal Trade Commission Act, thereby subjecting resale-price maintenance, as other price-fixing practices, to those federal antitrust controls 315

which safeguard the public by keeping the channels of distribution free.88/ 88 From this recommendation for repeal, Walter Adams dissents. As he puts it "In an effectively competitive economy devoid of significant and powerful monopoly segments resale price maintenance laws would probably be superfluous. But, in an economy where pervasive oligopoly markets confer upon some firms the power to discriminate in price and other terms of sale, the independent businessman needs protection against the discriminatory favoritism which gives the large retailer competitive advantages unrelated to superior efficiency. 

In such an economy it is essential that the basic antitrust laws be supplemented by prohibitions on certain anti-competitive and unfair types of price discrimination. "In this connectinn, two observations are in order. 

First, every agency system and every consignment sale involve retail price determination by the manufacturer. 

Yet neither the agency or consignment system has come under attack (although it is true that some forms thereof have been successfully prosecuted under the antitrust laws). These forms of "fair trading" would persist even if the Miller-Tydings Act and the McGuire Act were repealed. 

Second, the Miller-Tydings exemption permits resale price maintenance only where the goods covered thereby are in free and open competition with goods of the same general class. 

Here is the quantitatively crucial problem from the consumer's point of view: if there is effective competition between manufacturers, the fact that each of them sees fit to fix minimum resale prices is as Justice Brandeis once observed of secondary importance. Within the context of economic reality, I agree with Justice Brandeis that it is more important to prevent horizontal price fixing (tacit or otherwise) on the manufacturing level than to squander meager antitrust appropriations on petty vertical restraints."

PRICE DISCRIMINATION 

The antitrust consequences of a business marked by discrimination in price or terms of sale are primarily governed by Section 2 of the amended Clayton Act. Section 2 of the original Clayton Act was directed at geographical price discrimination destructive of competition among sellers.89/ The emergence of chain stores and mail-order houses in the twenties, however, was feared to jeopardize the survival of independent wholesalers and retailers, and shifted the market significance of discriminatory practices to the devitalization of competition among buyers. A Federal Trade Commission investigation, instituted to analyze the causal market forces in the "independent's" decline and to propose appropriate legal remedies, revealed discriminatory concessions from suppliers as partly responsible for the mass distributor's competitive advantage, and deemed Section 2 of the Clayton Act, as then interpreted in judicial decisions, inadequate to restore a competitive balance. 90/ the Federal Trade Commission's recommendations for legislative revision 91/ The Robinson-Patman amendments of 1936--actually exceeding superimposed more stringent prohibitions on the existing framework of Section 2 of the Clayton Act, expressly enumerated conspicuous types of 

9 H.R. Rep. No. 627, 63d Cong., 2d Sess. 8-9; id. Pt. 2, at 2-3; 

S. Rep. No. 698, 63d Cong., 28 Sess. 43-44 (1914). 

90/ F.T.C., Final Report on the Chain Store Investigation 57-58, 85, 96-97 (1934). 91/ The Commission had merely recommended revision of the Clayton Act so as to declare it illegal "to discriminate unfairly or unjustly between different purchasers... Id. at 96-97. 11 317

: اقدار AMAMamb secre which Patm unde not the the in

secret concessions, and penalized the discriminatory discounts on which mass buyers were suspected to thrive. The design of the Robinson- 

Patman amendments was to facilitate proof of the substantive violation, with statutory exceptions justifying price differentials challenged under the Act.

Section 2(a) of the Clayton Act now prescribes that a seller may not charge different prices for goods of "like grade and quality" if the discrimination causes certain deletorious market effects, unless the practice is "justified" by one of several exculpatory provisos in 2(a) and 2(b). Sections 2(c), (d) and (e) define and ban certain specified ancillary discriminatory practices. Section 2(f) forbids receipt by buyers of the discriminatory concessions forbidden elsewhere in the Act. Aside from these provisions in Section 2, a supplementary criminal prohibition vaguely duplicates and partly overlaps these civil sanctions. Finally, functional discounts and "delivered" price quotations are common pricing practices that pose significant legal issues which derive from the interplay of express and implicit provisions in the Act.

THE PREREQUISITE OF "GOODS OF LIKE GRADE AND QUALITY" 

Section 2(a) confines the statute to discriminations "between different purchasers of commodities of like grade and quality. "like grade and quality" concept originated in Section 2 of the unamended: Clayton, Act That legislation prohibited price discrimination The with adverse competitive effect, but excepted differentials "on account 318

of dis 1. ข่า "grad the selle 

D 20 rest ver any 1

of differences in the grade, quality, or quantity of the commodity sold," In the course of the Robinson-Patman revisions of 1936, the "grade and quality" criterion was shifted into the language defining the offense proscribed. Consequently, while under old Clayton 2 a seller might defend by pointing to significant variations in the grade or quality of his merchandise sold at differential prices, under the restructured Act the legal prohibitions simply could not come into play until a plaintiff demonstrated that goods of "like grade and quality" were involved in the allegedly discriminatory transaction. Furthermore, any legally cognizable difference in either "grade" or "quality" immediately ousts the Act, and hence need not reflect itself in a price differential proportionate to the qualitative disparity. 

Few adjudications have interpreted "like grade and quality". 

Courts have held, however, that a seller may not be charged with price discrimination for quoting a combination price for joint purchases of two items lower than the total price for both if seperately bought.92/ 

Also, commodities made of cheaper materials or ingredients have not been deemed of "like grade and quality" with standard items, thus permitting a seller to quote substantially lower prices to some buyers without risk of illegality.23 on the other hand, nominal physical differences in the appearance of some items not affecting their 927 93/

Perhaps more significant, however, are several decisions by the Federal Trade Commission which disregard brand names and labels as determinants of "grade and quality", and thereby expose to attack price variations in a seller's product line though some goods are promoted under individual wrappings or slogans aimed at a distinctive market or purchaser class.95/

The primary function of the "like grade and quality" criterion is to reasonably confine the price discrimination statute to comparable private business transactions. 96/ To be sure, the legislative history of the Robinson-Patman enactment discloses little of the draftsmen's aims in this regard; nor had the administration of the pre-Robinson- 

Patman Clayton Act developed a revealing gloss on the legal text.

It is, of course, clear that the "like grade and quality" concept is relevant solely to compare two or more items sold by one seller to his several customers, and not to measure the similarity of his goods with those of a competitor. E.g., E.B. Muller & Co. v. Federal
