into play only in reasonably equivalent business transactions involving the sale of nearly identical goods. The "like grade and quality" concept, we think, was designed to serve as a rough guide for separating out those commercial transactions insufficiently comparable for price regulation by the statute.

The sparse judicial interpretations of the "like grade and quality" provision, we think, reasonably subserve this end, and hence do not suggest legislation revision. Actual, genuine, physical differentiations tailored to an individual product buyer's use, and not merely decorative or fanciful, probably remove a case from the reach of the Robinson- Fatman Act.97/ To that extent, we believe, the decisions take realistic account of the limitations which must qualify the scope of the statute." 

On the other hand, business actualities contradict the Commission's past policy of wholly ignoring brands and trade names in gauging "goods of like grade and quality" under the Act. Heavy national advertising and sales promotion have cultivated significant consumer preferences in a brand-conscious American public. Thus, commodities containing precisely identical ingredients but packaged under a distinctive mark or label may not seem alike to the consumer at all. Consequently, nationally advertised "premium" goods are "competitive" with unknown 96 (cont.) However, a comparison with a rival's goods may bear on the validity of the seller's justification of a discriminatory price reduction as meeting a rival's price offer, via the "meeting competition" defense expressly authorized in Section 2(b) of the Act.

entities or "economy" brands only at a significant margin in price.

A price discrimination law can consider heavily advertised and anonymous or private-brand merchandise on an equal legal footing only at a serious distortion of economic facts. 

Since the Commission's interpretations of "like grade and quality" have not yet been approved by reviewing courts--and hence may be reconsidered by the Commission itself--the statutory "like grade and quality" concept presently demands no legislative reform. However, we doubt the wisdom of ignoring readily provable customer preferences for branded goods in any determination of "like grade and quality". It may well be that total abandonment of a physical test in favor of a prerequisite of economic identity might permit facile evasion of the statute by insignificant variations in the packaging, advertising, or design of goods marketed at differential prices, Yet we believe that tangible economic differences ought not be entirely disregarded, and should achieve reasonable recognition under a realistic view of the statutory term "grade" as distinct from any purely physical consideration of "quality." Hence we propose a flexible interpretation of the statute's "like grade and quality" concept to take due account of both physical and economic factors, applied with a practical judgment responsive to the realities of the marketplace. Nothing more precise, we think, could do justice to the inherent complexity of the problem. 322

COMPETITIVE "INJURY" AND PROOF OF PRIMA FACIE VIOLATION OF THE PRICE DISCRIMINATION LAW Section 2(a) of the Clayton Act in pertinent part forbids persons 

"to discriminate in price" whenever the effect of the discrimination "may be substantially to lessen competition or tend to create a monopoly in any line of commerce, or to injure, destroy, or prevent competition with any person who either grants or knowingly receives the benefit of such discrimination, or with customers of either of them"--unless the price differential is justified through one of several defensive provisos enumerated in Section 2(a) and 2 (b). crimination In addition, Section 2(b) provides that "upon proof being made, at any hearing on a complaint under this section, that there has been disthe burden of rebutting the prima facie case thus made by showing justification shall be upon the person charged with a violation of this section, and unless justification shall be affirmatively shown, the Commission is authorized to issue an order terminating the discrimination."

The competitive injury criterion in Section 2(a) represents an evident effort by Congress to broaden the prohibitions on price discrimination in Section 2 of the original Clayton Act.. Hostile judicial interpretations soon eviscerated the price discrimination bans of old Section 2. 

The Supreme Court in 1929 did overrule previous contrary decisions in order to extend protection to injured buyers as well as to competition 323

on the seller level.98/ Nevertheless, the Federal Trade Commission's 

Final Report on the Chain Store Investigation, concluding in 1934 that a seemingly uncheckable trend threatened to eliminate "independent" wholesalers and retailers from the competitive scene, deemed Section 2 inadequate to curb the excessive concessions to mass buyers which were viewed as partially responsible for the independent businessman's decline. 

The resulting legislative amendments retained the competitive effects clause of old Section 2, banning discrimination that might "substantially lessen competition or tend to create a monopoly in any line of commerce"--but added a further test reaching discriminations whose effect might be to "injure, destroy, or prevent competition with any person who either grants or knowingly receives the benefit of such discrimination, or with customers of either of them." At the same time, the amendment in Section 2(b) sought to assure that in administrative proceedings the respondent, and not the Commission, had the burden of proving that challenged discriminatory prices were justifiable under one of the defensive provisos in the Act. 

The major controversy surrounding this part of the law arose after nearly a decade of enforcement. In most proceedings involving discriminatory prices challenged under the Act, findings of illegality coincided with demonstrably significant adverse market effects. Above all, the 

Commission in each case undertook to adduce affirmative proof of compet-

But in 1945, a per curiam Court of Appeals opinion in the Moss case overturned law previously settled by another appellate court, and held that the accused seller had the burden of demonstrating that competitive "injury" did not result, once the bare fact of his differential pricing was shown.92/ Rejecting the Seventh Circuit's Staley decision which required the Commission to find adverse effects on competition before its order could be sustained, 100/ the Moss opinion saw Section 2(b) as a device "shifting the burden of proof to anyone who sets two prices."

That departure which presumed adverse competitive effect from the mere fact of a substantial price differential was accepted by the Supreme Court in 1948. A dictum in the Cement Institute case, involving competitive "injury" among competing sellers (i.e. "primary line injury") declared that "Section 2(b) provides that proof of discrimination in price.. makes out a prima facie case of violation."101/ In the Morton Salt case, decided a week later, the Supreme Court referred back to Cement, cited the Moss case with approval, and expressly adopted its "presumptive injury" rule in a case adjudicating "injury" among customers buying 

Some years later at different prices ("secondary line injury"). 102/

Subsequent decisions, however, have refused to accept the "presumption" doctrines déveloped in Moss and Morton Salt. One Court of 

Appeals in a private damage case intimated that the plaintiff in a price discrimination case must demonstrate adverse effects on competition, and that the fact of differential pricing alone could not "make out a prima facie case. #104/ Also, a district court in recently dismissing a multi-faceted antitrust suit refused to infer competitive injury under the Robinson-Patman Act from the bare evidence of price differentials.105/ 

But most prominently, in Minneapolis-Honeywell, the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit observed there was "no need" for a respondent to justify "unless its discriminations do or may tend to injure competition;" this issue presupposed an examination into the market consequences of the challenged pricing practice, since the Act required substantial, not trivial or sporadic, interference with competition to establish violation of its mandate."106/ And in a reinterpretation perhaps foreshadowed by its Standard Oil opinion two years previously, the Supreme Court's Automatic Canteen decision last year "read the

According to the

Commission's present view, and Moss notwithstanding, in a price discrimination case "counsel supporting the complaint has the burden of proof to establish the necessary injury." Since "differences in price without competitive injury are not illegal," "injury" was one essential element of the statutory offense which the Commission's staff must prove in order to establish a prima facie case of violation. 

Furthermore, the Commission's recent General Foods pronouncement applied to "primary-line" and "secondary-line" cases alike. To be sure, a price slash designed to endanger a rival seller's survival presents an economic issue differing from the impact on the customer level of a discriminatory price concession. ination law, the Commission's declared standard for judging the requisite injurious market effect in all cases was "the substantiality of the effects reasonably probable." As charted in the Minneapolis- 

Honeywell decision, moreover, this test presupposed a realistic inquiry into the challenged discrimination's business context and market effects, The "presumptive injury" rule, in our view, proceeds from a funda mentally erroneous interpretation of Section 2(b). As Automatic Canteen recognizes, Congress in the procedural text of Section 2(b) sought to ensure that--once an administrative proceeding disclosed an affirmatively banned discrimination--a cease and desist order would issue unless the charged seller prevailed under one of the several defenses. Section is 

2(b) thus did not dispense with any element of the offenses defined in the express prohibitions of the Act; it merely reaffirmed as to administrative proceedings in price discrimination cases the common law evidentiary rule. This rule in fact obtains in judicial price discrimination proceedings, whether instituted by the Department of Justice or private plaintiffs, where the procedural provisions of 

Section 2(b) cannot in terms apply. In sum, the "prima facię" case in Section 2(b) refers not to any discrimination, but only to discriminations affirmatively banned when causing the adverse market effects described in 2(a), and unlawful unless justified by the seller through one of the several defenses.

We think, on the other hand, that the Commission's recent General Foods decision, in accord with the Court of Appeals' interpretations 328

in Mo 

FLOOM APA SAM 

ref con and le On

WO of 1

in the Staley and Minneapolis-Honeywell cases and their sequels, reflects a sound and accurate reading of the Act. To prevent further confusion arising from the obviously troublesome text of Section 2(b), and to codify the rule of General Foods which expresses the original legislative intent, this Committee recommends clarifying legislation. 

On the one hand, outright deletion of the procedural clause in 2(b) would merely eliminate surplus verbiage; the evidentiary principles of the common law--which the "infelicitous language" of Section 2(b) purports to restate--would then automatically come into play. On the other hand, a clarifying amendment would explicitly attain this end, by revising the procedural text of Section 2(b) to read: 

"Upon proof being made, at any hearing on a complaint under an appropriate subsection of this section, that there has been discrimination affirmatively prohibited..., the burden of justification under an applicable proviso shall be upon the seller charged with a violation of this section. So amended, Section 2(b) would not only reflect accurately both the original framers' intent and the Federal Trade Commission's current view, but also would accord with the interpretations of the law as promulgated by the Supreme Court in Automatic Canteen and the Seventh Circuit in Staley and Minneapolis-Honeywell. At the same time, the recommended revision would reaffirm that the law insists on firm proof and findings of adverse competitive effects as an essential element of the price discrimination offense, and that a demonstration of even sizeable differentials is not by force of law tantamount to a prima facie illegal discrimination. 329

In contrast, the Committee believes that the "competitive injury" terminology of Section 2(a) in its present form can be administered to conform with overall antitrust policy. The statutory text--retained from the original Clayton Act--that forbids only price discriminations responsible for a "substantial lessening of competition" or "tendency to monopoly" readily comports with dominant Sherman Act aims. A realistic interpretaion of the 1936 additions banning discriminations whose effect may be to "injure, destroy, or prevent competition with" grantors or recipients--can, in our opinion, ensure equal compatibility with basic antitrust directives. 

Such interpretations, we think, are signaled by recent adjudica tions. Recent Federal Trade Commission pronouncements in Clayton Act cases indicate an intention to vindicate its expert status by according thorough economic analysis to market data. 110/ Such reinvigoration of function we particularly approve in price discrimination matters, where any per se approach inevitably over-simplifies essentially complex economic issues. At the same time, courts have tended to abandon prior misinterpretations of the Act, in favor of the workable principles of the Minneapolis-Honeywell case. Hence all tribunals are free to construe Section 2(a) in accord with the Sherman Act's policy of free competition, and guided by the Automatic Canteen decision's declaration of a "duty to reconcile" Section 2(a), insofar as the text of the Act

permits, "with the broader antitrust policies that have been laid down by Congress.

Consonant with these policies, this Committee recommends that analysis of the statutory "injury" center on the vigor of competition in the market rather than hardship to individual businessmen. For the essence of competition is a contest for trade among business rivals in which some must gain while others lose, to the ultimate benefit 111 of the consuming public. Incidental hardships on individual businessmen in the normal course of commercial events can be checked by a price discrimination statute only at the serious risk of stifling the competitive process itself. Nor should a competitive price reduction be singled out as responsible for "injury" if alternative means of access to goods at the lower price are available to the buyer, anyway.

Such a view jibes with the text of Section 2(a). We emphasize that it is not "injury" to competitors but adverse effects on "competition with" parties privy to discriminations that the statute expressly 112/ forbids Hence we believe that criteria of competitive effect which narrowly focus on individual competitors' sales or profits rather than the health of the competitive process itself literally go beyond the terms of the law. Similarly in need of change is the recent Commission practice of crowning successful pricing discrimination proceedings with cease and desist orders that simply forbade a respondent henceforth to quote any differential prices in sales among competing purchasers, or even to non-competing customers for whom rival sellers vied.114/ 

These broadgauge orders sprang from a Commission policy which bound any seller once guilty of price discrimination to adhere thenceforward to a nondiscriminatory, i.e. one-price, marketing system. From this he might secure dispensation--if warranted by supervening factual or legal developments--only by a formal request for modification of the outstanding order to the FTC.115/

Such orders constitute, as one Supreme Court Justice aptly observed, "an undiscriminating prohibition of discrimination,"116/

By commanding a respondent to abatain from all price differentials that may violate this pervasive law, such an order "really promulgates an inaccurate partial paraphrase of its indeterminate generalities; "117/ confounding rather than clarifying the respondent's legal duties, it inherently frustrates reasonable attempts in good faith to comply. As administered, blanket cease and desist orders produce a system of mounting penalties for Robinson-Patman violations, the first delict provoking an order to cease and desist, while subsequent transgressions first draw Court injunctions, followed by penalties for contempt--without at any stage supplying legal guidance beyond what the perplexing text of the Act reveals. Confronted with an inscrutable order of indefinite scope, a seller may well forego any differential price reductions rather than risk legal strife. 118/ One aspect of the resultant problem was resolved by the Supreme 

Court in the Ruberoid case.

In the Court's view, a respondent need not clear every price differential in advance with the Commission "each time, for example, that a competitor's price reduction requires it either to lower its price in good faith to meet the lower competing

We consider the Ruberoid principle a reasonable reconciliation of effective enforcement techniques with the preservation of competitors' pricing freedom. We agree that a respondent must not utilize the pretext of inconsequential pricing variations for insisting on a fullscale litigation afresh. At the same time, however, the dynamics of the competitive process might irreparably suffer if all sellers once entangled in the anti-price discrimination law were condemned to compulsory pricing uniformity under perpetual FTC supervision and price control. Hence we recommend that the concept of "changed circumstances"--decisive in determining whether a seller subject to an order must refrain from all future price differentials or may litigate their validity--be realistically applied to liberate even those previously unlawful price differentials when facing a "new competitive situation."125/ At all events, neither a differential genuinely responsive to a new business development nor a pricing schedule revised in good faith should be condemned unless causing the requisite adverse market effects, and unless indefensible under an express statutory proviso.

To help achieve this end, Commission expertese can make more precise the mandates its orders impose. It is true that the Supreme 

Court in the Morton Salt case reproved an order which equivocally authorized a less than five-cent differential--if not causing competitive "injury" cognizable by the Act.126/ But the Court's rebuke of such administrative ambivalence, we think, in no way commends cease and desist orders whose blanket prohibitions place every price concession at the seller's peril. In fact, the Court stressed findings as to the market impact of price differentials as "One of the reasons for entrusting enforcement of this Act primarily to the Commission, a body of experts;" such findings must underpin "orders definitely restraining the particular discriminatory practices which may tend to injure competition without justification."127 Consonant with the 

Court's observation in the Ruberoid case, we cannot ignore that "Congress expected the Commission to exercise a special competence in formulating remedies to deal with problems in the general sphere of competitive practices."128/ While the Supreme Court in the particular factual context of that case sanctioned a flat prohibition on all price differentials as a permissible exercise of the broad Commission discretion in devising implements for future compliance, 129/ Ruberoid

radiates no implication disfavoring administrative remedies more finely drawn. 

The Commission, for example, might well analyze the evidence in price discrimination proceedings to fashion remedies which foster compliance with concrete commands. For example, proof of "cost savings" though having fallen short of exonerating a challenged price differential may nevertheless demonstrate that some smaller differential is "cost-justifiable"; 130 or the record may reveal trade patterns indicating that some lesser discriminatory concession might not cause adverse market effects, if any, sufficient to qualify as "injury" under the Act.131/ In either event, the Commission might alloy its expertise with the raw data in the record to forge findings reasonably relating amounts smaller than the particular condemned price differential to demonstrable "cost savings" or potential "injury." 

Thus a specific cease and desist order might preserve expressly the respondent's prerogatives to quote those differentials which emerge as "non-injurious" or otherwise "justifiable" from the agency's market or accounting analysis. Such carefully formulated orders would achieve their legitimate task of terminating illegal discriminations, without obstructing a seller's flexibility in attuning his prices to market shifts--a process essential to effective functioning of a free economy.

THE COST DEFENSE 

Price discriminations which cause the proscribed adverse competitive effects are nevertheless justifiable if the differential does not exceed savings in certain of the seller's costs. An express proviso in Section 2(a) declares that nothing in the Act "shall prevent differentials which make only due allowance for differences in the cost of manufacture, sale, or delivery resulting from the differing methods or quantities in which such commodities are to such purchasers sold or delivered."

Congress contemplated this cost proviso to fairly adjust the protection of small buyers with the welfare of the consumer, Although substantial price favors to some customers were viewed as seriously prejudicing their rivals' opportunities to compete, the interest of the consumer in the fruits of mass production and low-cost distribution might irreparably suffer by any flat ban on every price differential, Congress accordingly designed the cost defense to accommodate roughly these competing demands, preserving for the public the benefits of efficient marketing methods, while at the same time subjecting to the affirmative prohibitions of the statute 338

FF

those "unearned" price differentials which could not be reasonably related to some savings in the seller's costs of manufacture, sale or delivery.132/ 

In practice, however, the cost defense has proved largely illusory. After one successful cost defense before the Commission in 1937, not until the B. F. Goodrich case seventeen years later did an accused seller in a fully contested proceeding succeed in establishing a complete cost defense to defeat every element in a discrimination charge. 

While in two other litigated and finally adjudicated proceedings a cost defense partially prevailed, the Federal Trade Commission in seven other recorded instances rejected attempted cost justifications out of hand. 134/ Sellers

To date, we believe that businessmen and consumers have largely been deprived of the cost proviso's intended benefits. 

The trouble begins with the prolix and perplexing statutory text. At one point the cost proviso expressly recognizes the propriety of price differentials reflecting economies in manufacturing, as well as sales and delivery, costs; yet elsewhere the proviso evidently insists that legally cognizable costs derive exclusively from methods or quantities of sale thus demanding that efficiencies in manufacturing be specifically related to distribution techniques before qualifying as a basis for permissible variations in price. 

In the absence of clearly articulated official guidance for resolving this as well as other areas of legal doubt, recent attempts to invoke the cost justification have involved or delivery 

mammoth expenditure of costly expert effort, without assurance that the resulting project would be tested against discernible rules of law.

Furthermore, the ambiguity of legal criteria has coincided with difficulties in the administration of accounting matters. 

The absence of officially sanctioned accounting standards has provoked needless dispute over easily reconcilable disparities in technique.142/ Also in price discrimination 

142 See, for example, the accounting controversy facing the adjudicatory tribunal, as reflected in the appellate court's opinion, American Can Co. v. Russellville Canning 

Co., 191 F. 2d 38, 51-52 (8th Cir. 1951). 342

proceedings, Commission staff accountants have registered a sometimes ambivalent appearance as expert witnesses who were accorded a measure of deference by the tribunal entirely disproportionate to their occasional role as partisan aids in the prosecution's case. And the Commission at times has wholly discarded and accorded no weight whatsoever to complex cost studies that merely fell short of accounting for the challenged differential by a fraction of a cent.143/ Under such circumstances, only the most prosperous and patient corporation could afford pursuit of an often illusory defense. 

Necessarily pressure builds to gain legal safety by withholding price differentials from more efficient buyers, thus denying to the public the benefits of mass production and economical distribution processes which Congress intended to preserve by enacting the cost proviso in Section 2(a), We further believe that several prolonged controversies have contributed to devitalization of the statutory cost In the first place, accounting is by no means an exact science; techniques of distribution cost allocation are still evolving, and several equally plausible and recognized methods are employed even in the more traditional defense. studies of manufacturing costs. 144/

concept of "cost" itself is steeped in uncertainty and not susceptible to universally valid definitions.

alone determines the seller's price in the market. In fact, since a seller's quoted prices directly influence his volume of sales 

which is the critical element in any calculation 

unit costs, in this "outlay" sense, 145/ of cost per unit sold may depend on price quotations as much as vice versa.

On the other hand, Section 2(a) presupposes a quite distinct conception of "cost" that is not a determinant of price at all. 

Rather, the statutory cost proviso represents purely a legislative dispensation for efficient marketing techniques, exempting from the Robinson-Patman Act's prohibitions those already quoted differential prices which demonstrably reward the comparative economies of one versus alternative methods of sale or delivery. Hence, the "cost" contemplated by 

Section 2(a) does not signify the aggregate outlay that constitutes one element in the complex of considerations which influence prospective pricing decisions by the seller; it is simply a retrospective monetary appraisal of the benefits deriving from a more compared with a less efficient distribution technique. 

145 Compare United States v. General Electric Co., 115 F. 

Supp. 837, 867-868 (D.N.J. 1953). 344

Finally, a firm's expenditures comprising the differences in costs as between alternative methods of distribution are typically spread among numerous products in varying ways; the breakdown of these "joint" costs among the several components involves largely subjective business judgments in choosing among several equally rational alternative 

Such considerations are obviously methods of allocation. L not suitable for periodic entry in a seller's regular books of account. As the Supreme Court remarked, a Robinson- 

Patman cost defense consequently must resort to data which "cannot be obtained from ordinary business records."--"It is not a question of obtaining information in the seller's hands," but "a matter of studying [his] business afresh."146/ 

Hence any requirement of mathematical exactitude in cost justification vastly oversimplifies a complex problem and imposes excessive demands..

We therefore propose the Commission adopt realistic standards properly reflecting inadequacies inherent in accounting measurements of price. Formulation of rules of good accounting practice is a task for accounting experts, and hence beyond our scope. Hence we commend the recent appointment by the Chairman of the Federal Trade Commission of an Advisory Committee to propose technical accounting

standards for rationalizing statutory cost justifications. 

Clearcut and officially promulgated accountancy guides can materially confine the area of legal controversy, and thereby contribute signally to improved availability and administration of the cost defense. However, administration of the cost defense by the Commission must evolve within the framework of a statute, and thus subject to legal analysis.147/ 

As a legal framework, therefore, we recommend recognition that a Robinson-Patman cost defense is not susceptible to testing by precise or mechanical rules. We advise a liberal interpretation of the statute's "due allowance" criterion as enacting a reasonable de minimis concept to exonerate a challenged price even if an attempted cost defense falls short of "justifying" it by a fractional amount; in similar fashion, realistic adaptation of this concept should halt legal pursuit of only partially "justified" price concessions whenever the "unjustified" balance alone could not reasonably 148/ suffice to bring the Act into play. Applied in this way, the Act should impede no price variation reasonably related to economies in any of the seller's costs deriving from significant differences among broad categories of commercial transactions.

QUANTITY LIMITS PROVISO 

While the cost justification proviso in Section 2(a) exempts price differentials not exceeding due cost savings flowing from economical "methods or quantities" of sale or delivery, that clause is immediately qualified by a further proviso which authorizes the Federal Trade Commission to "fix and establish quantity limits as to particular commodities or classes of commodities, where it finds that available purchasers in greater quantities are so few as to render differentials on account thereof unjustly discriminatory or promotive of monopoly in any line of commerce; and the foregoing shall then not be construed to permit differentials based on differences in quantities greater than those so fixed and established." 

150 (cont'd) principles, which may reasonably flow from differences in the categories of transactions involved." 

This textual revision restates the basic elements which we have recommended for administrative interpretation. 349

pedomfy HOUD Lenges

The quantity limits proviso eclipses one area of this cost exemption, by authorizing the Federal Trade Commission to impose a ceiling beyond which cost savings resulting from economical quantities, but not methods, might no longer be lawfully reflected in a lower price. Dormant for fifteen years, 151/ it was for the first and only time implemented by the Federal Trade Commission in 1951. In that case, the Commission's directive fixed a quantity limit as to rubber tires and tubes for motor vehicles. The validity of the Commission's order was challenged by numerous affected parties and is presently in the chamels of judicial review 152/ The quantity limits proviso is both ineffectual and redundant. Since it strikes only at quantities but not methods of economical marketing, unaffected are efficient types of mass distribution other than quantity sales. In addition, because cost justification of a quantity discount becomes progressively more difficult for the largest quantities, operation of the cost defense would itself impose perhaps equally effective and certainly more evenhanded limitations on allegedly undesirable concessions. 

151 Quantity Limit Rule 203-1, 17 Fed. Reg. 113. 

152/ The District Court's dismissal of the suit on jurisdictional grounds was reversed and remanded for a hearing on the merits. B. F. Goodrich Co. v. Federal Trade Commission, 208 F. 2d 829 (D.C. Cir. 1953).

Moreover, the quantity limits proviso complicates the Act with novel and perplexing criteria. For example, nothing defines the "fewness" of buyers on which the invocation of the proviso depends, or relates an "unjustly discriminatory" quantity discount to the express price discrimination provisions in Section 2(a). Further, the proviso refers not to size of discount but to fewness of buyers, thus posing the paradox of applying the rule when only a few rather than many purchasers receive the discount of which those remaining ineligible might complain. At all events, the proviso necessarily undertakes to threaten price differentials which reflect economies in efficient distribution--thus offending the consumer interest which the cost defense was designed to preserve. 

We believe that any

Above all, this Committee considers the quantity limits proviso directly at war with "the broader antitrust policies that have been laid down by Congress." rational antitrust policy must leave the American business community free to explore new methods of distribution. 

Arrangements to impede competing distributive techniques have long been viewed as unreasonable restraints of trade.153/ 

153/ E.g., United States v. Frankfort Distilleries, 324 U.S. 

293 (1945); Ramsay Co. v. Associated Bill Posters, 260 U.S. 

501 (1923); Eastern States Retail Lumber Association v. 

United States, 234 U.S. 600 (1914); Montague v. Lowry, 193 U.S. 38 (1904). 351

Hence we deplore the quantity limits proviso's singling out and penalizing of the quantity discount system. And while a free economy must place primary reliance on the play of market forces as the determinant of price, the quantity limits proviso, in our view, defeats this policy through ineptly sanctioning a crude form of price-fixing by administrative flat where competition must safeguard the public interest. Accordingly, this Committee recommends that proviso be repealed. 

'CHANGING CONDITIONS" EXEMPTION 

Price discriminations affirmatively banned by Section 2(a) of the Act are additionally justifiable under a proviso which exempts "price changes from time to time where in response to changing conditions affecting the market for or the marketability of the goods concerned, such as but not limited to actual or imminent deterioration of perishable goods, obsolescence of seasonal goods, distress sales under court process or sales in good faith in discontinuance of business in the goods concerned." 

The "changing conditions" proviso has figured in only two recorded litigations, In one, judicial dicta intimate that price fluctuations in wheat might constitute a sufficient 352

changing condition affecting the market for" flour to exempt 154/ an otherwise unlawful price differential in flour sales.- 

More recently, however, an appellate court, by analogy to the examples enumerated in the text, restricted the proviso to situations "similar" to the unloading of seasonal and obsolescent or perishable and deteriorating merchandise, or forced sales compelled by judicial process or discontinuance of a merchant's business line.155/ In rejecting a 11 local boycott of the seller's goods as insufficiently "similar" to meet the proviso's tests, the Court failed to establish criteria for measuring the requisite degree of "similarity. 

Consequently, judicial interpretations of this undeveloped 'changing conditions" proviso have not yet crystallized. 11 The "changing conditions" proviso sought to carve out a reasonable exemption for a class of commercial transactions otherwise vulnerable as price discriminations. The prohibitions of the statute presuppose discriminatory treatment of customers in analogous transactions involving similar goods under comparable market conditions at approximately the same time. 

Only when these basic elements of the statute are met can the "changing conditions" exemption, as any other of the statutory provisos, come into play. With this proviso, 154 Huber v. Pillsbury Flour Mills Co., 30 F. Supp. 108 (S.D.N.Y. 1939). 

155/ Moore v. Mead Service Co., 190 F. 2d 540 (10th Cir. 1951).~ 353

Congress tried to assure that a seller's pricing might without risk of illegality respond readily to quickly unfolding market events, even though this prejudiced some rival buyers who purchased almost simultaneously in equivalent commercial circumstances but at a higher price which preceded the "change". 

Such superficially "discriminatory" sales among competing customers, we think, were the object of the Congressional exemption--particularly since a seller thus shifting his prices in a genuine response to market fluctuations could not explain a differential through resort to the cost defense or other exculpatory proviso.

With this view of the "changing conditions" proviso, we believe its broad interpretation comports best with the Congressional intent as well as broader antitrust objectives. 

Toward this end, an independent meaning should be accorded to the statutory "changing conditions affecting the market" to which a competitor might respond by revising his price, wholly apart from the "marketability of the goods concerned." 

In any event, we view the significant common denominator in these far from homogeneous examples not as a decay of the seller's goods or business position, but as a spontaneous shift in market conditions under way or impending beyond the seller's control. Such commercial adjustments seller should be able to reflect in good faith by revised price quotations on short notice, though this may incidentally prejudice some customers who bought at less favorable prices in the immediate past. 354

eral al dupekes tahtoo's T

Since no adjudications have promulgated any fixed interpretation of the "changing conditions" proviso, we find no warrant for recommending legislative change. Were revision in order, we would suggest, as a restatement and clarification, an exemption of "discriminations in price made in good faith because of changes, actual or imminent, in the market or the marketability of the goods concerned."

THE "GOOD FAITH" MEETING OF COMPETITION DEFENSE 

Finally, price discriminations prohibited by Section 2(a) when causing the requisite competitive "injury" are nevertheless justifiable by virtue of an express proviso in Section 2(b) which declares that nothing in the Act "shall prevent a seller rebutting the prima facie case thus made by showing that his lower price or the furnishing of services or facilities to any purchaser or was made in good faith to meet an equally low price of a competitor, or the services or facilities furnished by a competitor." 

The "meeting competition" defense constitutes a narrowed version of an equivalent exemption in Section 2 of the original Clayton Act. That former proviso, excluding from the statute's prohibitions any price discriminations "in the same or different communities made in good faith to 355

both sao to dowy og aflat fows all eher now th nothobulky of oh gekhibally vas od að wu adarsh toy bayes of 6 Getperpros? sorgus eritatoes 05 of sitket bag, ob obga coisiaaksid traded to one ethni

meet competition," was viewed by Congress in 1936 as a legal defect. As regarded by the proponents of the Robinson-Patman 

Act, the old proviso tolerated the predatory undercutting of prices by powerful rivals bent on the destruction of smaller competitors in the guise of merely meeting their competition. 

In the Robinson-Patman amendments, the "meeting competition" proviso was accordingly contracted so as to permit a seller in good faith to meet but not beat an actual competitor's equally low price.

Two major Supreme Court decisions have embroiled the scope and legal effect of the Section 2(b) "meeting competition" defense. In the Staley case, a seller was charged with price discrimination through a geographic pricing system which exacted "phantom freight" from customers located near his plant but distant from an artificial basing-point.157/ 

The Court rejected a defense grounded in asserted meeting of "competition" through adoption of the basing-point system previously established by the industry. In the 

Court's view, a seller could not claim "good faith" by emulating other sellers' patently unlawful pricing system which, in fact, had raised and not lowered prices. In another phase of its opinion, however, the Court in defining 

156/ FTC Final Report on Chain Store Investigation, Sen. Doc, 

4 (74th Cong., 1st Session) at 90 (1935). But of.American

Can Co. v. Ladoga Canning Co., 44 F. 2d 763 (7th Cir. 1930). 

157/ 324 U.S. 746 (1945); see also Federal Trade Commission v. Cement Institute, 333 U.S. 683 (1948). 156/ 356

the scope of the defense emphasized that "the statute does not place an impossible burden upon sellers;" a businessman who had "knowingly discriminated in price" need merely "show the existence of facts which would lead a reasonable and prudent person to believe that the granting of a lower price would in fact meet the equally low price of a competitor."158/ 

This adjudication was followed in 1951 by the Standard 

Oil case.159/

The Federal Trade Commission had contended that meeting a competitor's equally low price operated only to rebut the prima facie case made by a showing of sales at different prices, but was nullified by any affirmative proof of competitive "injury" caused by the challenged differential. 

A closely divided Supreme Court, however, rejected this contention, and construed the "meeting competition" proviso as affording an "absolute" defense, incidental "injury" notwithstanding. And meeting a competitor's price in the 

Standard case could not cause legally cognizable "injury," for the challenged lower price was available to the "favored" customer from other suppliers, anyway. In the Court's view, Congress had in 1936 contracted the scope of the original proviso by confining it to "price differentials occurring

While the Supreme Court in the Standard Oil decision settled the "meeting competition" proviso as a complete rather than a semi-defense to a discrimination charge, the Court did not elaborate on Staley or clearly define the essential elements of a successful justification. As a result, serious problems of statutory interpretation today confront tribunals--in government proceedings or private damage suits 162/.. adjudicating the legality of a seller's prices in the light of his "meeting competition" defense. Difficulties cluster around the following complex issues: meeting of a competitor's unlawful prices; sporadic or regular competitive differentials; exact dollar-for-dollar meeting or undercutting

In fact, not a single seller in a recorded case to date has succeeded in finally justifying a challenged discrimination by recourse to Section 2(b)'s "meeting competition" defense.

This Committee approves the result of the Standard Oil decision as consonant with the Nation's antitrust policy. 

Whatever the interpretation of the substantive price discrimination offense, we think that a seller's right to meet a competitor's prices by granting price differentials to some customers, without reducing his prices to all, must remain an essential qualification to any anti-price discrimination law. For a seller constrained by law to reduce prices to some only at the cost of reducing prices to all may well end by reducing them to none. As the Commission recently recommended to Congress "the right to meet a lower price which a competitor is offering to a customer, when this is done in good faith, is the essence of competitive economy."

Anything less, we think, would move the price discrimination statute into irreconcilable conflict with the basic policy of the Sherman Act.

In the Committee's view, however, the "absolute" status which the Supreme Court accorded the "meeting competition" defense must not be undermined by interpretations thwarting 359

its effectiveness. Towards this end, we suggest the following guides: (1) In accord with the Supreme Court's reasoning in the analogous Automatic Canteen and Staley cases, a seller should at least be deemed to have met a lawful price unless it is shown that he knew or should have known otherwise. 

Restriction of the defense to authorize the meeting of only a competitor's lawful prices--a limitation which some Committee members deem wholly anomalous--should not force a seller to compete at his peril. A businessman who operates in the haste and pressures of the market cannot prophesy whether the competitor's price which he must meet may later be held unlawful; nor is over-all antitrust policy furthered by information exchange programs to spread such knowledge among competitors. (2) We do not regard the Staley decision as confining the "good faith" defense to meeting competition on "sporadic" or isolated occasions, or some but not all of the time. A workable defense must authorize a seller to cope with competitive pressures so long as they exist, regardless of the frequency of the price reductions that competitive circumstances warrant. However, the Section 2(b) defense should not permit collusive price-matching among sellers, and 360

On the other hand, Section 2(b) ought not presuppose the seller's departure from a previously uniform non-discriminatory price scale, a past circumstance not remotely related to present genuine responses to a competitive situation. (3) Although the "meeting competition" defense authorizes a seller only to meet but not beat competitors' prices, we recommend a reasonable adaptation of this limitation to the actualities of business. Consonant with the Supreme Court's formula in the Staley case, a seller should be held to show only "the existence of facts which would lead a reasonable and prudent person to believe that the granting of a lower price would in fact meet the equally low price of a compe- 164/ titor." 

An incidental undercutting of the prices quoted by others, when in the course of genuinely meeting one particular competitor's equally low price offer, hence should not invalidate a seller's defense. At the same time, a seller responding to a rival's price should not be confined to quotations for a precisely equivalent quantity of goods.165/ 

163/ Compare the Corn Products and Staley cases, 324 U.S. 726 and 746 (1945) with Corn Products Mfg. Co., 47 F.T.C. 587 (1950); and cf. Federal Trade Commission v. Cement Institute, 333 U.S. 683 (1948). 164/ 324 U.S. at 759-760. 

165/ We deem the Court's dictum in Federal Trade Commission v. Standard Brands, 189 F. 2d 510, 515 (2d Cir. 1951) appropriate to the particular facts of that case, yet not establishing a "quantity" rule to limit a seller's freedom realistically to meet a competitor's lower price. 361

Furthermore, of obvious significance in testing the validity of a competitive quotation of a "delivered" price is the relevant definition of price under the Section 2(b) defense. We think Section 2(b) refers to the actual, laid down cost--inclusive of freight--paid by each buyer. In this way, a seller could legitimately quote a "delivered" price to distant buyers in the same dollar amount as the f.o.b. mill figure quoted by the local rival nearby. Any other interpretation of "price" would legally disable spatial competitive concessions, since the distant seller's "delivered" price quotation, though in truth only equalizing the local rival's geographic advantage, would be viewed in law as undercutting his price.

Nor do we think that a seller should be forced to meet a competitor's equally low price to the last fraction of a cent. Potent factors other than naked price determine a customer's choice among competing offers--especially with consumer goods reflecting a broad range of quality, design, or brand. While basic homogeneous commodities such as oil, steel and cement normally present slight problems of consumer differentiation, electrical appliances or chemicals, for example, involve a broad range of significant competitive factors other than price. Here a mechanical test would foil the objective of permitting a realistic equalization of an actual competitive situation. An inflexible cent-for-cent rule would enable a 362

(4) Standard Oil does not confine the "good faith" proviso solely to defensive reductions to retain an existing customer. 

The Supreme Court in that opinion merely employed language describing the case at bar; it did not promulgate a general doctrine surrounding each seller with customers which may be exploited without fear of a rival's price attacks. Such a limitation in any event would not be in keeping with elementary principles of competition, and would in fact foster tight and rigid commercial relationships by insulating them from market forces. (5) We recommend that the term "good faith" be utilized solely to test the seller's adherence to the basic objectives of the meeting competition proviso: facilitating price reductions in genuine response to competitive market pressures in order to equalize a competitive opportunity. In practice, this will disqualify the seller to whom meeting of competition is only an incidental by-product of a scheme to monopolize or other objective inimical to overall antitrust policy. 

To facilitate our proposals, we also recommend passage of amendatory legislation. Although our detailed recommendations in each case adhere to the present text of the law, and hence are entirely susceptible to administrative implementation, extraordinery circumstances militate in favor of legislative reform. In March, 1953, a majority of the Federal Trade Commission criticized the Supreme Court's holding as rendering the prohibitions on price discrimination "too weak;" in June, 1953, a reconstituted Commission majority expressly repudiated and withdrew that former view, and endorsed a vigorous interpretation of the "good faith" defense.

These diametrically opposed basic attitudes toward the "good faith" defense have naturally produced incongruities and inconsistencies in the Commission's decisional process, thereby generating conflict and antagonisma not yet allayed.168/ 

Legislative revision of the "meeting competition" proviso, by unequivocally accommodating that defense to the basic aims of antitrust policy in accord with the Supreme Court's decisions in Standard Oil and Automatic Canteen, would, in our opinion, resolve existing confusion, end the simmering controversy, and enable the Commission to get on with its task. 

We therefore recommend 169/ revision of the "meeting competition" proviso in Section 2(b) to read substantially as follows: 

168/ E.g., Standard Oil Co., F.T.C. Dkt. 4389 (Jan. 16, 1953); Initial Decision in E. Edelmann & Co., F.T.C. Dkt. 

5770 (March 4, 1954), pp. 16, 18. 

169/ Walter Adams dissents from the preceding analysis and this proposal. In his words: "As long as Sec. 2(b) affords an absolute defense to a charge of price discrimination, 365

"In any proceeding involving an alleged violation of this section, it shall be a complete defense to a charge of discrimination if the seller shows that his lower price, or his furnishing of services or facilities, was made in good faith to meet the lower price of, or services or facilities of comparable value furnished or offered by, a competitor: Provided, That a seller shall not be deemed to have acted in good faith if he knew or should have known that the competitor's price or offer was unlawfully discriminatory." 

169 (cont'd) it is unlikely-except in the rarest of circumstances that recourse will be had to the cost justification provisions under Sec. 2(a) which, from respondent's viewpoint, is certainly more difficult to sustain. 

This means that the central objective of the R-P Act as embodied in Sec. 2(a), viz. to protect competition and competitors against injurious distortions of market pressures not derived from superior efficiency, will effectively be circumvented and undermined. Unless the underlying purpose of the R-P Act is to be ignored, "good faith" cannot be accepted as an absolute defense but must, instead, be weighed against the probable injury that might occur or had occurred as a result of discriminatory practices. In other words, "good faith" should remain an absolute defense except where the effect of a given discrimination may be to substantially lessen competition or tend to create a monopoly. 

Legislative clarification of Sec. 2(b) along these lines appears desirable, and such clarification is recommended in lieu of various pending proposals to emasculate the Act. 

Unless Sec. 2(b) as now interpreted by the courts is modified, the small buyer shall again be placed at the mercy of mass purchasers seeking to entrench their market dominance not alone by superior efficiency - but by the exertion of sheer market power. The "good faith" defense may become a cloak for various forms of basing point or quasi-basing point systems. 

The enforcement agencies may be diverted from their basic task of discovering and prosecuting price discrimination which has anti-competitive effects to the futile psychoanalysis of sellers who employ discrimination as a technique of market rivalry. 

Intent rather than market effect may thus become the enforcement standard for a vital piece of antitrust legislation. 

Such an eventuality is not, I believe, in the best interests of the consumer or the best interests of a competitive economy. 

Until we have achieved that consummation devoutly to be wished; until the objectives of the Sherman Act have become an economic reality, we shall have to rely on such statutes as the Robinson- Patman Act to curb the abuse of market power in the form of direct or indirect price discriminations.' 366

THE "BROKERAGE" AND "PROPORTIONALLY EQUAL" ALLOWANCES OR SERVICES PROVISIONS 

In addition to the direct and indirect price discriminations proscribed by Section 2(a) of the Act, Sections 2(c),170/ (a) 171/ 

170/ Section 2(c), the "brokerage" provision, declares:

That it shall be unlawful for any person engaged in commerce, in the course of such commerce, to pay or grant, or to receive or accept, anything of value as a commission, brokerage, or other compensation, or any allowance or discount in lieu thereof, except for services rendered in connection with the sale or purchase of goods, wares, or merchandise, either to the other party to such transaction or to an agent, representative, or other intermediary therein where such intermediary is acting in fact for or in behalf, or is subject to the direct or indirect control, of any party to such transaction other than the person by whom such compensation is so granted or paid. 

171/ Section 2(d), known as the "proportionally equal allowances" provision, holds:

That it shall be unlawful for any person engaged in commerce to pay or contract for the payment of anything of value to or for the benefit of a customer of such person in the course of such commerce as compensation or in consideration for any services or facilities furnished by or through such customer in connection with the processing, handling, sale, or offering for sale by such person, unless such payment or consideration is available on proportionally equal terms to all other customers competing in the distribution of such products or commodities. 367

and (e)172/ contain prohibitions on specified forms of discriminatory concessions to "favored" buyers. The Commission's

That it shall be unlawful for any person to discriminate in favor of one purchaser against another purchaser or purchasers of a commodity bought for resale, with or without processing, by contracting to furnish or furnishing, or by contributing to the furnishing of, any services or facilities connected with the processing, handling, sale, or offering for sale of such commodity so purchased upon terms not accorded to all purchasers on proportionally equal terms. 368

Brokerage 

Interpretation has developed Section 2(c) into a simple prohibition on payments to all distributive intermediaries except wholly "independent" brokers. One ambiguity was settled by an appellate decision which extended the prohibition to cover any "agent" or "representative," whether or not he acted "in fact for" the buyer or in his "behalf."173/

More significant were adjudications which for all practical purposes read the "services rendered" exception out of the Act. Allowances and Services

Sections 2(d) and 2(e), concerned with analogous marketing practices, have developed through parallel judicial interpretation. Minor discrepancies in these twin texts were ironed out by courts ready to resolve the two subsections into a harmonious whole. The interpretative process thus interpolated into 2(a) the prerequisite of competing customers, and an interstate commerce requirement into Section 2(e).178/ Decisions equally have not distinguished the "customers" of 2(d) from the "purchasers" of 2(d), and have considered allowances "available" and services "accorded" whenever openly offered to affected buyers. 179/ 

The criterion of "proportional equality" in Sections 2(d) and 2(e) has recently been construed by the Federal Trade Commis sion. In the recent Trade Practice Rules for the Cosmetics Industry

and its opinion in the Soap cases 180/ the Commission has evolved a rule of thumb for testing the requisite "proportional equality" in allowances or services by comparing the promotional benefit with the dollar volume of purchases by various customers. In actual practice, promotional programs can thus conform to the law in three basic ways: (1) payment of a dollar allowance per unit of promotional service rendered by each buyer, up to a uniform maximum percentage of his dollar volume; (2) a simplified plan, granting each buyer a set dollar allowance per unit of merchandise bought, on condition that he perform a specified minimum quantum of promotional services; (3) the seller's direct furnishing of promotional services to the buyer, worth up to a uniform percentage of each buyer's volume. The Commission's recent clarification of 2(d) and 2(e) also recognized that not all customers entitled to participation in a seller's promotional plan can effectively utilize each type of promotional service tendered, or perform that form of service which the seller desires to reward with an allowance under his plan. However, to ensure appropriate benefits to all qualified customers, the Commission expects the seller to offer bona fide alternative means enabling all buyers to participate. This Committee considers the prevailing interpretations of the "brokerage" clause wholly incompatible with broader antitrust objectives. Goods are sped to the consumer through marketing functions, whether performed by independent "brokers" or other businessmen who have invested capital and services in the "middleman" phase of the marketing process. A legal disqualification of all but the "pure" broker's distributive services is thus at war with business realities. Moreover, the essence of antitrust policy in distribution is to assure that the consumer benefits by vigorous competition along each step of the way. Yet the "brokerage" clause as presently interpreted enacts a preferred position for the "independent" broker, thus discriminating against competing factors in distribution who are arbitrarily denied compensation for genuine marketing functions which they perform. Joint buying organizations for pooling the resources of small businessmen have conspicuously suffered from rigid 184/ "brokerage" clause enforcement.

In our opinion, the virtual legal monopoly conferred by Section 2(c) on one type of middleman seriously stymies competition in the channels of distribution, and exacts tribute from the consumer for the benefit of a special business class. 

184/ E.g., Biddle Purchasing Co. v. Federal Trade Commission, 96 F. 2d 687 (2d Cir. 1938); Modern Marketing Service, Inc. v.

Federal Trade Commission, 149 F. 2a 970 (7th Cir. 1945). 

374

Contrary to the restrictive interpretations of the brokerage clause, the allowances and services provisions, in our view, have been administered in a workable way. We share the Commission's recognition in the Soap cases that "no standard could be laid down which would ensure exact proportionality with the mathematical accuracy of a slide rule. "185/ Hence we approve the evident intention of the Commission to accept any promotional plan "both honest in its purpose and fair and reasonable in its "186/ application. 

However, as a matter of clarifying its enforcement policy, we recommend that the Commission promulgate a policy statement expressly sanctioning the following basic types of promotional plan: (1) percentage allowances of the buyer's gross purchase, conditioned on his performance of services reasonably equivalent in value; (2) allowances at set rates for given promotional services by the buyer, with the total allowance confined to a uniform percentage of the buyer's gross volume; (3) promotional services by the seller approximating in value a uniform percentage of the recipient buyer's gross volume. Further, in each case equivalent types of promotion best suiting particular customer classes should be authorized, so long as a reasonable relationship between the benefits accorded each buyer class is

Promotion systems whose legality is now implicit in the determinations of the Commission would thus be unmistakably qualified under the "proportionally equal" test of 2(d) and (e).

Nevertheless, many factors argue for legislative reform of the "proportionately equal" as well as the "brokerage" provisions. An administrative reinvigoration of the "for services rendered" exception in the brokerage clause--in order to recognize every genuine distributive function performed by any form of legitimate business enterprise--would help restore equal legal opportunities for all types of distribution, and revive that competition among middlemen whose benefits the consumer must now by law forego. Such reappraisal of more than a decade of administrative rulings, however desirable, is complicated by several appellate adjudications which adopt the Commission's previous restrictive interpretations. At the same time, an administratively imposed requirement of competitive "injury" before condemning a seller's promotional plan under the "proportionally equal" clauses must take account of an appellate determination expressly holding the other way. In neither case, however, has the Supreme Court rendered a final determination.187/ 

187/ The Supreme Court has consistently declined to review those Court of Appeals decisions interpreting Sections 2(c) and (d) & (e) as enacting per se violations. 376

Jopol sequ Widerled welven

More fundamentally, we disapprove the present disparity in the statutory consequences which attach to economically equivalent business practices. Today, "direct" or "indirect" price discriminations under Section 2(a) do not transgress the law unless causing adverse market effects and unless unjustifiable under one of the defensive provisos. In contrast, "brokerage" concessions or "proportionately unequal" allowances or services are illegal per se. This legal quirk facilitates manipulation and fosters confusion, since the Act places a premium on cloaking any discriminatory concession in terms of the desired legal result. 

Virtually identical trade practices have been deemed "allowances" in one case and "indirect discriminations" in another, 188/ while the "brokerage" clause has been invoked even against reductions in net price in direct transactions which dispensed with fees for independent brokers.189/ The decisions, moreover, reveal no guide for distinguishing a justifiable "indirect" discrimination from a flat per se offense. Such legal incongruities, we believe, frustrate equally the Commission's legitimate enforcement objectives and businessmen's good faith attempts to comply.

189/ See, e.g., Great Atlantic & Pacific Tea Co., 106 F. 2d

Antitrust enforcement should not be complicated by diverse legal consequences solely dependent on whether a discriminatory concession masquerades as a "brokerage", "allowance", or "service" rather than a naked quotation in price. For, as the

Supreme Court has recognized, in any reasonable implementation of antitrust objectives "the crucial fact is the impact of the particular practice on competition, not the label that it 

And we consider "broader antitrust objectives" opposed to legal interpretations which penalize normal business conduct either wholly harmless in market effects, or at least justifiable in terms of the defenses authorized in price discrimination cases. Consequently, the statutory standards governing brokerage as well as allowances or services should be accommodated to the provisions governing price discrimination in Section 2(a). By viewing these specified practices as expressly defined instances of the "indirect" discriminations governed by Section 2(a), reconciliation with the broader aims 

190/ E.g., Federal Trade Commission v. Motion Picture Advertis ing Service Co., 344 U.S. 392, 397 (1953); see also United States v. Masonite Corp., 316 U.S. 265, 280 (1942); Federal Trade

(1953). 378

of antitrust legislation as well as elimination of internal statutory anomalies would automatically result. 

Such integration, we believe, is best accomplished through minor textual amendment. Sections 2(c), (d) and (e) are presently introduced by declarations "That it shall be unlawful" to perpetrate the specific offense which the text of each subsection defines. We recommend statutory revision by amending in each case this quoted introductory clause to read "That it shall be deemed an indirect discrimination in price within the meaning of Section 2(a)," to grant the given discriminatory concession described in the then following text of each subsection. language of 2(c), (d) and (e) would thus remain precisely in its present form, save only for the indicated revision in each introductory clause.191/ The

So modified, the legal sanctions in Sections 2(c), (a) and (e) would correspond with those governing price discriminations in Section 2(a). Discriminatory concessions in whatever form would face the test of competitive "injury" as a requisite of 191/ As a minor perfecting amendment, we also recommend deletion of that language in Section 2(c) forbidding a buyer "to receive or accept" an unlawful "brokerage" concession.

Since our proposal with respect to Section 2(c) clearly defines any prohibited "brokerage" as an "indirect price discrimination, " its "knowing" receipt or acceptance would be covered--as all other discriminations--by the express prohibitions directed at buyers or other recipient "persons" in Section 2(f). See discussions, infra. Hence the words "or to receive or accept" in 

Section 2(c) should be excised as redundant. 379

illegality, and could be legally justified by successful resort to any applicable exemptive proviso. The revised text, however would automatically preserve a seller's present privilege of directly refuting the offense in terms of its affirmative prohibition in each subsection--by justifying a brokerage concession as genuinely "for services rendered," for example, or by vindicating an allowance or service as furnished to each customer on "proportionally equal" terms.

THE BUYER'S LIABILITY Statutory Text 

Unlike the provisions in Subsections 2(a) through (e), addressed primarily to sellers who accord discriminatory concession, Section 2(f) declares it "unlawful for any person engaged in commerce, in the course of such commerce, knowingly to induce or receive a discrimination in price which is prohibited by this section." Although Robinson-Patman prohibitions were predominatly directed at discriminatory concessions coerced by powerful buyers, the legislative process relegated 

Section 2(f) to a minor role. Because the original Section 2 

of the Clayton Act struck at predatory price cuts destructive of competition as between the seller and other suppliers, no provision governing discriminations exacted by buyers was essential to accomplish the 1914 draftsmen's intent. Congress in 1936, though faced by the growth of chain stores and other mass merchandisers with marketing problems at an entirely 380

:D To modabere; afectores 30A aurgato sdi 1 menu nors jout

different level of the distribution process, chose to retain the existing statutory framework of old Section2--but restructured its prohibitions through textual revision. Yet the bills introduced in both Houses of Congress which ultimately became the Robinson-Patman Act reflected no reference to illegal practices on the part of buyers; the drafters concentrated on weakening the test of competitive "injury" qualifying the Clayton Act's prohibitions on discriminations by sellers. Discriminatory concessions which allegedly enabled big buyers to menace the economic survival of smaller rivals--the essential evil reached by the Act --were thus checked by circuitously fortifying the law's prohibitions on sellers granting the discriminations. In fact, the present prohibitions of Section 2(f) first emerged as an amendment offered in debate on the floor of the Senate. Passed by that chamber, and accepted by the House conferees, Section 2(f) represented a lastminute afterthought addition to the anti-discrimination law. 192/ 

As disclosed by its scant legislative history, the provision was designed to facilitate resistance to "sacrificial price cuts" through enabling the cornered seller to fend off an aggressive buyer by pleading the illegality of his excessive demands. 193/ 

In accord with this purpose, knowing receipt of an illicit concession became the core of the buyer's offense. 

192/ H. R. Rept. No. 2951, 74th Cong., 2d Sess. 8 (1936). 

193/ 80 Cong. Rec. 9419 (1936), 381

(2)

Despite its apparent significance in realizing the Actig objectives, Section 2(f) has been sparingly invoked. Compared with over 300 proceedings against sellers charged with according discriminatory concessions, the Federal Trade Commission has directly challenged coercive bargaining by buyers less than one-tenth this number of times.194/ While the first Commission action under 2(f) resulted in a dismissal of the charges, 195/ all subsequent proceedings culminated in orders to cease and desist that provoked neither detailed legal analysis nor even reached judicial review--until the Supreme Court's landmark Automatic Canteen decision recently promulgated the authoritative interpretation of Section 2(f) which to date prevails. 196/

The Automatic Canteen case concerned discriminatory concessions secured by a large buyer of confectioneries for resale through numerous vending machines. In administrative proceedings, the Commission's staff demonstrated that Automatic Canteen had obtained prices as much as one-third below its rivals. the Commission's view, the demonstration that the buyer knowingly received a favorable concession--at least when sufficiently sizeable to cause "injurious" market effects--established a prima In 194/ The Commission has to date issued twenty complaints under 

Section 2(f). 

195/ Bird & Son, Inc., 25 F.T.C. 548 (1937). 

196/ 346 U.S. 61 (1953), reversing 194 F. 2d 433 (7th Cir. 1951) which had affirmed the Commission's decision, 46 F.T.c. 861 (1950). 382

nyer biet bore UVa 14^

facie 2(f) violation. Thus cast on the buyer was the burden of disproving the illegality of the price he obtained. 

This construction of Section 2(f) was rejected by the Supreme Court. By the Court's interpretation, the statute firmly established knowing receipt of an illegal concession as the essential element of the buyer's offense. To transgress Section 2(f) a buyer not only must obtain an unlawful concession, but also must be reasonably aware of its illegality; conversely, exoneration followed from either a successful showing that the challenged price quotation was not in fact illegal, or that the buyer had no reason to know otherwise.197/

The issue of "knowledge" thus became focal. Mere "knowledge" by the buyer that his price was better than quotations to rivals, according to the Court, was in itself insufficient as a prima facie case of reasonable awareness of its illegality. Such a ruling necessarily would have required the buyer to prove affirmatively-in order to rebut the prima facie violation--that the price was not truly illegal because "non-injurious" to competition or otherwise "justified". However, the Court realized that a buyer charged with accepting a favorable differential could not ordinarily be expected to possess information of the seller's cost data adequate to negativing possible illegality through a "cost justification" 

197/ 346 U.S. at 70-71. 383

༄.་ ་་༥ 129

of the seller's price. Rather, the Court coined a rule of "convenience" and fairness by which the production of evidence as to cost savings, wherever appropriate as an element in the buyer's illegality, became the task of the Commission which was obviously better equipped than the buyer for investigating his supplier's books of account.

At any rate, the Commission might always

Contrariwise, the Commission in administering Section 2(f) was not saddled with an unattainable task. The Court's opinion traced sample patterns of factual proof adequate for inferring the buyer's illicit knowledge. concentrated sellers enjoying a significant degree of control over price, the exertion of countervailing force by some large and aggressive buyers bargaining for concessions can contribute materially to lower prices for all.206/ Not only is one reduction likely to spread; but each entering wedge enhances the negotiating position of other traders who can insist on equal concessions from the supplier with the ancient gambit of buying elsewhere unless he accedes. And unless competition on the buyers' level is wholly defunct, the ultimate consumer necessarily stands to benefit by lower prices. Legalistic impediments to this normal bargaining process, we think, might well deprive the public of gains that under effective competition it has a right to expect. 

It is too early to evaluate detailed implementation of the legal principles enunciated by the_Automatic Canteen decision. 

Although several pending complaints against buyers have been dismissed by the Federal Trade Commission on the basis of the

convenience" will doubtless be struck in equivalent fashion as to other issues critical in testing the validity of a price quotation under the Act. Further adjudications may well hold the buyer to proof as to his reasonable awareness of the potential destruction of competition that a challenged discrimination might generate at his level of distribution; contrariwise, the Commission might more suitably demonstrate any devitalization of the competitive process among the suppliers with whom the buyer dealt. Future interpretations of the requisite "knowledge" should not confer a legal bonus on professed or genuine ignorance by buyers, thereby prejudicing the operations of informed purchasing agents who range the market always on the alert for a lower price, Accordingly, while we fully approve the "rule of convenience" promulgated by Automatic Canteen as a future guide, its precise application to concrete cases cannot yet be told. 

Nevertheless, two textual deficiencies in Section 2(f) may produce incongruous results. While the statutory prohibitions on concessions by sellers come into play so long as one of the transactions comprising the discrimination occurs in interstate commerce, the counterpart liability of the buyer in Section 2(f) is doubly confined. The text of that subsection not only requires the charged buyer himself to be "engaged in commerce", but additionally that the illicit concession he obtains likewise occurs 11 "in the course of such commerce. As a consequence, a seller located in one state may offend Section 2(a) by granting 389

3. est sro når pred in poly work farden of Heat

concessions favoring a local buyer over out-of-state rivals, though a meticulous construction of the text in Section 2(f) might well permit his otherwise guilty intrastate buyer to escape. 

One other textual defect may produce anomalies. Although 

Section 2(a) and (e) reach the seller who accords "non-proportionally equal" "allowances' or "services or facilities" ancillary to the sale, Section 2(f)--strictly read strikes only at buyers who knowingly get an illegal discrimination "in price.' In fact, the Supreme Court's Automatic Canteen opinion adverted but fastidiously declined to resolve whether the "price" discriminations in 2(f) subsumed the discriminatory concessions governed by 11 Sections 2(d) and (e).210) Hence in some circumstances a buyer might finesse statutory liability entirely by extracting from his suppliers discriminatory concessions disguised as "allowances" or "services" rather than price.211/

We therefore propose some textual revision in the nature of minor perfecting amendments. In the first place, these drafting omissions are due to inadvertency in the course of statutory enactment, and fail to express the genuine legislative intent. In our 

210/ 346 U.S. 73 n. 14. 11 211/ The Commission, however, has in the past proceeded under Section 2(f) against recipients of forbidden "allowances.

view, furthermore, the law must not place a premium on manipulation or subterfuge. Hence we recommend that the text of Section 2(f) be revised so as to encompass beyond doubt the solicitation of the particular forms of discrimination affirmatively proscribed for sellers in Section 2(d) 212/ and (e).

Secondly, we advise revision in order to harmonize the statute's "interstate commerce" requirements for sellers and buyers, rendering the buyer's responsibility com- 213/ parable to the seller's.

We recommend that these proposals be implemented by revising the text of Section 2(f) to read: "(f) That it shall be unlawful for any person engaged in-semmerce--in-the-course-of-such eemmeree, knowingly to induce or receive any discrimination in-prise which is prohibited by this section.'

Apart from the civil prohibitions on discriminatory practices enacted by Sections 2(a) through (f), a parallel criminal ban largely duplicates and overlaps Section 2.

This criminal provision forbids any person, on pain of fine or impris,nment, (1) "to be a party to, or assist in, any transaction of sale, or contract to sell, which discriminates to his knowledge against competitors of the purchaser, in that any iscount, rebate, allowance, or advertising service charge is ranted to the purchaser over and above any discount, 11 rel ate, allowance, or advertising service charge available at the time ɗ such transaction to said competitors in respect o a sale of goods of like grade, quality, and quantity; (2) "to sell, or contract to sell, goods in any part of the United States at prices lower than those exacted [elsewhere] for the purpose of destroying competition, or eliminating a competitor;" or (3) "to sell, or contract to sell, goods at unreasonably low prices for the purpose of destroying competition or eliminating a competitor."
