Appended to the bill's civil provisions on the Senate floor, the criminal ban vaguely reiterated the Act's civil provisions and created an additional remedy for vindicating the law. 215/

The prohibitions of Section 3 readily divide into three segments: While Section 2 forbids consummated discriminatory concessions from sellers to buyers, the criminal ban reaches every "knowing" participant in any discriminatory sale or arrangement. In this way criminal responsibility extends beyond the contracting parties to anyone connected with negotiations for a discriminatory bargain, whether in fact performed or not. However, this provision penalizes only concessions which are in no way "available" to disfavored parties, and in any event comes into play only when goods of "like quantity", as well as "grade" 215 As explained by the Conference Report, Section 3 "sets up special prohibitions as to the particular offenses therein described and attaches to them also the criminal penalties therein provided. Section 3 illegalize essentially predatory practices: 

territorial price discriminations or arrangements and "unreasonably low prices" whenever quoted "for the purpose of" destroying competiton or eliminating competitors. 

Hence these latter provisions exceed the civil bans of Section 2(a) not by only reaching unexecuted as well as completed transactions, but also in part governing price cuts not necessarily discriminatory. Moreover, Section 3 is not expressly qualified by exculpatory provisos such as enable a civil respondent to "justify" a concession otherwise illegalized under Section 2. 

Although Congress authorized the Department of Justice and local United States attorneys to enforce Section 3, public enforcement organs have largely forsaken this law. In some few actions, charges preferred by the United States included some allegation of Section 3 violations as part of a wider antitrust 216/ offense.

has relegated its enforcement to private treble damage litigants. Such private damage litigation has generated serious problems of interpretation. While some courts have confessed serious doubt of Section 3's constitution- 222 221/ others have sustained the Act.

In our view, eighteen years of Section 3 enforcement have neither furthered the national interest nor realized Congressional purpose. On the one hand, the enforcement organs of the United States have abstained from invoking this provision. Contrariwise, private plaintiffs have emerged as the principal enforcers of its difficult prohibitions, freely rushing in where the Government perhaps fears to tread. At the same time--perhaps animated by these insights--Judicial decisions have relegated Section 3 to a precarious legal posture, facing further tests of basic constitutionality and recognition as an antitrust law.

Section 3's vague text has not yielded reasonable proscriptions on pricing practices detrimental to a competitive economy. To be sure, judicial construction of "knowledge" or "purpose" may well read into the literally absolute prohibitions of Section 3 those defenses, which may "justify" price differentials under Section 2.

provision, furthermore, applies only to discriminations among purchasers of equal quantities; the law may be avoided by an insignificant variation in the quantities sold. Since these strictures of Section 3 seem ineffectual against minimally astute businessmen, they threaten with criminal penalties only innocents unsuspecting of such odd quirks in the law. At the same time, Section 3's prohibition of predatory pricing is legally redundant; predatory price slashing obviously constitutes an attempt to monopolize, already rendered a crime by Section 2 of the Sherman Act, and an unfair method of 225/ competition, banned by Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act. 

Accordingly, we recommend Section 3's repeal as dangerous surplusage. Doubts besetting Section 3's constitutionality seem well founded; no gloss imparted by history or adjudication has settled the vague con- 226/ tours of this harsh criminal law. 

At all events, we believe that acceptance of Section 3 as a basis for private treble damage litigatim involves highly dubious statutory construction and, more important, finds support neither in the legislative intent nor overall antitrust policy. Hence any authority to enforce Section 3 should be restricted to responsible officials of the United States. Such drastic legislation threatening common and competitive pricing practices with the risk of criminality--if tolerated at all--should be accessible only to the Government, whose aim to discipline Section 3 in the public interest has been demonstrated by self-restraint.

FUNCTIONAL DISCOUNTS AND DELIVERED PRICING PRACTICES 

Apart from express provisions governing discriminatory practices, functional discounts and "delivered" prices pose significant legal issues under Robinson-Patman. Functional discounts traditionally represented a rough economic recognition by manufacturers of distributive services performed by various types of marketing intermediaries. 

At the same time, quotations of a "delivered" price that included transportation to the buyer's door evolved in heavy industry markets where high freight charges necessarily entered the prospective customer's calculations. These practices, though obviously engendering price differentials among a seller's customers, had attained no settled legal status prior to the Robinson-Patman amendments of 1936.227/ 

Robinson-Patman draftsmen strove to remedy the consequent uncertainty through express provisions defining the legal status of functional discounts and "delivered" prices under the proposed anti-price discrimination law. As reported from the House and Senate Judiciary 

Committees, the bills exempted any price differential related to the buyer's functional status in the distributive hierarchy.228/ In addition, 227/ See detailed discussion, infra. 

228/ H.R. Rep. No. 2287, 74th Cong., 2d Sess., pp. 8-9 (1936), in relevant part explained the pertinent provision which expressly permitted "price differentials depending solely upon whether the purchaser buys, 'for resale to wholesalers, to retailers, or to consumers, or for use in further manufacture,' and makes specific provision for the classification of customers in those several categories. This exemption is contained by implication in present section 2 of the Clayton Act, since it places no limit upon quantity differentials of any kind nor upon differentials not 399

the House bill expressly defined the concept of "price" as the amount received by the seller net of actual freight, thus rendering any quotation of a "delivered" price inherently discriminatory and legally suspect. 229/

The statute as enacted, however, retained no reference to functional discounts or "delivered" pricing. Hence these significant pricing practices occupy a pecularly shifting legal status, dependent on the uncertain interplay of several statutory considerations rather than any one decisive provision.

FUNCTIONAL DISCOUNTS 

Functional discounts are a traditional pricing technique by suppliers to accord some monetary recognition of distributive intermediaries' services. When a producer wishes to shift the burdens of warehousing, delivery, and financial risk in servicing scattered small accounts, a scaled system of price concessions can compensate various middlemen for assuming these tasks. The typical functional

mukoing

or trade discount system provides for graduated discounts to customers classified according to task in the distribution chain--the seller's schedule specifies percentage reductions to wholesalers, jobbers, and retailers in diminishing amounts. Since such discounts prevail irrespective of the quantities involved in a particular transaction or even aggregate volume over a period of time, they reflect rough and long-range estimates by the supplier of the economic advantage in dealing with broad customer classes performing characteristic marketing functions.

Functional discounts enjoyed a sheltered legal position under the original Clayton Act. Since the 1914 draftsmen's concern centered on predatory pricing by powerful sellers, functional differentials among various buyers warranted no express exemption. Nevertheless, the Federal Trade Commission in two early cases challenged functional discounts as illegal price discriminations under Section 2.230/ But these rulings were soon eclipsed by judicial determinations which declined to extend Section 2's bans to discriminations affecting the buyer level, and enunciated dicta subsuming all price differentials under the supplier's assertedly unqualified privilege to deal on any terms of his own choice.231/ Though this rationale was superseded by

Although the Commission has quite recently projected the "price by use" theory beyond its Standard Oil ruling--challenging functional discounts accorded to genuine wholesalers who resold to retail outlets which they partly owned or controlled243/--the pending Standard Oil proceeding epitomizes the equivocal character of functional discounts under the Robinson-Patman Act.

Such doctrines threaten a serious clash with legitimate marketing techniques as well as broader antitrust objectives. In our view, the "price by use" doctrine anomalously compels affirmative discrimination against a substantial class of distributors. The integrated middleman typically relieves his supplier not only of risk, storage, transportation, and administrative services, but has invested substantial capital in the facilities and equipment essential to performance of these tasks. Genuine marketing functions, moreover, benefit the supplier equally whether performed by a "non-integrated" pure whole- 

241/ (cont.) supplied by wholesalers may render such retailers his "purchasers" entitled to equal price treatment under the Robinson-Patman Act, thereby subjecting even a one-price policy as between these wholesalers and other retailers to legal risks.

saler, an integrated distributor selling only through his owned outlets, or a "split-function" middleman reselling partially at each of two levels; conversely, the economic costs of capital investment and marketing services by each is the same. A legal rule disqualifying an integrated distributor from discounts recognizing all his genuine functions deprives him not only of rewards for distributive services performed, but also denies him a return on invested plant and equipment. In short, a rigid "price by use" rule effectively discriminates against the integrated buyer by compelling him to perform part of his marketing functions free. 

Such an approach to functional pricing, we think, creates artificial impediments to the development of efficient distribution techniques. Analogous to the economic impact of undiscerning "brokerage clause" enforcement, this aspect of the Commission's administration as to functional discounts unjustifiably penalizes one form of marketing organization for the benefit of another--at the consumer's expense.

The "price by use" doctrine enacts a preferred position for the non-integrated distributor who is left free to collect his due functional rewards, but gains an unwarranted competitive advantage over the integrated rival disfavored by law. And to the extent the 

Commission's interpretations prescribe economic discrimination against integrated marketers, the true value of their function is pocketed by the seller rather than passed along to the consumer through the pressures of competition. 408

In our view, imposing on any dual supplier a legal responsibility for the resale prices of his independent distributors equally contradicts basic antitrust policies. Under the Commission's rulings, any seller serving the market through parallel channels of distribution risks violation of the price discrimination law. For if an intermediate distributor underprices the supplier's quotations in competition for sales to the retail or ultimate consumer levels, the Commission's rationale charges the seller with consequent market effects. It is of course true that the seller might insure his safety under the Robinson-Patman Act by controlling the prices at which his distributors resell. But resale-price fixing, though at present enjoying a qualified exemption from antitrust prohibitions is, in our view, incompatible with the basic tenets of a free and competitive economy. 244/ What is more, the almost invariably requisite policing and reporting mechanisms for the detection of price cutters may incur antitrust illegality, entirely apart from the legal status of the resale-price agreement itself. 245/ 

The supplier's theoretical alternative of consciously adhering, in his sales to retail customers, 244/ See discussion, supra, at pp. 306 et seq. Moreover, resale-pricing arrangements between suppliers and distributors simultaneously competing for sales to the retail level might be deemed "horizontal" or as "between competitors", and hence outside the protection of the Miller-Tydings and McGuire Acts. In sum, we think that the presence of variously organized marketing units aggressively competing is an essential force in generating the pressures for eliminating wastes and rendering more effective service to the consumer. No one pattern of marketing organization should be inherently preferred or prejudiced by the anti-price discrimination law; hence we disapprove the rule of "price by use" as unjustifiably discriminatory in favor of non-integrated distributors against 248/ integrated marketers. Furthermore, we deem resale-price fixing repugnant to broader antitrust objectives; for that reason the concept

of the dual supplier's responsibility for the resale pricing tactics of his distributors--an inevitable incentive to resale-price control-must be deplored. With such anomalous implications for effective competition in the distribution process, the current legal administration of functional discounts, in our opinion, warrants change. 

However, the demonstrated incongruities determining the present legal status of functional discounts, in our view, are not effectively resolved by statutory amendment. Modern marketing techniques have inspired a multitude of disparate distributive organizations not readily susceptible to precise classification by title or name. 

Traditionally wholesalers, jobbers, and retailers comprised independent successive links between the manufacturer and his ultimate market, each responsible for a clearly defined set of duties. Today's marketing methods, however, have disrupted this former symmetry. Brokers and jobbers of numerous kinds contribute a genuine service, though assuming far from the full complement of distributive functions which the traditional full-line wholesaler performed. Contrariwise, emerging mass merchandisers of various sorts have fused many functions formerly the task of several autonomous enterprisers, and cut across and overlap distributive levels where other intermediaries operate. The consequent proliferation of modern marketing units defies neat nomenclature and descriptive labels. Such complexities not only would foil the formulation

Correspondingly, a genuine functional discount or not whether the buyer is integrated does not constitute a discrimination in the economic sense, since it merely compensates the distributor's functions on the basis of long-range estimates of savings accruing to the supplier who need not invest in facilities or services to perform the operation himself. A concept of "discrimination" confined to price differentials among economic equals on the buyer level would necessarily terminate the statute's control over predatory price cuts among non-competing customers, where destruction of the seller's rivals is the crux of the charge.251/

DELIVERED PRICING 

Characteristic of the geographical range encompassed by the American system of distribution, many sellers supply distant markets through "delivered" prices which reflect some element of freight. Unlike an f.o.b. mill quotation which. sets a price effective at the seller's plant, "delivered" prices incorporate transportation charges and hence refer to the point of delivery, 1.e. the buyer's location. Such "delivered" price quotations are customarily computed by three essential formulas. In single or multiple basing-point pricing, one or several cities serve as referents for a "base" price to which is added the actual freight charge to the buyer's door; in zone pricing, the country is carved into geographic sectors within which all buyers pay the same price 415

of going guten sepeti

regardless of location, though the price varies among the several zones; a uniform "delivered" price system treats the entire nation as a single market, charging the identical price to all buyers anywhere. "Delivered" pricing has faced frequent challenge under the antitrust laws. In a series of proceedings against trađe associations during the 1920's, "delivered" pricing methods constituted one facet of allegedly concerted and cooperative efforts among industry members which the Department assailed under Section 1 of the Sherman Act. In the Linseed case the

Supreme Court condemned an industry-wide zone pricing system utilized in a wider concerted plan to stabilize prices and terminate competition among the participants. subsequent. The ultimate fate of "delivered" prices under the 

Sherman Act thus hinged on their market context: "Delivered" pricing systems were condemned if instrumental in price-fixing conspiracies but were acquitted otherwise. 

More complex was the two-pronged attack by the Federal 

Trade Commission on "delivered" pricing practices. Since the unreasonable restraints forbidden by Section 1 of the Sherman 

Act constitute "unfair methods of competition" proscribed by Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act, the Commission in several proceedings challenged via Section 5 industry-wide "delivered" pricing system used thwart price competition.

Cement opinion exposed the vice of the pricing practice as "injury" to the vigor of the competitive process. For the participant sellers who by meticulous matching of each other's quotations had succeeded in throttling all price rivalry among themselves. While this finding of "injury" among sellers spelled illegality under the price discrimination provisions of Section 2, the simultaneous violation of the Federal Trade Commission Act--as in the earlier Sherman Act cases--hinged on forbidden concerted activity among the industry members. 

This tight collusion was evidenced not only by persistent unnatural rigidity in the industry's price movements, but corroborated by business reprisals and punitive boycotts for enforcing strict adherence to an artificially maintained high level of price.

More recently, the Supreme Court delivered an antitrust opinion which indirectly illuminates the validity of "delivered" prices under the Sherman and Federal Trade Commission Acts. 

The Rigid Steel Conduit case, an aftermath of the Cement adjudication, had evidently imperiled even individually quoted "delivered" prices, provided only the seller's rivals, though acting with equal autonomy, likewise adopted that pricing method.267/ method. 267/ In the Theatre Enterprises decision, however, the 

Supreme Court pinpointed the "crucial question" in an antitrust conspiracy case as whether the suspected action among competing sellers "stemmed from independent decision or from an agreement, In the Court's view, "business behavior" tacit or express. "268/ was certainly "admissible circumstantial evidence from which the fact finder may infer agreement."

But the Court disavowed any holding that "proof of parallel business behavior conclusively establishes agreement, or phrased differently, that such behavior itself constitutes a Sherman Act offense. Circumstantial evidence of consciously parallel behavior may have made heavy inroads into the traditional judicial attitude

toward conspiracy; but 'conscious parallelism' has not yet read conspiracy out of the Sherman Act entirely. #269/ Basic to any valid legal appraisal of "delivered" pricing is a recognition of the complex role performed by geographic pricing in a national market. Particularly in the sale of basic homogeneous commodities, where transportation costs mount high and the buyer's susceptibility to advertising slogans and fancy wrappings runs low, rivalry among suppliers must consider the substantial competitive advantages of proximity to the consuming market. To compete successfully for sales in a distant area, sellers must quote prices to prospective customers which--after taking into account the element of freight--cannot exceed the offer of a more favorably situated rival. A disadvantageously situated seller might, of course, neutralize a rival's transportation advantage by initiating a corresponding cut in price at the mill only applicable to distant customers in the competitor's marketing territory. Or he could reduce his mill price to all customers in order to tempt those located in remote areas. 

But several factors may persuade the seller to prefer an appropriate method of "delivered" pricing over outright cuts in base price, available to all buyers or confined to those

remote. "Delivered" price policies, for example, permit effective national advertising campaigns, and also facilitates realistic comparison with geographically dispersed alternative sources of supply. A "delivered" price may also arise where freight charges among a myriad of dispersed local markets appear too slight to warrant separate calculation for each individual sale. Contrariwise, the alternative reduction in base price to some customers--discriminatory by definition-may arouse not only hostile public relations, but also invite legal inquiries under the anti-price discrimination law. 

Rather than reducing base prices to all customers for the sake of gaining a few sales in the distant market, a seller may well decide to meet competition as he finds it, and quote "delivered" prices for invading markets away from home.

On the other hand, a "delivered" pricing system is equally susceptible to employment as a collusive device for price stabilization. Where sellers desire to suppress price competition, a strictly enforced compulsory "delivered" pricing formula among all industry members can materially contribute to success. A uniformly maintained industry-wide system of "delivered" quotations immediately simplifies each participant's pricing structure; he merely sets a base price which the formula automatically converts into the actual invoice cost to each customer. At the same time, rather than necessitating a complex series of individual freight computations for numerous buyers, 425

Harp 7- 

#njpentabopata potra-Mae ads robes balchipat llegid ng enad urbedbok asd wick nth calow wo% a yafeting No difon

the "delivered" price list quickly discloses the effective prices that everyone pays. Such simplification facilitates mutual surveillance of business rivals' price offers, enabling any departure from a fixed price to be quickly caught. To this extent a "delivered" pricing system eases the task of policing and supervision which is indispensable to effective collusive stabilization. 

Hence "delivered" pricing, as other methods of price quotation, standing alone is wholly equivocal; its business significance derives from the market context in which it appears, The Nation's antitrust policy is not necessarily advanced by the alternative, enforced f.o.b. mill pricing on the part of all sellers. Collusion can flourish under f.o.b. system as well. True, the complicated freight calculations needed to compute the actual laid down cost of goods to each customer might sufficiently complicate every competitor's pricing schedule so as to foil mutual surveillance, foment uncertainty, and thus aggravate the task of maintaining adherence to a rigged price. On the other hand, f.o.b. pricing may create other handicaps to competitive price movements. 

If a seller--unless he wishes to quote more favorable base prices applicable only to distant customers--must reduce his base price to all in order to compete in rival territory, the incentive mounts to refrain from geographic forays--particularly when volume close to home is heavy and the potential business to be 426

gained afar is comparatively slight. In this way, compulsory f.o.b. pricing may contribute to insulating each seller's home market from its rivals' invasion. 

Given the conditions that make for collusive pricing, a further dubious consequence may attend compulsory f.o.b.. 

Whatever else, "delivered" pricing encourages the interpenetration of geographic markets, thereby creating wide opportunity for that competitive price-shading which keeps terms of sale supple and responsive to market shifts. On the other hand, a compulsory f.o.b. price would reveal any sale in a distant area as due to a mill price concession by the unfavorably located seller, and immediately stimulate the conspirators' efforts to dam the leak in the collusive front. By thus increasing the risks of any attempted individual price reductions, a compulsory f.o.b. mill pricing system may in the long run conduce to an artificial average price pegged at even higher levels than otherwise. 

Since all methods of price quotations are economically neutral, we approve the Sherman Act adjudications which impartially condemn "delivered" pricing--as other business arrangements--only when part of a conspiracy. In the sale of basic homogeneous commodities, informed buyers will never pay more to one seller than to his rival for identical and standardized goods; some tendency toward price uniformity is hence inevitable, and may indicate effective competition 427

and successful collusion with equal ease. But as a practical matter, the disciplined maintenance of a "delivered" pricing system over a period of time as an instrument of collusion will disclose some overt index of agreement among participating sellers. Persistent standardization and surveillance of freight charges, delivery methods, service extras and discounts is a complex task; an industry-wide price rigidity is not, likely to persist over the years unaccompanied by the arbitrary enforcement aids that spell conspiracy. Although the law may from circumstantial evidence infer the fact of agreement in the use of "delivered" pricing systems, as in other price-fixing conspiracies or concerted efforts to abolish competition, the crucial question," as formulated by the Supreme Court in the 

Theatre Enterprises case, must be whether the challenged conduct "stemmed from independent decision or from an agree- "270/ ment, tacit or express.

We accept the Supreme Court's latest pronouncements as pertinent to collusive "delivered" pricing systems as to other concerted trade restraints.

Here the Court concededly has "never held that proof of parrallel business behavior conclusively establishes agreement or, phrased differently, that such behavior itself constitutes a Sherman 271/

We equally approve the Federal Trade Commission's recent analysis of "delivered" pricing under the Robinson-Patman Act.

It is undeniable that all "delivered" prices, however computed, might be regarded as discriminatory in a purely theoretical economic sense. A "delivered" price necessarily means that some customers, depending on relative geographic location, receive the same goods but get less transportation than distant buyers receive per dollar paid; conversely, the seller charges "delivered" prices that do not correspond with the varying freight costs incurred. In the world of business, however, such theoretical discriminations in a "delivered" price must as a practical matter leave prospective buyers cold. Their choice among rival sources of supply, as well as their own competitive position versus others in further resale, depends on the actual, laid down price which they must pay out when invoiced for the purchased goods, not on the seller's 272/ costs or net returns. 

Since the paramount legal inquiry 272 This obviously differs from the denial to an integrated buyer of functional discounts or "brokerage" concessions, thereby depriving him of returns on capital invested in the requisite distributive facilities. In the theoretical "discrimination" arising from a "delivered" price which does not reward a customer's geographical proximity, it cannot be assumed that he invested in a location near a particular supplier's mill in anticipation of a freight advantage. 

As a matter of fact, in view of the traditionally established "delivered" pricing in basic industries which doubtless enters into fabricators' investment calculations for plant location, the opposite assumption is equally plausiblē--entirely apart from the many other factors attracting buyers of basic industrial goods to locate. near consuming markets. 429

of the Robinson-Patman Act centers on the "injurious" handicaps imposed by discriminations on "disfavored" customers, it is this "actual price" paid by buyers which determines their competitive standing vis a vis each other, and hence must be the significant index of legality under the Act. 

In our view, therefore, the Federal Trade Commission's recent rulings in the National Lead and Chain Institute decisions accord with the dominant purposes of the Robinson- 

Patman enactment as well as explicit Congressional intent.273/

Under the Commission's "actual price" criterion, only signigicant disparities in laid-down cost could prejudice individual buyers' competitive vigor sufficiently to invalidate a "delivered" price as illegally discriminatory.274/ The "mill net" alternative 

273/ In each case the Commission issued a cease and desist order on the basis of a Federal Trade Commission Act Section 5 violation rooted in collusive pricing conduct. To the extent these orders deny sellers the right independently to quote "delivered" prices in meeting rivals' offers, they not only fail to promote effective competition, but also may be vulnerable as prohibiting what the law permits. See pages 333, et. seq.; 

359, et seq. supra. 

274/ For example, a nationwide uniform "delivered" price, as indicated in the Staley opinion, would not create a legally cognizable discrimination at all; a zone pricing system would not discriminate as among buyers within each zone;' and the significance of any discrimination inhering in single or multiple basing-point systems would be coextensive only with the disparities in actual price paid by each buyer. 430

theory--either for ascertaining or measuring legally cognizable discrimination--would render all "delivered" prices inherently discriminatory and legally suspect. Such an interpretation, however, flouts the unmistakable intent of the draftsmen who repudiated a provision designed to brand all "delivered" pricing discriminatory per se.

We recognize that the equivocal nature of "delivered" pricing casts a peculiar responsibility on antitrust enforcement organs. Whether prosecuted as Sherman Act conspiracies in restraint of trade, unfair methods of competition under the 

Federal Trade Commission Act, or even Robinson-Patman price discriminations, the vice common to all "delivered" pricing systems has been their collusive employment for industry-wide regimentation throttling price competition among the partici pants.275/ 

Since the self-same geographic pricing practice spurred by competition in one business context may be conceived in collusion in another market structure, necessarily a careful appraisal of all significant economic factors must precede resolution of the crucial question: does competition or collusion animate a given "delivered" price? 

In our view, the Sherman Act adjudications of "delivered" pricing systems, by focussing the legal inquiry on the element 

275/ Compare note 260 supra. 431

CA

of conspiracy and concert of action, tacit or express, accord with overall antitrust policy and face up to the essential economic problem. 

Where "delivered" prices are pursued as discriminations under the Robinson-Patman Act, however, highly complex questions of policy arise. Such proceedings rarely resemble the issues typically adjudicated under that Act. Unlike the customary "injury" to competing sellers or disfavored customers victimized by the challenged discriminatory practice, the crux of the matter in "delivered" pricing cases becomes the devitalization of the competitive process through collusion among the discriminators themselves.276/ 

Whatever genuine "injury" any of the seller's customers sustain is essentially the characteristic burden of monopoly borne not only by all buyers, but also by the general consuming public, whenever sellers conspire to abolish competition so that stabilized and artificially high price levels are maintained, Since this sort of "injury" is the inevitable consequence of every pricefixing scheme, any element of price discrimination in "delivered" price quotations remains a largely incidental consequence of the real antitrust offense.

Rational antitrust policy may thus well be fostered if all "delivered" pricing systems suspected of collusion are pursued 276/ Ibid. 432

#tent I ng odmenitos MERID ideekidel oda rebad Pjestad ež "yulub" to Fios

as conspiracies in restraint of trade under the Sherman Act.'

It is true that the Robinson-Patman and Federal Trade Commission statutes by obviating all proof of conspiracy invite shortcut prosecutions of a "delivered" price. But law enforcement through indirection must necessarily encourage statutory distortion and foment legal confusion, frustrating any rational counsel and thwarting all voluntary efforts to comply. While in no event, for example, should collusive price-matching by conspiring sellers be defensible as "good faith meeting of competition" to justify otherwise forbidden discriminations under the Robinson-Patman Act, this result should not hinge on strained Section 2(b) interpretations--such as a legal disqualification of any "systematic" or "regular" meeting of competition--which may arise to plague genuine competitive reductions in price. In such a case, collusion ought to constitute the accusation, rather than a rebuttal to a defense against a misplaced discrimination charge. 

At any rate, since any competitive "unfairness" generated by "delivered" pricing resides primarily in collusion and possibly in unjustified price discrimination, no discernible antitrust policy is subserved by penalizing it as an "unfair method of competition" forbidden by Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act, unless the elements of conspiracy or indefensible discrimination appear. 433

bovegrijk jy we 

The Ledeng gas 32

In sum, we recommend that the law carefully distinguish between competitive and collusive "delivered" pricing. It is our view that the aims of overall antitrust policy are fostered when sellers are free to meet competition in distant markets by quoting "delivered" prices to equalize more favorably situated rivals' advantages in freight. Conversely, however, basic antitrust considerations firmly dictate that "delivered" pricing employed to effectuate price-fixing conspiracies be relentlessly pursued. This delicate adjustment, should be guided by recent adjudications defining the essence of antitrust conspiracy as well as the legally relevant concept of "price."

At the same time, since under current legal doctrines the test of "delivered" prices as an instrument of business rivalry often comes in the context of a "meeting competition" justification, the strengthening of the Section 2(b) proviso recommended by the Committee would correspondingly foster the competitive "delivered" pricing which comports with overall antitrust policy. 434

V. ANTITRUST ADMINISTRATION AND ENFORCEMENT 

Closely related to substantive antitrust issues are problems of administration and enforcement. 

These involve not only the Department of Justice and the Federal Trade Commission but also treble damage litigants whose suits necessarily figure in any enforcement study. 

The Department, on the one hand, must first gather evidence to determine if complaints received signal probable violations. Once facts are garnered, then at hand is the decision to proceed and, if suit is to be pressed whether civilly, criminally, or both. Relevant here becomes what penalties or equitable relief may be appropriate as well as the change of avoiding trial through consent settlement. Should consent negotiations fail, crucial becomes means for assuring quick and fair trial of disputed issues. After trial is completed, the 

Department must consider how best to enforce its judgment as well as the extent future violations may be avoided through advance clearance and release.

The Commission, on the other hand, faces some of these same problems as well as issues involving voluntary compliance, such as trade practice conferences and informal settlement processes. Together, both the

Commission and the Department, of course, must best 436

3. V! ders &shows wif .. C 

1: '.

divide their diverse processes and skills to avoid duplication and maximize effective enforcement.

In addition to these government enforcement agencies, also significant are private treble damage proceedings, These may pose problems of their own, such as the practicability of suit, whether damages shall be trebeled in all cases or only where violations are willful, whether the United States may recover damages and, finally, whether private antitrust proceedings should be governed by diverse state statutes of limitation or some uniform federal law. 

All these issues this Report considers.

THE DEPARTMENT OF JUSTICE 

A. Antitrust Investigations

The inevitable generality of some statutory antitrust prohibitions renders facts of paramount importance. Accordingly, effective enforcement requires full and comprehensive investi gation before formal proceedings, civil or criminal, are commenced. Incomplete investigation may mean proceedings not justified by more careful search and study. Public retreat by the prosecutor may then be difficult, if not impossible, and the result may be a futile trial exhausting the resources of the litigants and increasing court congestion. Thus the adequacy of investigatory processes can make or break any enforcement program, 437

Present procedures enable the Department of Justice to employ compulsory process to obtain both documentary and testimonial evidence at every stage of criminal and civil antitrust proceedings--except during the investigative stage of a matter in which civil proceedings are, from the outset, contemplated.

Where indictment is contemplated, the federal grand jury is equipped with ample powers to permit the fullest investigation. The grand jury subpoena may be used to compel the discovery of all documentary material reasonably required as well as the testimony of witnesses under oath. 

In the investigation of civil matters, on the other hand, the Department must: (a) depend upon the voluntary cooperation of those under investigation; (b) file a civil complaint and make use of discovery processes under the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure; or (c) make use of the grand jury.

These procedures do not satisfy civil enforcement needs. 

Voluntary cooperation of parties under investigation has often been sufficient, but compulsory processes are required in some cases. Moreover, a government agency should not be in a position of sole dependence upon voluntary cooperation for discharge of its responsibilities. 438

4. J. $ ..

Filing a civil complaint enables resort to the compulsory discovery processes, under the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, such as interrogatories, motions to produce documents, depositons, etc. These methods have been extensively used in antitrust cases and provide discovery powers almost as sweeping as a grand jury. But they come into play only after a complaint has been filed. Thus the Department cannot utilize them to determine whether the institution of formal proceedings is warranted. Moreover, the filing of a skeleton complaint in hopes that the Federal Rules' discovery procedures will unearth facts essential to a valid accusation is unwise. For we agree with the Judicial Conference of the 

United States that no plaintiff, not even the Government, may "pretend to bring charges in order to discover whether actual charges should be brought." 2/ These Rules "were not intended 

1/ Interrogatories, according to Rule 33 (F.R.Civ. P.) "may relate to any matters which can be inquired into under 

Rule 26(b)." Rule 26(b) (F.R.Civ. P.), in turn, provides for depositions pending trial "regarding any matter, not privileged, which is relevant to the subject matter involved in the pending examination ***, [N]ot ground for objection," 26(b) continues, is "that the testimony will be inadmissible at the trial if---[it] appears reasonably calculated to lead to the discovery of admissible evidence' Beyond interrogatories and depositions, discovery and production of documents are available to secure evidence "not privileged" and "material to any matter involved in the action (F.R.Civ. P. 34)

to make the courts an investigatory adjunct to the Department of Justice. 11 3/ The last alternative is the grand jury. Its use where civil proceedings are contemplated from the outset cannot be justified on the purely formal ground that the Sherman Act defines a criminal offense appropriate for consideration by a grand jury even though it may later be determined that equitable relief is more appropriate. In reality, resort to grand jury in essentially civil investigations stems from lack of an adequate civil discovery alternative. 

We believe that the use of criminal processes other than for investigation with an eye toward indictment and prosecution is an abuse of the grand jury. Such action subverts the Department's policy of proceeding criminally only against flagrant offenses and debases the law by tarring respectable citizens with the brush of crime when their deeds involve no criminality.

We recognize that the Department has been handicapped and accept the Judicial Conference conclusion that present civil investigative machinery is inadequate for effective antitrust enforcement. The problem is, therefore, to devise a civil discovery process for use where civil proceedings are initially contemplated and voluntary cooperation 

3/ Id. 67. 

4/ Report of the Judicial Conference on Frocedure in Antitrust and Other Protracted Cases, 13 F.R.D. 62, 67. 440

by those under investigation is lacking. 5/ We reject the proposal for legislation authorizing the 

Department of Justice to issue the type of administrative subpoena typically employed by regulatory agencies. Unlike the Federal Trade Commission, for example, the Department of Justice is entrusted only with law enforcement. The grant of subpoena powers suggests broader regulatory powers, structural reorganization, a system of hearing officers and a panoply of administrative procedural protections which the Committee is not prepared to recommend. We would, in addition, disapprove any subpoena power that would permit prosecuting officers to summon sworn oral testimony by placing businessmen under oath indiscriminately. Such authority is alien to our legal traditions, readily susceptible to grave abuse and, moreover, seems unnecessary. 

To enable fair and effective enforcement by the Department, the Committee recommends legislation, applying only to relevant documents possessed by parties under investigation which would: 

1. Authorize the Attorney General, in a civil antitrust investigation, to issue and have served upon any corporation, partnership or association a Civil Investigative Demand. This could require the production of existing correspondence and other business records and data or copies thereof, not privi- 

5/ See e.g., compulsory process granted the Federal Trade 

Commission, 15 USC (1952) 49. 447

leged, in the possesion of the party served. Such documents must, however, be relevant to particular antitrust offenses stated to be under investigation. In addition, the Demand must describe the records and data sought with reasonable specificity, so as fairly to identify the material demanded, as well as specify a reasonable time for its production. 

2. Create the office of Custodian in the Department of 

Justice and require that all documents produced in response to a Demand be delivered to the Custodian or his deputy at some convenient place. The Custodian would be charged with receiving and preserving all such documents. He should make them available only to the Antitrust Division or Federal Trade Commission personnel participating in the pending investigation and, under reasonable conditions, to representatives of the corporation, partnership or association that has delivered them. 

3. Restrict the use of documents produced in response to a Demand to (a) the pending investigation, (b) submission before a grand jury, (c) Antitrust Division or Federal Trade Commission proceedings that may ensue; and require that they be promptly restored to their rightful owner thereafter. 

4. Vest the United States District Court for the Judicial district in which the recipient maintains its principal place of business with power to entertain motions; 442

Demand; or (iii) the adequacy and specificity of the description of the material required to be produced. 

If the Demand does not conform to the statute the Court would have the power to modify or set it aside entirely. 

5. Provide that a Demand may be served and enforced by the courts against any corporation, partnership or association subject to the jurisdiction of the United States. 

6. Provide that whether compliance with a Demand is effected in response to a court order or to the Demand itself or informally, all Constitutional and statutory safeguards and immunities shall be fully preserved. 

The Attorney General should resort to this Demand only after a specific request for production has proved unsatisfactory. Its availability should end the necessity for utilizing the grand jury process in civil antitrust investigations. Thus, it complements, not supersedes, the grand jury, which retains its proper role in criminal investigations. 443

Complementing grand jury recourse, this proposed Demand should enable the Department to gather quickly the wealth of data needed to determine probable violation.7/ 

B. The Decision to Proceed

Once evidence is secured, then at hand is the decision (1) to proceed at all and, if the facts are deemed to warrant action, (2) should it be criminal, civil, or both. In either event, (3) what relief should be demanded. Finally (4) involving all these issues, what procedures best assure each decision is intelligently made.

The burdens of antitrust proceedings on all parties are generally so severe that litigation should be contemplated only after investigation discloses a probable offense and only if the Department, after evaluation of all probable defenses, is convinced that effective relief is obtainable. With this overall guide, a balanced enforcement program should include national and local restraints within the reach of federal antitrust. 

Industry-wide and regional proceedings of course play a crucial I/

Report. Instead the Committee may read Messrs. Clark and 

Rostow's views in the accompanying Comments.

enforcement role. Not to be minimized, however, is the importance of striking down "local" restraints within the reach of the commerce clause. Initially, such market clogs may be of great importance to the people and the economy of a particular area. Often these restraints, carried out by small concerns without experienced antitrust counsel, are of a flagrant type which antitrust compliance has largely removed from national markets. Since state antitrust laws, with few exceptions, have not been fully developed or enforced, national antitrust policy must make adequate provision for dealing with all market restraints within its ambit. 

The decision to proceed made, then at hand is the choice of civil or criminal attack. The Sherman Act, inevitably perhaps, is couched in language, broad and general. Modern business patterns, moreover, are so complex that market effects of proposed conduct are only imprecisely predictable. Thus, it may be difficult for today's businessman to tell in advance whether projected actions will run afoul of the Sherman Act's criminal strictures. With this hazard in mind, we believe that criminal process should be used only where the law is comparatively clear and the facts reveal a flagrant offense and plain intent unreasonably to restrain trade. 

The Assistant Attorney General in charge of the Antitrust Division has advised the Committee that, "In general, the following types of offenses are prosecuted criminally: (1) price-fixing; (2) other violations of the Sherman Act where there is proof of a specific intent to restrain trade or to monopolize; (3) a less easily defined category of cases 446

which might generally be described as involving proof of use of predatory practices (boycotts, for example) to accomplish the objective of the combination or conspiracy; (4) the fact that a defendant has previously been convicted of or adjudged to have been, violating the antitrust laws warrants indictment for a second offense.

There are other factors taken into account in determining whether to seek an indictment in cases that may not fall precisely in any of these categories. 

The Division feels free to seek an indictment in any case where a prospective defendant has knowledge that practices similar to those in which he is engaging have been held to be in violation of the Sherman Act in a prior civil suit against other persons. "It should also be added that in determining to bring a case on the criminal side, the Department does not foreclose itself from filing a companion civil case against the same defendants. This is done in situations where it is believed that in addition to the criminal penalties, effective injunctive relief can be obtained. 

On the other hand, we avoid filing civil actions where nothing more is involved than ordinary price-fixing, because an injunction against price-fixing adds nothing to the injunctions already contained in the Sherman Act itself."

He has also informed us that before a criminal action is brought the following procedures are currently invoked: "In the first instance a recommendation is made by the attorney in charge of the particular investigation. If he is in the field, his recommendation is then reviewed by his field office chief. It is then reviewed by the appropriate litigation section chief in Washington. The section chief's decision is reviewed by the first or second assistant to the Assistant Attorney 

General and by the Assistant Attorney General.

Review at the various levels is effected not only by oral conferences, but also by written memoranda of facts detailing the evidence against each proposed defendant.' 11 447

PLET COMES

The Committee generally endorses the first three policy standards for the use of the criminal process, with the reservation that all criminal cases should be confined to instances where proof of violation is clear and the law settled. Price fixing and boycott cases, for example, have, on occasion, involved proceedings wholly without precedent where the parties engaging in the practice under attack have done so in reliance upon the inapplicability of the antitrust laws. Examples would be the criminal cases attacking agreements with respect to commission 8 rates of real estate brokers,- the practice of medicine and the business of group health2/ and premium rates on fire insurance.10/ 

The Committee believes that use of the criminal process in these cases was improper.

We also have grave doubts as to the validity of the fourth policy standard set forth above--that a second offense necessarily warrants indictment. If confined to cases in which the second offense is substantially the same as the first, no serious objection arises. However, the idea of a second offender in antitrust has no statutory basis and may not be relevant in every instance. L 8/ United States v. National Association of Real Estate Boards, 80 F. Supp. 350 (D.C., D. Col.. 1948). 

2/ United States v. American Medical Ass'n., 317 U.S. 519 (1943). 

10/ United States v. South-Eastern Underwriters Ass'n., 322 U„S. 

533 (1944). 448

The possible offenses under the antitrust laws are so numerous that any second offender criteria must take full account of the large number of quite different offenses under the law, the differences in decisional law at different times, the intentions of the parties and similar considerations. Unless this is done, substantial injustice may often result.

In sum, then, we reject as impracticable the proposal to by statute draw a line between those violations to be attacked, on the one hand, by indictment and, on the other, by civil proceeding. 

Sufficient, instead, are general enforcement criteria to insure criminal action will be limited to outright market price-fixing, divisions of territory, boycotts, racketeering and the like when such misdeeds occur in areas where antitrust coverage is well settled.

II Closely related to the question of proceeding civilly or criminally is the problem of relief appropriate to the violation charged. To be decided in a criminal case is the penalty to be demanded and, in a civil proceeding, what relief, in light of the violation alleged, will best "restore workable competition in the market."11/ Practicularly relevant here are two specific issues: first, should the present $5,000 ceiling for criminal fines be 

11/ United States v. United Shoe Machinery Corp., 110 F. Supp. 

295, (D. Mass., 1953) aff'd per curiam 347 U.S. 521 (1954). 449

raised? And second, what criteria should govern demands for dissolution, divorcement and divestiture? 

Should the Present Criminal Penalties be Increased? 

Since 1890, when the Sherman Act was passed, the maximum fine for a criminal violation has been $5,000. 

At the Committee's request, the Antitrust Division has compiled an analysis of fines imposed during the period from January 1, 1946 to February, 1954. According to this analysis, the average of fines assessed during the period was approximately $2,600. Numerous fines of $100, $150, $200, $250, $350, $400, and $500 were imposed. 

From this compilation, we conclude that the figure of $5,000 as the maximum fine has probably been adequate in most cases. 

Relevant in appraising the adequacy of the $5,000 fine ceiling is that, with high income tax rates, fines paid are not deductible as business expenses by either individuals or corporations. Also severe may be the cost of defending an antitrust proceeding as well as the threat of treble damage actions in the wake of government success.

Similarly, not to be passed over is the deterrant effect on a respected businessman of 450

Nofelvill confl od gen ad ar 

130w umioval p chiedo olo faraidha un neticoleh anotace anfest wither be sowie wit sa 24

any criminal indictment as well as the relative effectiveness generally of increasing the amount of punishment as compared with increasing its celerity and certainty. 

Counting all these factors, we feel that, to make some account for inflation, the present $5,000 ceiling should be increased to $10,000.12/ 

12/ With the necessity for any proposed ceiling increase, Herbert Clark and Fred Fuller disagree. Walter Adams, on the other hand, believes that the maximum fine should be increased to $50,000. In his words, "Various Congressional committees have repeatedly recommended that the maximum fine be so increased. On May 5, 1953, the 

House passed H.R. 2237 to accomplish that objective, and the bill is now pending in the Senate. As House 

Report No. 271, 83d Congress, 1st Session, points out: 'Little imagination is required...to realize how paltry the Sherman Act penalty of $5,000 is today when compared with the vast assets of many companies and their annual profits. Indeed, a $5,000 fine is so light a burden upon the corporate treasury for open violations of the antitrust laws as compared with possible profits to be derived from illegal practices that... 'violation is regarded by some businessmen as good business risk.' A cursory glance at penalty statistics will corroborate this conclusion.' 

Moreover, as a former Chief of the Antitrust Division observed: 'A fine of $5,000 for violating a law which is so important to our economy....was inadequate when it was passed in 1890, and it certainly is inadequate today when the purchasing power of the dollar has decreased threefold, and when the corporate structure of our industry is so much more complicated and so much more widespread than it was at that time.' I agree with the Congressman who not long ago commented that the cost of sinning ought to go up at least as much as the cost of living." 11 457

2. Divorcement, Dissolution and Divestiture 

"In an equity suit," the Supreme Court has observed, "the end to be served is not punishment of past transgression, nor is it merely to end specific illegal practices. A public interest served by such civil suits is that they effectively pry open to competition a market that has been closed by defendants' illegal restraints."13/ Applying this general policy over the sixty odd years of Sherman Act history, courts have in only 24 litigated. cases entered decrees requiring divordement, divestiture or dissolution.14/

Such "Judicial restraint" reflects the Supreme Court's view that since, "divestiture is a remedy to restore competition and not to punish those who restrain trade, it is not to be used indiscriminately, without regard to the type of violation or whether other effective remedies, less harsh, are available."15/ "Judges," generally, one Court noted, "in prescribing remedies have known their own limitations. „16/ Thus, Courts as a rule have refused divestiture absent some clear showing of its necessity to effective competition, its practicality and, in light of surrounding circumstances, its basic fairness.

Court reluctance to grant drastic relief is firmly grounded in considerations of policy. As Judge Wyzanski put it: "In the antitrust field the courts have been accorded, by common consent, an authority they have in no other branch of enacted law. * * *

C. Review Procedure Within the Antitrust Division 

This evaluation along with other crucial enforcement decisions, requires realization that the Antitrust Division is far more than a group of prosecutors enforcing a criminal statute. In addition, it administers laws which treat important and difficult problems in American industrial activity. Making and winning a case is a normal function of a prosecutor. But making and winning a case is not always the most effective procedure for the enforcement of the Sherman Act. Also required is careful analysis of economic and marketing problems as well as an understanding review of business conduct and basic questions as to the public interest. These and related questions are best considered in an atmosphere not dominated by a zealous prosecutor bent solely on court success. 

We realize, of course, that the Assistant Attorney

General and the staff responsible to him and the Attorney

General review proposed actions to seek some detached appraisal of the advisability of formal proceedings. In many situations, however, we believe that certain problems demand careful consideration by one or more experienced staff lawyers and economists having no prosecuting or other responsibilities. 

No set review procedure is recommended so that the Assistant Attorney General in charge of the Division may designate ad hoc or continuing groups as the need and the problem at hand may suggest.

The designated group would review any matter submitted by the Assistant Attorney General on his own initiative or in response to the request of a potential or actual defendant. 

The Division Head could, in his discretion, submit any problem and accept or reject any recommendation. However, the following situations seem appropriate to the Committee for the advice of this review group: 8. b. c. where a substantial question of policy or a serious doubt exists as to whether any proceedings should be instituted and if so, whether such proceedings should be civil or criminal; where important issues arise involving the relief to be sought in civil proceedings either in advance of a complaint or at the close of litigation; where crucial deadlocks have arisen in the negotiation of consent judgments; d. where proposals have been made by parties under investigation for settlement by negotiation of a consent judgment prior to the filing of a complaint. 460

This recommendation, we recognize, treats matters of staff organization and Division internal procedure. Nonetheless far more than mere management detail is involved. 

We propose an experiment which, if it gains respect through experience, can, we believe, play an important role in promoting fair and effective enforcement. Details of timing, organization, whether parties have a right to review in given situations, we leave to the teachings of operation. Instead we set forth only the outlines of a principle designed to assure detached and considered judgment.26/

D. Consent Settlement Procedures 

This detached view may be crucial, for example, in the give and take of negotiation of consent settlements. To defendants, potential or actual, such settlements may avoid the publicity and expense of trial; to the government, caught in the vice of increasing complaints and decreasing enforcement resources, their economy may make or break enforcement success. 

Indeed, from 1935 to date, 72% of civil actions brought were terminated by consent decrees. 

26/ From this Review Procedure proposal, David W. Robinson and Kenneth Kimble dissent: They feel: (a) it would be cumbersome and unduly wasteful since (b) the same function is now carried on by the Assistant Attorney General and his First of Second Assistant. 461

peba of, 7 J dac ev 

Las byby Bodah

Realizing the importance of the consent settlement process, we reject any notion of its curtailment. 

Some have urged that the Division on occasion has - banking on pre-trial settlement, brought weak cases. The Division, that argument runs, would be more selective were it required to try every case. With that contention, this Committee disagrees. Instead, we reiterate the need for consent procedures and consider the following means for their improvement: 

1. At various times in the past, Department practice was to refuse to negotiate prior to the filing of a complaint. 

This refusal was supported on the grounds that a complaint crystallizes the issues and thereby expedites settlement negotiations, and that otherwise potential defendants would use these negotiations as a means for delay. Recently, however, the Department has experimented with negotiations before complaint and has indicated an interest in giving this practice a full trial. The result sought is a saving of time and money as well as increased cooperation between business and the Government. That experiment this Committee endorses.

We recommend pre-filing negotiation whenever the Division deems it feasible for efficient enforcement. 

2. Current Department practice is to negotiate consent judgments with fewer than all defendants. As a result, we realize, a judgment may be entered against the litigating defendants which is less severe than that entered against 462

กษ feen und sodottajien de bedrongia wou focutar a Jagon t: r !

consenting parties. The remedy for any inequity, however, is not to cease negotiations with fewer than all defendants. 

Instead, the Department should move to make the consent judgment co-equal with the litigated decree where, for example, the litigated judgment was based on a substantive rule of law applicable to all defendants or where dissimilar judgments will prejudice the effective competition contemplated by the 

Sherman Act. 

3. Current practice requires consent negotiations, first, with the trial staff assigned to the case until agreement has been reached. Thereafter, the judgment is submitted to the 

Judgment Section staff for review and further negotiations often follow. The result may be unneeded duplication of efforts both by Division staff and private counsel. Such waste might be avoided, we suggest, by some Division procedure aimed at crystallizing the Government's position before negotiation begins. Thus renewed and separate negotiation, after agreement has once been reached, may be minimized.27/ 

27 This recommendation may possibly be achieved by more effective use of procedures established in Division Office 

Order No. 11, September 15, 1952. That order provides for transfer of negotiations from the Trial to the Judgments Section when "the judgment is (a) either a satisfactory basis for settlement, or (b) is of such a nature that further negotiations by the Judgments Section would probably be desirable" (underscoring added). By frequent exercise of Trial Division discretion as to when further negotiation will be required, needless duplication may be saved, 463

4. At present, the Department requires defendants to submit the initial draft of a consent judgment. We realize this practice stemmed from criticism of the Division's one time custom of filing companion criminal and civil proceedings with an eye toward using the pending prosecution to coerce civil settlement. To eliminate this pressure, the Division adopted the practice of requiring defendants to request settlement and submit a decree. The evil criticized, now abandoned, was, of course, real. Nevertheless the remedy chosen was purely formal and now unnecessarily burdens defendants who, for example, have had no experience with antitrust matters or who cannot determine from the complaint the relief the Government considers necessary. Accordingly, we recommend the Department submit the initial draft of a consent judgment in response to a good faith request by defendants. 6.

In consent negotiation, the Department should not seek relief (1) deemed by the Supreme Court to transgress constitutional boundaries; or (2) which, in the particular case, under no circumstances could be obtained after litigation. Some urge that, since the Division, no mere private litigant, enforces a federal statute, it should demand whatever relief, in the public interest, its bargaining position may coerce. 

That view, we believe, ignores the prosecutor's responsibility to stay within statutory and constitutional bounds. It threatens our goal of equitable law enforcement and, accordingly, should be rejected. 464

E. Trial Problemș 

When consent negotiations fail, prospects of trial loom large. Problems then arise because complex antitrust litigation often requires protracted and expensive pre-trial and trial proceedings. These may add real burdens to already congested calendars as well as to the Government and private parties alike. As the Judicial Conference Report of September, 1951, put it, then existing conditions, "if permitted to continue *** might threaten the judicial process itself in respect to complex controversies."28/

In allaying this problem, a variety of techniques may play an important role. Thus, adept court control of discovery and depositions, formulation of a trial plan, careful limiting of documents and witnesses, use of a spokesman for multiple counsel, all may without prejudice speed up complex proceedings. Many such devices are considered at some length in the May, 1954, Report of the American Bar Association

Committee on Practice and Procedure in the Trial of Anti trust Cases. These techniques must, of course, be adapted to the problems of a particular case. In this report, we focus only on four general issues: (1) Should one judge be assigned throughout a case for all purposes? 

28/ Procedure in Antitrust and other Protracted Cases, A 

Report Adopted by the Judicial Conference of the United 

States, September 26, 1951 at p. 4. 465

(2) How can issues best be particularized and pre-trial discovery most effectively used? (3) How can the period of relevant inquiry most fairly be limited? (4) Should issues be separated for trial? More specifically, should hearings on relief be separate from trial on the merits? 

1. At present some Federal districts assign antitrust litigation to one judge for all purposes. Many, however, do not. All antitrust litigation, public and private, should be assigned, as a regular practice in all districts, promptly after the action has commenced to one judge for all purposes. 

Thus the time needed for more than one judge to acquaint himself with each case's complexities may be saved. In addition, the perspective of the entire litigation may more readily be brought to bear on each separate phase of the case. 

2. Another prime problem is the lack of sufficient particularity of the issues to be tried. When issues have not been carefully defined, there is under the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure no effective limit upon the scope of discovery and, as a result, courts are turned into investigating agencies. 

Contributing to this problem are (1) liberality in modern pleading practice, (2) the Government's lack of adequate means for investigating civil offenses before a 466

Dinepido jo usy ylbenzbek' Tedikom odl

complaint is filed and (3) the reluctance of trial courts to resolve disputed issues of law in advance of trial, to compel the parties to reduce broad issues to those genuinely in dispute and to require that these issues be stated with particularity. 

To meet this problem, we recommend: 1. 

Where pleadings do not present issues of fact or law with sufficient particularity for efficient trial preparation, the court and counsel should promptly undertake to particularize the issues to be tried by: 8. 

A motion by the defendant under Rule 12(e) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure for a more definite statement. This rule should be construed liberally in antitrust cases to require the plaintiff to state its charges in full and with adequate particularity. b. Pre-trial conferences at which the parties and the court should have before them a detailed statement of the issues which should be prepared initially by the plaintiff. As the conference

As the conference proceeds, the court may also require the defendant to state its position. C. 

Interrogatories addressed to the plaintiff to discover the particular facts relevant duly to issues to be raised at trial. Pre-trial conferences should then be focussed on the answers to such interrogatories. 467

allege of bas odpcal 30440

2. If either party, in a private or Government case, anticipates the use of any discovery process, on an extensive or minor scale or with respect to some or all issues, the court, where feasible, should require a pre-trial statement stating to its satisfaction the issues as to which discovery is sought; and, when approved by the court after hearing, the pre-trial statement, unless and until modified for a good cause, would constitute the plan for discovery and mark its permissible limits.

We realize, of course, discovery may be more important to treble damage plaintiffs than to the Government. For while the Government, under our proposal, would have compulsory process before instituting proceedings where either civil or criminal action is contemplated, a private party must depend upon discovery under Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, With this distinction in mind, our recommendation should not limit any proper use of discovery but rather prescribe a procedure to prevent abuses that have frequently occurred when discovery has been allowed to run its course without adequate court supervision. 468

3. 

In all civil or criminal Government proceedings, private actions or Federal Trade Commission hearings, the 29/ should be the guide 

Oregon State Medical Society opinion, where the proof offered by either party reaches back more than a reasonable number of years. If that case's teaching is not followed by trial counsel, the court should by pretrial order point the following guides: a.

The function of a civil proceeding where injunctive relief is sought is to forestall future violations and dissipate the effects of past violations. b. Required for violation is proof of a real threat of future violation or a contemporary violation of a nature likely to continue or recur. 

C. If a cause of action is established by such conduct, then proof of past offenses is immaterial and adds nothing. d. If a cause is not so proved, then the case should be dismissed. e.

Background evidence is only justified to explain the present and predict the future.

In appropriate cases, a pre-trial order should fix a cut-off date for proof. This date ordinarily should not be inflexible but require a showing of good cause for the receipt of evidence concerning earlier events. In fixing a cut-off date the following considerations are relevant: 

29 343 U.S. 326 (1952). 469

i. The plaintiff should never be prevented from offering direct evidence of the formation of an alleged conspiracy no matter when it occured. But when proof is of agreements or relationships commenced long prior to the complaint, or extending over a long period, then proof of conduct pursuant to the conspiracy may profitably be limited to reasonably current practices. ii. Where proof of conspiracy is not direct but consists of long enduring conduct from which conspiracy is to be inferred, then both counsel and the court should cut the period of proof to the bone. Of course, no hard and fast rule is practical; any cut-off date must turn on the circumstances of the particular case and accord plaintiff a fair opportunity to prove his cause as he sees fit. 

Nonetheless, in many cases it may be appropriate to require plaintiff to commence with proof of events no further back than six or ten years and, after completing proof from the selected date to the date of trial, proceed with proof of earlier events only for good cause. iii. Charges of monopolization or attempts to monopolize under Section 2 of the Sherman Act present considerations different from claims of conspiracy under Section 1 or 2. 

Accordingly, the general standards of the Oregon State Medical Society case should be modified to permit proof of the structure and development of the alleged monopoly, and acts done to 470

To the extent these events reach into the distant past, however, rules of relevance and materiality should be strictly applied to keep such early proof to its essential minimum. 

4. Separation of issues for trial

Separate trials are desirable under the following circumstances: 8. 

In non-jury treble damage cases, the issue of liability should be tried before the issue of damage. Thus the later issue of damages, frequently more complex and costly to prove than violation, would not be reached until its relevance is assured. b. 

Where the validity of patents is in issue, that issue should be segregated. c. In rare instances, it may be convenient to proceed from one issue to another, hearing all the evidence of all parties on one issue before proceeding to the next. But this procedure may not be used if any party defendant desires to make a motion to dismiss at the close of plaintiff's case. The use of this procedure is, therefore, dependent upon the consent of all parties defendant.30/ d. Where the Government seeks dissolution, divestiture or other complex relief posing knotty economic problems, 30 A like recommendation is supported by analysis in 

American Bar Association, May 1, 1954, Report of the 

Committee on Practice and Procedure in the Trial of 

Antitrust Cases, pp. 25-26. 47

.rowing aids ce bas totales oiuppe 

There of thodi gautamiton am starsjer que parez mene et gestion. podneteja wos to Jolet 12066

hearings on relief should be conducted after trial on the merits. Thus proof of violation will not be enmeshed in the complex of added data possibly relevant in fashioning remedies.

Adoption of these recommendations, we suggest, may ease the burden of protracted antitrust litigation on courts, the Government, and private parties. Inevitably such litigation poses a plethora of economic and business problems. 

Nonetheless we do not feel an independent staff of economic experts should be provided to assist courts in the trial of antitrust cases no matter how involved. The place of an economic expert in antitrust trials is no higher than an expert in any field, namely, as a witness on the stand, where his opinions meet the test of analysis and crossexamination as well as of contradicting, supplementing or explanatory testimony.

Similarly, we reject the suggestion that courts are not equipped to consider and determine Sherman Act cases, including those seeking divorcement, divestiture and dissolution.

Such a proposition rests upon the inadmissible premise that the law has grown so complex--in this case a penal statute--that parties be denied the right to judicial determination of their conduct's legality. 472
