F. Compliance with and Modification of Antitrust Judgments. 

Effective enforcement requires more than winning cases.

Once trial is over and judgment entered, then crucial becomes the decree's enforcement. This task is indeed far-reaching. 

For, presently the Department is responsible for enforcement of some 378 judgments entered since 1890. Not all judgments, of course, pose equal enforcement difficulties. And antitrust judgments, enforceable by criminal and civil contempt actions, carry strong sanctions. Nonetheless, with full recognition of this task's difficulties and importance, we recommend: 

1. The Department should conduct regular studies to determine whether its judgments have been effective to restore competition. Frequently useful in this task might be Section 

6(c) of the Federal Trade Commission Act authorizing the Commission "upon the application of the Attorney General" to "make investigations" and report to the Attorney General on how any antitrust decree "has been or is being carried "131/ out. In the past, despite occasional "compliance campaigns", compliance has usually been investigated only upon receipt of complaints. As a minimum, the Department should investigate compliance with its judgments not only upon receipt of complaints that appear to be well founded, but also on its own motion when it has reason to believe any 

31/ 15 U.S.C. (1952), 46(c). 4773

judgment has been violated. At the same time, the Department should be careful not to conduct random investigations burdensome and wasteful to all concerned, 2. The Department should consent to judgment modification where the defendant can show that a change in circumstances makes its continuation unchanged incompatible with antitrust goals. In the past, the Division almost without exception has refused to agree to substantial modification of even the broadest decree provisions. Similarly, courts are reluctant to modify decrees entered either after trial or by consent.32/ 

To "effectuate *** the basic purpose of the original consent decree," courts may approve modifications after entry.33/ "the But if the party opposing modification can, in the language of the recent Ford case, show "actual disadvantage" or persistence of an inequality" stemming from change, the terms of the original decree must stand intact.34/ 

As a result, the Division should stand ready to consent to judgment modification whenever antitrust goals will be promoted. 

32 U.S v. Swift & Company, 286 U.S. 106, 114-15 (1932). 

33/ Chrysler Corporation v. United States, 316 U.S. 562 (1942). 556, 34/ Ford Motor Company v. United States, 335 U.S. 303, 322 (1948). 474

3. Finally, a special judgment enforcement problem stems from decree provisions which, designed to dissipate the effects of past unlawful practices, ban conduct which would otherwise be lawful. To meet this problem the Department has devised (1) a provision which expressly expires after a stated period, and (2) a provision under which defendants may after a stated period show that conditions have changed so that relief is no longer necessary. These procedures, we believe, are a forward step and should be continued. 

G. Advance Clearances and Releases 

Finally, apart from problems of investigation, trial, consent negotiations and judgment enforcement, the Department faces the broader question of the extent Division clearance procedures can halt violations before they occur, The Department of Justice now has no authority to give advisory opinions 

However, under the so-called "railroad release" it will bind itself not to resort to criminal action under certain conditions. 

Also, in Section 7 cases both the Department and the Federal Trade Commission have a procedure for giving full clearance at least for the particular merger. 

We recognize, of course, that the generality of the Sherman Act has been criticized as offering no standards by which a businessman can determine the legality of a proposed course of conduct. Further difficulty is said to arise from conflicting and confusing court decisions. 475

.Quin baid 

Marybet ( 

4 $6. .: 281100

Compliance is hazardous, it is argued, because the Sherman

Act is a criminal law and failure to make an accurate guess as to the validity of contemplated action may result not only in criminal penalties but also in the stigma of criminal prosecution. The hazards involved are intensified, the critics contend, because of the fact that, although proof of intent is usually required in criminal actions, a defendant may be convicted under the Sherman Act, as now administered by the Department of Justice and interpreted by the courts, without proof of specific criminal intent.

To meet these problems, it has been suggested that the Department should be authorized to issue advisory opinions as well as to issue rules and regulations under procedures similar to those employed by the Internal Revenue 

Service. Alternatively some urge, especially where an industry rather than an individual company seeks clearance, the Federal Trade Commission should be authorized to conduct conferences at which all concerned would be represented, including the Antitrust Division, the industry involved and the consumer. On the basis of the facts presented to it at the conference, the Commission would decide whether the arrangement under consideration would be valid.35/

Objections have been made to these and like proposals on the following grounds; 

1. As a prosecuting agency, the Department should not be expected to issue clearances in advance. 

2. Any such clearance would in any event be of narrow utility. Since it would have to be limited to the facts known at the time, it would be subject to revision in the light of subsequent developments. 

3. In view of the intricate and far-reaching nature of modern business, the Department should not be asked to make broad decisions, not based one set of investigated facts, which might well effect the economic life of an industry. 

After careful consideration, the Committee recognizes the value of informal discussions of problems between counsel for the Department and for private parties but recommends no change in the existing procedures of the Department of Justice for advance clearances and releases, The Committee recognizes that the uncertainty of antitrust's strictures may plague good faith business efforts to stay within the law. The scope of this entire report indeed reflects our concern with so far as possible marking out the bounds between legal and proscribed conduct. II. The Federal Trade Commission 

Many of these same problems of course are faced by the Federal Trade Commission. Committee attention, however, has focused only on (1) the Trade Practice Conference, (2) informal settlement procedures and (3) general problems of compliance with and modification of Commission orders. 

A. The Trade Practice Conference 

Since 1918, the Commission, without express statutory authority, has developed the Trade Practice Conference as an informal procedure for securing voluntary industry-wide compliance. Conference rules may be designed, for example, to prohibit unfair competitive practices, to control false and misleading advertising, and to promote non-deceptive description and labeling of industry products. These Rules are adopted in conferences between industry and Commission representatives and are finally promulgated by the Commission itself.

Group I rules, covering mostly unfair methods of competition and, occasionally Clayton Act or restraint of trade problems, generally are carefully guarded restatements of existing statutory and decisional law. Group II rules aim 478

boeroot -i

to promote sound business practices or voluntary cooperation in accord with existing law. In addition, more specific rules control false and misleading advertising and nondeceptive description and labeling of industry products. 

Examples are rules comprising a comprehensive code for labeling and advertising in the cotton yard goods, silk, linen, hosiery, luggage, rayon and silk dyeing, printing and finishing, watch and watch case, and radio receiving set manufacturers.

Those Group I rules phrased as restatements of statutory and decisional law are of little use and tend to discredit the whole program. Examples are rules, covering price discriminations, brokerage and advertising allowances. Similarly, tending to discredit the program are vaguely phrased Group II rules governing contractual obligations, the settlement of business disputes fairly, and the misuse of buying and selling power. 

We believe Trade Practice Conference Rules could be effective means for treating industry wide problems like advertising allowances, brokerage and price discrimination.

A prime example of how these rules may aptly be used is the recent cosmetic rules giving content to the standard of "proportionally equal terms" in Sections 2(a) and (e) of the Robinson-Patman Act. We have been advised the Commission is presently reviewing the Trade Practice program with a special 479

eye toward extending its use to Clayton Act and restraint of trade cases. We approve of efforts thus to extend Conference rules through precise statements that may give effective guidance to particular industries. In so doing, however, great care must be exercised to avoid approval of rules that run afoul of antitrust proscriptions. Thus it is important that the Department of Justice be fully informed of proposed rules and that its objections, if any, be voiced prior to their approval by the Commission. Toward this end, procedures should be developed between the Commission and the 

Department of Justice. 

B. Informal Settlement Procedures 

Beyond Trade Practice Conference rules, the, Commission, to encourage informal settlements, employs two procedures: (1) stipulations and (2) consent orders. 

1. We understand existing stipulation processes are under study by the Commission and revisions are likely in the near future. Accordingly, in that area we make no formal recommendations, but merely suggest relevant considerations.

Under existing procedures, no complaint is issued and parties may execute a stipulation admitting the facts and agreeing to halt challenged practices. This process had thus far been limited, first, to cases involving unfair methods of competition or deceptive acts and, second, to instances where the Commission feels that the law was transgressed only unintentionally through misunderstanding or lack of care. 480

Bard admonadeda getmang Agcontt csicse be undury Desesong Bol 122

On whether this device should be extended to antitrust offenses as well this Committee is divided. The majority feels that in the interest of settling controversies short of costly litigation, stipulation should be available in certain instances involving antitrust charges. Its use, they believe, should turn on whether the violation charged is, to the Commission's view, flagrant or deliberate. Where obvious misdeeds are at bar, settlement should not be permitted short of a complaint and consent order.

With the majority view some members disagree. They believe that settlement by stipulation should not be permitted in any antitrust matters. Stipulations, they suggest, are both an ineffective remedy for past antitrust violations and no deterrent to future missteps. Were such settlements permitted, they conclude, antitrust enforcement would be the loser. 

2. Until recently, Commission consent order practice was that settlements will be negotiated only (1) after the Commission has issued a complaint; (2) prior to the taking of any evidence; (3) if all respondents agree to the settlement; and (4) if the settlement includes a statement of the alleged unlawful practices, a statement the respondents need not admit but may not deny. 481

perjed bed the

On May 7, 1954, the Commission amended its rules of practice to extend use of consent orders. As amended, the consent order rule - 

1. Eliminates the previous requirement that consent settlements contain findings of fact; 

2. Permits disposition of a case by consent at any stage of the proceeding; 

3. Allows settlement of a case as to some or all of the issues or respondents; 

4. Authorizes hearing examiners to accept or reject stipulations containing proposed consent orders, with acceptance subject to Commission review and with rejection subject to appeal to the Commission. 

Under the amendment, respondents must admit only jurisdiction.

In addition, they must agree that the complaint may be used in construing the terms of the order; that the order shall have the same force as if entered after a full hearing; and that the order may be modified or set aside in the same manner as other orders. The rule further provides respondents may waive the entry of findings of fact and conclusions of law, as well as further procedural steps before the hearing examiner or the Commission. Finally, respondents have the right to Court test of the order's validity. 482

In these new procedures, this Committee concurs. 

Any advantage in the previously required fact statement was outweighed by the time and expense required for agreement on its contents. In addition we agree that unwise was the formerly blanket ban on consent orders after any taking of evidence. This prohibition aimed to bar respondents from using consent negotiations to delay hearings. Such delay, we suggest, may be obviated by continuing proceedings during negotiation. Finally, we concur that the 

Commission should have discretion to settle with less than all respondents when it can thus obtain effective relief and still not prejudice hearings involving remaining parties. 

For these reasons, recent Commission procedural changes should improve crucial consent processes.

C. Compliance with Commission Orders 

Whether an order is entered by consent or after hearing, ever present is the problem of its enforcement. 

The Commission may issue orders under Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission 

Act or under the Clayton Act. 

1. Orders issued pursuant to $5 of the Federal Trade

Commission Act become final (1) if respondents seek no court review within 60 days or (2), if review is sought, upon the order's affirmance. From 1938 through 1950, orders "finalized"--either through court affirmance or lapse of time--were fortified with a legal sanction in Section 5(1) which penalized 483

Moreover, the size of the potential penalties imposed by the amended Section 5(1), we believe, is wholly disproportionate to the offenses involved. The prohibitions of 

Section 5 of the Federal Trade Commission Act, directed against unfair and deceptive acts in commerce, make no distinction among businesses large or small. Nevertheless, small business enterprises have been a major target of cease and desist orders entered against violators of Section 5. 

A $5,000 fine multiplied by each day of violation of a final order can readily mount into totals ruinous to any but the most affluent offenders. To be sure, the $5,000 per day forfeiture represents only a legislative maximum which need not be imposed in every case. But unless courts ignore applicable legislative declarations, the legislative maximum must be viewed as directly influencing actual penalties assessed. 11

This exorbitant ceiling seems clearly unneeded. Since 

1938, there has been only one civil penalty proceeding to enforce an antitrust order and 80 suits to enforce other types of orders under the Federal Trade Commission Act. 

In 90% of these cases, courts have entered judgments for less than $1,000 for each separate offense. In only one case did the judgment reach even the pre-1950 $5,000 maximum. 

Accordingly, the present $5,000 per day ceiling is unnecessary and we believe should be revised downwards. 

Commission orders under the Clayton Act, in contrast, do not become final by lapse of time. Such order must first be affirmed by a United States Court of Appeals upon application by the Commission and a showing that the order has been violated.40/ 

Thereafter, enforcement requires contempt proceedings for violation of the court's enforcement decree. Since 1941, there has been only one successful contempt proceeding to enforce a Clayton Act order.

We realize the Commission has recently announced plans 

Their plan calls, to improve its enforcement program. initially, for a systematic and selective review of over 4,000 cease and desist orders, 8,000 stipulations to cease and desist and 2,000 trade practice rules. In addition the Commission will no longer limit compliance investigations to cases where complaints have been received. 

Other steps announced by the Commission include: 

1. Closer coordination between the general investigation staff and those primarily responsible for compliance with orders, stipulations and trade practice rules, 2. More frequent use of procedures for requiring the filing of special follow-up reports "showing the manner and form of compliance with cease and desist orders. 

3. A more informative letter of notification to (a) respondents under orders and (b) parties to stipulations concerning the action taken in response to their reports of compliance. 

4. A more effective program for enlisting the cooperation of industry members to effect industry-wide observance of trade practice rules.

These new Commission procedures, this Committee believes, offer real hope for enforcement improvement. In eliminating past laxity, the Commission should not, of course, conduct haphazard investigations both burdensome and wasteful. Wisely administered, the recent rules will be a step forward. 487

Related Jurisdiction of the Department of Justice and the 

Federal Trade Commission

Apart from problems each of the enforcement agencies faces in fashioning its own procedures and policies, together the Division and Commission must best utilize their enforcement resources in areas where both have statutory responsibility. 

The Clayton Act, for example, makes the Attorney General and the Commission each responsible for enforcement of its Sections 42/

Accordingly we reject two suggestions equally drastic--on the one hand, abolish the Commission's antitrust function--or, on the other, transfer from the Department to the Commission all antitrust matters. And we focus attention on means for assuring achievement of the two agencies' joint task.

In June, 1948, each agency agreed other by an exchange of cards before initiating any investigation, grand jury proceeding or trade practice conference.

Should one agency object to the action proposed, the question of which agency should proceed is submitted to a joint conference. This information exchange, however, does not extend to so-called railroad releases and similar informal rulings (except, recently, as to proposed mergers) nor to any subsequent developments in proceedings once initiated (except proposed trade practice conference rules. 

52/ This "Memorandum of Agreement" established a "systematic mutual exchange of information regarding pending anti-monopoly investigations and of each new investigation at the time it is directed." It provided for creation of a "card system" 

These "cards are to be made in duplicate, exchanged between the two offices, and a file of such exchange cards retained in each office. The information to be shown on the said cards will include the following for each pending investigation and each new investigation at the time it is directed; the file number, the title, specification of product or products involved, and a statement of the charges involved." 

The agreement continues that "upon receipt of a card disclosing *** a new investigation has been directed, information is to be conveyed back as to whether or not any matter is pending in ** [the other] agency concerning the specific party or parties, commodity, and charges. 

Should there be no matter pending * * the submitting agency can proceed without further liaison. If, on the other hand, a matter is pending, further liaison is to be effected before the new investigation is undertaken. However nothing herein contained shall in any way limit either agency in making an independent decision as to what investigation it will undertake." 491

".Ánvicvni teris fate pet Snacdogo sprod id too aleted quiding, fu

Beyond this formal agreement, from case by case action an enforcement pattern has emerged. Thus, apart from cases where Clayton Section 3 forms part of a larger Sherman Act charge, the Department has brought only one case solely under 53/ Section 3 of the Clayton Act.

Similarly, unless a Clayton 

Act Section 2 offense comprises an element of Sherman Act violation, the Division apparently recognizes Commission procedures are better suited to primary enforcement of price discrimination bans.

From all this we conclude the two agencies have attempted with limited, but ever increasing, success to cooperate in their dual administration of the antitrust laws. To further implement their coordination, we suggest: 

1. Extension of the the present card exchange procedure to all proceedings and rulings of both agencies. If feasible, railroad releases, clearances under Sections 7 and 8 of the Clayton Act and comparable informal rulings might be specifically included. Neither should take any steps which might affect the other without prior clearance and thereafter each should keep the other informed of significant subsequent developments. 

2. To avoid duplicating investigations, the investigative files should, to the extent permitted by existing law, be made fully available to the other. 

53/ United States v. United Shoe Machinery Corp., 258 u.s. 451 (1922). 492

hroup Hay 7: *:

3. Commission economists should regularly consult with their Division counterparts so that they may effectively perform the functions contemplated by Section 6 of the Trade 

Commission Act. 

4. Antitrust investigators, now somewhat scattered through the Federal Bureau of Investigation, should be consolidated into a distinctive unit of the Bureau 54/ and made readily available to the Division and, at its option, to the 

Commission as well. 

5. The two agency heads should regularly meet to review significant investigations contemplated or commenced, discuss what proceedings, if any, should be brought, and which agency is best equipped to handle them. Which agency should continue a given proceeding should turn, not on any arbitrary allocation of jurisdiction, but on a case-by-case account of past experience, present manpower and remedies available to each agency. 

Each agency, of course, is required by law to enforce all of the statutes within its jurisdiction. Nevertheless, the different facilities and procedures of each should be considered. Thus, evidence justifying criminal prosecution and severe punishment should be turned over to the Department of Justice. On the other hand, Commission administrative procedures available should be utilized in cases where the 54/ Supporting this conclusion is a Staff Report, United States versus Economic Concentration and Monopoly, p. 50, dated

public *** as allies of the Government in enforcing the antitrust laws; 156/ the means chosen, on the other hand, is to give "the injured party ample recompense for the wrong suffered "27/ by allowing threefold recovery of damages.

During the Shermuz. Act's first fifty years, private antitrust plaintiffa succeeded in only 13 of 175 actions brought.58/

Since World War II, however, this picture has altered sharply. From June 1947 to June 1951, private antitrust suits pending in District Courts jumped from 118 to 367. Recoveries moreover, increased almost correspondingly. 

Since 1951, growth has been even more rapid. 

This burgeoning of treble damage recoveries brings sharply into focus several basic issues. Initially, does private suits' enforcement role require damages be trebled in all cases or only where willful violations are at bar? 

Should the United States be permitted to recover damages?

And finally, should antitrust damage suits be governed by diverse state statutes of limitations or some uniform federal provision? To these issues we turn.

1. Current is a proposal to substitute the trial judge's discretion to double or triple damage awards for the present 59/ mandatory treble damage requirement Supporting this change is the argument that the surging contours of antitrust sometimes engulf unwitting violators. Especially so in the 

Robinson-Patman area where, in some instances, violation can hardly be predicted. To treat such unknowing transgressors on a par with willful wrongdoers seems arbitrary and unfair, Moreover, making triple recovery discretionary, many ! urge, will not impair private suits' deterrent effect. For the conscious malefactor cannot expect the benefit of such discretion. And the unwitting violator, seeking to act in conformity with the law, is naturally unaffected by the threat of triple damages.

Nor will incentive to private suit be curtailed. The inducement of mandatory trebled damages is no longer necessary to encourage suits by injured persons, 60/ development of both the procedural 

The extraordinary and substantive law, 59/ B. R. 4597, 83rd Cong., 1st Sess. (1953).

* current yield.63/ Finally, monopoly increases in plaintiff's costs may be inferred from estimates of what market price 64/ might have been under competitive conditions. 

Despite these developments, those opposing removal of the mandatory trebling provision urge that, since good faith, good intentions and even admitted social gains may afford no defense to an antitrust violation, they should not determine treble damages. The criminal penalty, that argument runs, is light; accordingly, there should be no weakening of the triple damage threat though it may, in individual cases, work injustice.

On balance, we favor vesting in the trial judge discretion to impose double or treble damages. In all instances, this would recompense injured parties. Beyond compensation, the trial court could then penalize the purposeful violator without imposing the harsh penalty of multiple damages on innocent actors.65/ 

63/ See e.g, Bordonaro v. Paramount Pictures Inc., 176 F 24

150 F 2d 738 (3d Cir. 1945); and cases note and cases note 60 supra..

65/ Eugene Rostow feels that relevant here is the distinction between major Sherman Act offenses, normally involving a substantial public interest, and those practices the Clayton Act proscribes as potential public injuries. Trebling of damages from Clayton Act violations should be left to trial court discretion. But not so, he feels, where Sherman Act violations are involved; in all such cases damages should be trebled. 498

lu

Ample precedent exists for vesting such discretionary power in the trial judge. Thus, the Housing and Rent Act gives the court discretion to impose liquidated damages up to three times the amount of the lawful rent in case of overcharges or unlawful evictions.66/ Similarly the Defense Production Act of 1950, establishing actions for violations of its price and wage provisions, gives the court full discretion to impose 671 damages up to three times the amount of the overcharge. 

Finally, the copyright and patent laws offer instances where the imposition of multiple damages is committed to the court's Judgment.68/

In none of the statutes, moreover, is any standard provided to guide the court's discretion. 

To do so in an antitrust damage context would similarly be impracticable. Discretion should turn on a variety of factors, including, for example, industry background, the business rationale for the challenged practices, the extent of injury, the legal advice preceding the challenged conduct and the record, if any, of past violations. These diverse factors are ill-suited to that statutory adumbration desirable to mold any jury charge. They can best be considered by a court in the exercise of its sound judgment.

This proposal, we finally emphasize, in no way saps the enforcement strength of private suits. Such proceedings have a vital role to play in aiding under-staffed government agencies to enforce throughout the nation antitrust strictures. 

2. Also proposed is to broaden Section 4's phrase "any person, who shall be injured in his business or property," to include the United States. Existing case law holds the United 

States is not a person entitled to sue under the statute.69/ 

There is sound reason for contending that the United States, when its property rights have been damaged by antitrust infractions, should have a right to recover its single damages, At the present time, States 70/ and municipalities 71/ may maintain such an action in their own right, although not on behalf of private persons. 72/ 

This same right, we believe, should be extended to the United States. 4

3. Toward a Federal Statute of Limitations

United States is the issue of what law shall determine the maximum time allowed between antitrust injury and private suit for redress. 73/ Since the antitrust laws specify no statute of limitations for private damage actions, courts! generally look to state law.74/ This approach, however, has produced more questions than answers.

80/ for example, the action was filed in 1938 and a consent decree entered against some of the defendants in 1940. Four years later, in August 1944, the government moved to modify the decree, seeking divorcement and divestiture, and the case was brought on for trial. After trial, appeal to the 

Supreme Court, and remand, consent decrees were entered against one defendant in 1948, one in 1949, and against the remaining defendants in 1950. Under these circumstances some courts have held that the statute of limitations was tolled from 1938 to 1950, notwithstanding the period during 81/ which the 1940 consent decree was in effect.

Ruling to 82/ the contrary, one court observed that were the statute tolled while any consent decree was in effect, the result would usually be a permanent suspension of limitations.

D Against this background, none challenge the need for a federal statute of limitations.83/ Currently, private antitrust action is rendered tedious by the issue of limitations needlessly magnified with arguments over which period governs, the events from which the statute runs, and the circumstances under which it is tolled--all made more complex by the chance of reference to the law of various states or to federal law. 

Finally, varying periods encourage "forum-shopping" and seem ill-suited for enforcement of a uniform federal policy. To remedy these evils, we recommend: (1) 83/ The enactment of a federal statute was supported: by the Department of Justice, Hearings on S. 1910, 81st

1. Section 4 of the Clayton Act. 

84/ be amended to provide a four-year statute of limitations running from accrual of the cause of action. 85/ 

2. The second paragraph of Section 5 of the Clayton Act be amended in the following respects: a. The reference should be to any civil or criminal action under Sections 1, 2, 3 or 4 of the Sherman Act, Sections 

73 or 74 of the Wilson Tariff Act, Section 3 of the Robinson- Patman Act, or Section 15 of the Clayton Act, thus making clear the intent uniformly to govern all actions involving some antitrust cause. b. The effectiveness of the suspension should be limited to the period of time during which the government action is actively pending and contested. This should avoid isolated court holdings that, when a consent decree is later opened, the statute is tolled during even the period the decree was in effect. 

847 Section 7 of the Sherman Act, unless repealed as an unnecessary duplication of Section 4, should likewise be amended. 85/ In urging a four-year period, the Committee recognizes that presently limitations vary from as low as one year to as high as ten years. (See e.g., American Tobacco Co. v. People's 

Tobacco Co., 204 Fed. 58 (5th Cir. 1913] [Louisiana one year];

Chattanooga Foundry & Pipe Works v. City of Atlanta, 203 V.S. 

390 [1906]. Tennessee [Tennessee- ten years], and the statute in 

Georgia may be twenty years, see 96 Cong. Rec. 10440 [1950]).

Compilations of the applicable statutory periods of the various states show that three-year and six-year periods are most prevalent, with the average for all jurisdictions falling close to four years. Sixteen states have six-year statutes, 506

The suspension period should, as to any defendant against whom the suspension is claimed, extend from the commencement or reopening of the proceeding to (1) the entry of a nolo plea or consent decree against that defendant, or (2) the date of other final action by the highest court acting in the matter. d. The claim of any person relying on suspension should be barred after one year from the end of the suspension period. In no event should such a person recover damages, accruing prior to commencement of the action, for a greater period than four years. But such person may recover damages accruing during any consecutive four-year period not otherwise 86 barred.

86/ with this aspect of our recommendation, Kenneth Kimble disagrees. He feels that (a) by its very nature this becomes not a statute of limitations but a statute limiting the period for which damages may be recovered. It has nothing to do with loss of evidence or difficulty of defense. Its sole purpose is to fix the damage period. He does not think such a purpose is salutary. If a person has been damaged there is no reason why he should not recover, particularly since other recommendations will make double or treble damages discretionary. (b) This recommendation is not consistent with the purpose. of the suspension statute. The purpose of that statute is to permit private plaintiffs to wait until the public action is terminated.
